PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
_________________________________________________________
INFORMATION NOTE
What this Part is about: This Part describes what information and
records the parties must disclose to each other and when and how the
parties may question each other about the dispute. The Part requires
the parties to share relevant and material information about the action in
order to clearly identify
- what is in dispute,
- what evidence is available about the dispute.
This information helps minimize surprises during Court proceedings and
avoids delay later in the litigation, assists the parties in evaluating their
own and the other partyâ€™s case, and facilitates resolution of the dispute or
elements of it.
This Part also contains rules for the evidence of experts and rules for
medical examinations by health care professionals.
Words and phrases used in this Part that have defined meanings in
the Appendix [Definitions] include: applications judge; claim; corporate
representative; corporate witness; costs award; Court; defendant;
expert; file; health care professional; judge; medical examination; order;
partnership; party; plaintiff; pleading; prescribed form; procedural order;
record; relevant and material; rules; third party defendant

5-3
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.1(1)
Purpose of This Part
5.1(1) Within the context of rule 1.2 [Purpose and intention of these rules], the
purpose of this Part is
(a) to obtain evidence that will be relied on in the action,
(b) to narrow and deﬁne the issues between parties,
(c) to encourage early disclosure of facts and records,
(d) to facilitate evaluation of the parties’ positions and, if possible,
resolution of issues in dispute, and
(e) to discourage conduct that unnecessarily or improperly delays
proceedings or unnecessarily increases the cost of them.
(2) The Court may give directions or make any order necessary to achieve the
purpose of this Part.
Rules of Court are to be interpreted to promote access to justice, prevent delays, and
encourage early disclosure and settlement; note Rr.1.2 and 5.1.¹
This Rule gives purposes and
discourages unnecessary delays or increases in costs.²
Maybe there is no express remedy (apart from costs) for a prolix afﬁdavit of records listing
separately many thousands of records, but R.5.1(2) may be relevant, e. g. to make a defendant
identify which of the thousands of records apply to it.³
Information Note
This Part does not apply to actions started by originating application unless the parties
otherwise agree or the Court otherwise orders. See rule 3.10 [Application of Part 4 and
Part 5].

Related Provisions
1.2 (Purpose and intention of the rules) ; 1.4 (Procedural Orders) ; 1.5 (Rule contravention,

non-compliance and irregularities); 1.7 (applying Rules by analogy) ; 3.10 (application to
originating applications); 4.31 (delay) ; 5.3 (modification of obligations in Part 5) ; 5.3(1) (b)
(discovery to be proportional to benefit) ; 5.12 (penalty for failure to produce records) ; 5.19
(limiting questioning) ; 5.25 (unreasonable questioning) ; 5.31 (use of discovery transcripts) ;
10.33 (costs of unnecessary steps).
DIVISION 1
HOW INFORMATION IS DISCLOSED
Subdivision 1
Introductory Matters
When Something is Relevant and Material
5.2(1) For the purposes of this Part, a question, record or information is relevant
and material only if the answer to the question, or the record or information,
could reasonably be expected

remedies?

R.5.2(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
(a) to signiﬁcantly help determine one or more of the issues raised in
the pleadings, or
(b) to ascertain evidence that could reasonably be expected to
signiﬁcantly help determine one or more of the issues raised in the
pleadings.¹
(2) The disclosure or production of a record under this Division is not, by reason
of that fact alone, to be considered as an agreement or acknowledgment that the
record is admissible or relevant and material.²

A. Questioning
See R.5.25n on what questions are appropriate on questioning (examination for discovery).
B. Relevant and Material: Tests
1. General Tests
Subrule (1) of this Rule is not much different from its 1999 predecessor.³
But that 1999 Rule
and the current R.5.2 are much narrower than the 1968 predecessor. Rule 5.2 says that the
question or record must “signiﬁcantly help determine. . . issues raised in the pleadings”. Note
“signiﬁcantly”.⁴
So the decisions before 1999⁵
must be approached with caution.
The general scope of discovery (written or oral) is what is “relevant and material” to any of the
issues in the lawsuit.⁶

5-5
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.2(2)
“Relevant” refers to issues in the pleadings, and “material” refers to the degree of helpfulness
on such topics.¹
Materiality refers to weight,²
or proof.³
Materiality does not require conclusively
helpfulness, but unrealistic or speculative quests do not sufﬁce.⁴
The information sought must be
reasonably expected to help signiﬁcantly in deciding one or more of the issues in the pleadings.⁵
Whether a document is relevant and material should not be overly strictly approached, and old
R.186.1 is carried forward into the 2010 R.5.2(1). Even after 1999 and 2010, examination for
discovery is still quite wide, and probably wider than relevance for trial. Helping to ascertain
evidence is an important branch.⁶
Requiring clarity or lack of dispute would create a Catch-22.⁷
A rational strategy or plausible theory is enough to found some discovery questions.⁸
The test
may be whether the lawsuit might end differently without the evidence in question, or whether
production of the record in question could lead in turn to production of other records indicating
existence of potential evidence on an important point.⁹
Whether a document is relevant and material can depend on the direct use of facts in it, or
on whether information in the document in question could reasonably be expected to ascertain
evidence which could reasonably be expected to signiﬁcantly help resolve an issue in dispute. Not
looking at that is error in principle, especially where counsel have addressed that point.
The test is not ﬁne or rigid; in interlocutory stages, a rational strategy is enough. The party
questioning should be allowed to learn facts clearly admissible at trial if obtained.¹⁰
At an early
stage in a suit, a judge may be unable to tell whether records are relevant or not. The disputed
ones here were said to give circumstantial proof, and circumstantial evidence is not to be applied
to bits of evidence in isolation.¹¹
At the production stage, the suggested inferences need not be
decided, nor helpfulness be conclusively proved. A plausible line of argument is enough, a low
threshold.¹²
If the records ﬁnally turn out to be irrelevant, the remedy is costs.¹³
Before the 1999 amendment, relevance had a very wide meaning here, far
wider than what would be admissible as trial evidence. It used to cover indirect
relevance, and what might be relevant.¹⁴
Tertiary relevance may no longer be

191 (¶ 3).

R.5.2(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
excluded.¹
The scope of relevance is narrower than before 1999,²
so if the events in the books,
logs or diaries are not directly in evidence, such records may no longer have to be disclosed?³
But
indirect relevance can be enough.⁴
The scope of relevance has been narrowed,⁵
and the issues in
the pleadings are now more important.⁶
Questioning (examination for discovery) is conﬁned to getting primary facts directly in issue,
or facts from which the existence of those primary facts may be directly inferred, always under the
aegis of the pleadings. The pleadings in the suit set the general outlines of what is relevant on an
examination for discovery.⁷
Deciding whether records have primary or secondary relevance and should be in an afﬁdavit
of records is not assessed too ﬁnely; a rational strategy in which the record in question would play
a material part is enough.⁸
If understanding is in issue, then the surrounding circumstances are relevant. Semble the
scope of questioning is still broad?⁹
The scope of examination for discovery is determined by the pleadings at the time the
questions are asked, not by later amendments to pleadings, and an amendment cannot be used
to undo that later.¹⁰
This refers to the pleadings as they stand when the application over materiality
is heard, even if they were narrower at the time that the questions were asked and have since
become broader through amendment.¹¹
It is all the pleadings which show what is “relevant and
material”,¹²
not just one pleading in isolation.¹³
Relevance depends on all the pleadings in the case:

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.2(2)
all causes of action, and all facts pleaded.¹
So what is admitted in pleadings (and discovery) is not
in issue, and conﬁrmatory evidence may not be relevant and material?²
Pleadings which make
something “relevant and material” must be an underlying factual allegation or dispute.³
Saskatchewan’s interpretation of a similar Rule was discussed, including proportionality and
a cost beneﬁt analysis.⁴
Duplicates of the same record need not be listed and veriﬁed.⁵
Though privileged records must be listed, irrelevant records need not be.⁶
Irrelevant passages
in a record to be disclosed may be blocked (“redacated”) only for true irrelevance, and for harm
from disclosure not sufficiently prevented by existing law or methods.⁷
2. Substantive Legal Issues
Any issues pleaded are open to discovery; this is not the time to decide that certain arguments
are legally untenable.⁸
If a question on examination for discovery is based on an arguable
proposition of law which will be debated at trial, the question should be answered.⁹
If relevance
of a record is disputed and relevance turns on how one interprets the contract being litigated, a
chambers judge should not try to interpret the contract before trial.¹⁰
Requiring clarity or lack of
dispute would create a Catch-22. Liability is not the issue to decide at this stage. The court must
take a very pragmatic view, looking from the perspective of the party who needs to prove the fact.
It is enough for counsel to disclose a rational strategy in which the document in question plays
a material part. Legitimate lines of inquiry are not to be cut off. And when one party has all the
knowledge and documents, it is unfair to summarily decide anything about liability.¹¹

5-8
R.5.2(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Though on their face the contested topics may not seem related, documents produced and
answers already given may show enough connection.¹
Conﬂicting evidence as to whether an
ofﬁcer or employee knows anything relevant is not enough to bar his or her examination; the test
in R.5.2 is “reasonably be expected” and “help determine”.
3. Answers to Questions
Relevance cannot be excluded or narrowed by the answer which a party gives on examination
for discovery. So even if he or she denies a suggestion, he or she may be questioned further
on the topic to test that denial, e. g. by seeing the records about that topic.²
A question does not
cease to be relevant because the witness’s earlier answer denies some suggestion or question.³
Denying that a fact is relevant is not enough to make it irrelevant.⁴
Rule 5.2 is not a summary
judgment rule, and a defendant cannot escape examination for discovery fully (or on certain
questions) by asserting that he or she is not guilty. The plaintiff can make the defendant pledge
his or her oath to that. The question is what issues the pleadings make relevant.⁵
That the opponent’s expert “needs” something does not make it producible or material.⁶
Bare
assertions or conjecture do not make a line of questions relevant.⁷
On the effect on undertakings of a later amendment of pleadings, see the AltaLink case.⁸
4. Elbow Room for Party Questioning
The ability to object to questions under R.5.25(2) (c) on the basis that they are unreasonable
or unnecessary should be narrowly interpreted. “Relevant and material” is still the touchstone. But
if the party questioned is a large body, to avoid undue work in informing the ofﬁcer, stricter tests for
materiality may be followed.⁹
A mere statement that it would be too much trouble or too expensive
to answer, is not a valid objection.¹⁰
However, Rules 5.3(1) (b) and 5.25(3) allow for an objection
that it would be wholly too onerous to inform oneself in the circumstances.
The person questioning has some scope to develop a topic, or to approach a question in an
indirect way.¹¹

5-9
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.2(2)
Irrelevant examination questions should not be allowed just because they are brief and will do
little harm.¹
Whether the plaintiff has insurance is irrelevant, even in a suit for pure economic loss.²
The witness cannot ask for the reasons for questions before answering them.³
Demanding too much detail and too many background documents on examination for discovery
(questioning) can go beyond what is relevant and material.⁴
5. Miscellaneous
Foundational questions (such as who are a company’s directors) are harmless and
pragmatically permissible.⁵
The tests for relevance at trial and on discovery are still different,⁶
so an order to produce a
record on discovery does not bar its owner’s arguing at trial that it is not relevant enough to be
admissible then.⁷
Relevance of an issue to any party lets all parties examine for discovery on it.⁸
Reasons for thinking a document irrelevant or insufﬁciently relevant need not be given.⁹
How a party’s written policies or manuals interpreted a basic undoubted duty, can be material.¹⁰
Where much of a document is relevant and material, clearly non-relevant parts of it may be
“redacted” (blacked out or masked).¹¹
Presumably the test of relevant and material means that
the parts redacted out must be of no primary or secondary relevance, not merely borderline or
doubtful.
C. Examples
1. Records
There are many cases on what records are material enough.¹²

Discipline of a driver after the accident, or his other accidents, are not relevant enough for an examination
for discovery in a motor vehicle suit; it is tertiary relevance. Auer v. Lionstone Hldg.2005 ABCA 78, 363 AR
84.

examples in the C. P. E. , Chapter 27, Parts D.5 and D.6. But be wary of pre-1999 cases; the Rule narrower

5-10
R.5.2(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Relying on invoices for a summary dismissal motion when the invoices were not in any afﬁdavit
of records by those parties, and despite previous orders to divulge and produce all relevant and
material records, is clear breach and the invoices are obviously relevant.¹
Where the plaintiff sues for a ﬁre and his claim is partly subrogated because his insurer partly
paid him, whether documents went to the plaintiff’s insurer neither adds to nor detracts from their
producibility to the defendant.²
If a record/document exists in multiple formats (including its native format), those formats must
be produced. There are advantages to access to the native format. If timing or possible alteration
are issues, then metadata are producible.³
2. Computer Searching
Finding and producing to the opponent electronic documents simply by using certain search
terms on the computer and then listing or tagging hundreds of thousands of documents, without
examining them for relevance, may be proper where there are allegations of fraud and there
are apparently interlocking transactions, and where the other side has been complaining about
failure to disclose records. An electronic copy of the search results from the hard drive was also
provided, so the recipient of it of course could do its own searching within that group.⁴
If there is a large volume of records barely relevant and material, R.5.3 lets the party
possessing them have the court either allow their omission, or impose terms such as costs or
inspection instead. A litigant ﬁrst used a computer search, then manually reviewed any document
with an electronic “hit”. The search terms seemed adequate and there was no contrary evidence.⁵
3. Policy, Motives, Thoughts or Belief
In a suit against a government ofﬁce, setting policy has secondary relevance, and ﬁles on it
are producible.⁶
Similar clauses to those litigated, but in contracts with non-parties, were held
producible as relevant to individual custom and reasonable expectations, and equitable relief.⁷
[Footnote 12 continued from prior page]
after that. The plaintiff in a personal injury case does not have to disclose the amounts of settlements and
their breakdowns in a prior action, as this is irrelevant: Milicevic v. Jakubec 2005 ABQB 654, 385 AR 148.
Value of property to be valued, at the wrong date, is relevant enough: Mustard v. Brache, supra. Someone
accused of dishonesty had to produce her whole previous criminal record, even crimes not involving
dishonesty: House v. Wawanesa Mut. Ins. Co.2006 ABQB 450, 402 AR 269. A claim of a constructive
trust makes relevant what happened to the funds, even if the value of the funds is known so discovery of
accounting records was proper: Yellowstone Prop. Consultants v. Abusalim 2008 ABCA 348, 440 AR
182. Similar injuries in collisions 5 years apart made papers from the other suit relevant enough: Jomha v.
McAllister (Hicks Est. ) 2008 ABQB 597, 96 Alta LR(4th) 340. Arguable there had been a scheme to get
away the customers of the other company, so enough relevance for production of invoices by the impugned
company to those customers: Poppies Int. v. Int. Hasco Trading Co. (M) 2008 ABQB 531, 95 Alta LR(4th)
292.

5-11
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.2(2)
Questions about what the witness understood or thought about their obligations are usually
not relevant or material because the test is objective. But what the witness understood, expected,
or what opinion he or she held at a speciﬁc past time, are questions of fact.¹
4. Similar Facts
Similar fact evidence (e. g. conduct of a party in other parallel cases) may be relevant enough
for discovery.²
The papers in foreign litigation over a very similar contract may be producible?³
Similar fact evidence is possible in a civil case, and so such topics can be relevant and material,
e. g. to see if impugned conduct was an accident or coincidence, or deliberate; “secondary” vs.
“tertiary” is not a helpful tool in such cases.⁴
If a plaintiff claims a variety of medical problems from one accident, and the defendant denies
that it caused the problems, and alleges that the plaintiff has had many previous accidents and
alleges many pre-existing conditions, then asking for records of previous medical diagnoses and
previous insurance pay-outs, is proper.⁵
5. Duration
If the duration of the duty sued over is not clear, discovery should not be limited to the shorter
possible time.⁶
If mental capacity is the issue, production of all medical records for 10 years goes
too far back and the 3 years before the sale was made, sufﬁces.⁷
Where the issue is value of
shares, later events not known at the time of sale cannot be relevant and material.⁸
6. Settlements
A settlement in the same suit with one party is relevant and material only to the question of
over-recovery, which arises later, and it need not be disclosed in the usual discovery early in the
suit.⁹
A settlement with this defendant in a separate suit by a different plaintiff on different facts
may well not be relevant and material in this suit, even if the legal principles involved may be the
same. A myriad of factors come into play in decisions to settle.¹⁰

(¶’s 60-65).
2 R. v. W. A. , supra; T. W. v. Univ. of Alta. Hosp.2000 ABQB 387, 267 AR 169; Nelson v. R. (1999) 243 AR 94
(old Rules). But cf. Cheyne v. R. , supra, Apotex v. R.2003 ABQB 750, 336 AR 219, and other cases cited
with them. Other complaints factually similar to the facts in the suit are relevant enough to be producible:
Cheyne v. R.2003 ABQB 244, 31 CPC(5th) 239. Or if the complaint is delay, the speed of handling others’
cases may be relevant enough also: Apotex v. R.2003 ABQB 750, 336 AR 219. Or agreements with others:
Goodswimmer (Sturgeon L. I. B. ) v. R.2005 ABQB 479, 385 AR 131. Complaints about other people
should be limited in time: K. v. E. K. (#2) 2004 ABQB 159, 362 AR 195 (¶’s 99 ff. ) A driver’s previous record

5-12
R.5.2(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
7. Later Steps
On precautions or remedies taken after the bad event, see the Araam case.¹
When ﬂow of money between various parties is a relevant issue, ﬁnancial documents showing
how the money moved around is relevant and material. The identity of who does business with
the parties is not relevant.²
8. Many Undertakings
That the party questioning asked for hundreds of undertakings does not necessarily show
that he was unduly “ﬁshing”; sometimes the two parties have a signiﬁcantly different perspective
on materiality, and hence on how to prepare to answer questions; and a suit over a number of
transactions entails many details.³
Or maybe the witness’ preparation was inadequate.
9. Financial Statements
The whole child-support system depends on adequate, accurate and timely ﬁnancial
disclosure. It is critical, and is a corollary of the legal duty to pay support. Conversely, disclosure is
inherently invasive, and disclosure duties are not endless and have limits and are proportionate,
and are not to be sought for collateral purposes or to revisit issues already resolved, nor to ﬁsh or
seek irrelevant or immaterial information. One common method of getting disclosure is R.12.41,
but it has limits, such as relevance and materiality, as explained in R.5.2.⁴
The court cannot order disclosure of income etc. , in a childless marriage, if every issue has
been formally settled, and no pleading attacks the settlement.⁵
In a suit alleging breach of a contract to share company proﬁts with the plaintiff, the defendant
denying the agreement, audit ﬁnancial records, and what changes were made to the ﬁnancial
statements of the company, are plainly relevant and producible. The defendant wanted precise
details before producing ﬁnancial records, but details are impossible without seeing the records.⁶
If information is needed to satisfy a certain formula in the governing contract, items not in the
formula are not needed.⁷
Damages for repudiation of a lease depend upon the agreed rent to date
and the present value of future rent. There is no authority for calculating it by reference to ﬁnancial,
tax, or accounting records of the owner. Such examination for discovery questions are not proper.⁸
10. Split Trial
In a class action where proceedings have been bifurcated, relevance should be determined
with regard to the common issues.⁹

5-13
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.3(1)
D. Miscellaneous
There is a criticism of objections to questions based on the breadth or precise wording of the
question. It is wrong to refuse any answer at all, if the objection is over-breadth. Counsel should
discuss this, and try to resolve it.¹
If a Chambers Judge uses the right test for relevance of records to be revealed, applying
it to the particular evidence can be upset on appeal only for palpable and overriding error. A
fortiori if he or she was the case management judge.²
What are proper examination for discovery
(questioning) questions is a discretionary decision owed deference on appeal.³
On a needless motion on the relevance of many papers in a long-term disability ﬁle of a
plaintiff in a personal injury suit, the case management judge decided by reviewing them.⁴
If relevance of a document is not certain on a motion to produce, and it may turn out at trial to
be irrelevant, a conﬁdentiality order may have to be made when pre-trial production is ordered.⁵
If one party insists on production of an arguably irrelevant document, which is harmless,
should the party holding it just acquiesce? That depends on the circumstances, especially how
acrimonious and distrustful the parties and their counsel are. In such a war to the death, it can
lead to a lot of lengthy expensive irrelevant disclosure.⁶
Because of R.5.2(2), a person does not bar himself from later moving to dismiss for prosecution,
by asking earlier for production of more information.⁷
Modiﬁcation or Waiver of This Part
5.3(1) The Court may modify or waive any right or power under a rule in this Part
or make any order warranted in the circumstances if
(a) a person acts or threatens to act in a manner that is vexatious,
evasive, abusive, oppressive, improper or tediously lengthy, or
(b) the expense, delay, danger or difﬁculty in complying with a rule
would be grossly disproportionate to the likely beneﬁt.
(2) In addition to making a procedural order, the Court may do any one or more
of the following:
(a) make a costs award under Part 10 [Lawyers’ Charges, Recoverable
Costs of Litigation, and Sanctions] or require an advance payment
against costs payable, or both;
(b) increase or decrease the amount of interest to which a person is
entitled;
(c) order future questioning to be conducted before a judge, application’s
judge or person designated by the Court;

1 Allen v. Telfer (M) 2018 ABQB 876, JCC 1501 10054 (¶’s 5-10).
2 Enron Can. v. Anadarko Can. Enr. , supra.
3 Adderley v.1400467 Alta.2014 ABCA 291, 580 AR 319 (one JA) (¶ 16).
4 Most were irrelevant but harmless. A few had slight relevance: Burton v. Brice 2006 ABQB 523.

R.5.3(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
(d) make any other order respecting the action or an application or
proceeding the Court considers necessary in the circumstances.¹
[Alta. Reg.124/10;136/22]
Information Note
A procedural order may include any order described in rule 1.4 [Procedural orders] or an
order under any other rule respecting practice or procedure.

Related Provisions
1.2(4) (remedies to be proportional) ; 1.4 (authority to make procedural orders) ; 2.8 (discovery

in class proceedings); 4.22 (security for costs) ; 5.1 (Purpose of Part 5; 5.19 (limits on
questioning); 5.25(2) (c) (unreasonable or unnecessary questions) ; 5.25(3) (too onerous to
inform oneself); 12.35 (advance costs in family matters) ; 12.37 (affidavit of records in family
proceedings).
Note that this Rule applies to both parties, not merely to the party seeking information.
This Rule lets the court intervene in two situations. The ﬁrst is where the demands made
during questioning (examination for discovery) or disclosure of records have become very
excessive.²
The second is where disclosure has been used for an improper purpose, and where a
full answer to some of the demands would produce expense, inconvenience or danger completely
disproportionate to the likely beneﬁt.³
Rule 5.3 sets a tough test. That a witness may know little is not enough.⁴
Delay under R.5.3
refers to future delay of the suit, not to past delay in making the request to question.⁵
Rule 5.3(1)
(b) on grossly disproportionate expense, delay, danger, or difﬁculty is not even close to being met
by being busy, and the Rule is not to be trivialized.⁶
Even if the Rule is triggered, the court need
not bar disclosure, and may impose terms, such as costs.
Much older powers to restrain are in subrules 5.17(2) and 5.19. They get little attention, yet are
salutary, for excessive questioning (examination for discovery) usually takes the form of examining
a considerable number of employees and ex-employees.
The ratio of questions asked to questions and answers from the transcript read in at trial does
not measure excessive length of questioning (examination for discovery), especially when the
party questioning is the defendant who has a chance to cross-examine at trial before he calls his
case.⁷
Trial read-ins are only one of a number of legitimate purposes for questioning (examination
for discovery).
The previous rule (R.216.1) was once used to excuse paying conduct money for a witness
from another province, which seems dubious.⁸

5-15
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.4(1)
On questioning, if a party could be required to identify which ﬁles are relevant to the claim,
then an order to “list” them as part of discovery of records is proper. Semble, that is not too
much work or expense even if the total number of ﬁles (relevant and irrelevant) is almost 15, 000,
especially if there is no evidence of expense etc.¹
That questioning (examination for discovery) of 100 plaintiffs would be unreasonable, was
a ground to certify a representative action.²
The Court may limit the purpose and scope of an
questioning of a class member or subclass member, in a class action.³
There are heavy penalties for improper disclosure of records got through the disclosure
process, so the danger of such improper disclosure has little weight. And privacy concerns can
be handled by restricting later dissemination of information. Restricting the question asked would
need evidence of a grossly disproportionate danger and why some restrictive order would not
sufﬁce.⁴
A request to defer giving disclosure of records until one has moved to strike or stay the suit
against that defendant should be granted in respect of that defendant.⁵
Rule 5.3 now extends to conduct of a medical examination of an injured person.⁶
If an afﬁdavit of records is unduly prolix, one might have hoped that R.5.3 might assist, but
doubts are suggested in the Adderley case.⁷
This Rule (and R.5.19) allowed setting aside an appointment for questioning (examination
for discovery) issued in response to a summary judgment application. Questioning for such
an application is discretionary and here the party seeking to question had raised the topics for
questioning and must know the facts.⁸
Two questions asked what position on liability or coverage the insurer would have taken
if a certain use of the vehicle had occurred. Those need not be answered because they were
hypothetical and irrelevant.⁹

appoint a corporate representative.

(a) inform themselves of relevant and material records and relevant and
material information before being questioned under this Division,
(b) if questioned under this Division, bring to the questioning any
records likely to be required in respect of which there is no claim of
privilege, and

On correcting errors in the answer, see R.5.27.
An incorporated party to a suit has no mouth or ears, and so a natural person must be selected
to be questioned on its behalf. In Alberta (unlike some provinces), the corporation selects its
spokesperson. The court is slow to interfere with that selection, for this person’s answers can
seriously harm the corporation, so the question is not who has the most knowledge. Semble, a
company’s selection of a spokesman with little personal knowledge does not allow the court to
order questioning another ofﬁcial (mayor) not otherwise examinable.¹
Failure of the company’s spokesperson to prepare for questions might be contempt in the right
case, but it would have to be a very clear substantial default with no prompt redress and a direct
unequivocal order?²
If a corporate party disclosed all its records in its afﬁdavit of records and had been then
questioned about them, and it is conceded that there are no other records, and if the employees
and ofﬁcers who are parties can be questioned about those same records, and those employees
and ofﬁcers’ afﬁdavits of records say they have no records, ordering a further afﬁdavit for those
people will serve no purpose.³
One existing and one former provincial Cabinet Minister were not ordered to be examined
for discovery in a lawsuit about changes in mining policy. Nine other government ofﬁcials had
already been questioned, and excerpts from transcripts of that were before the court. Rule 5.17(1)
(b) says ofﬁcers or former ofﬁcers may be examined if they have or appear to have relevant and
material information acquired because of holding that ofﬁce, and the Proceedings Against the
Crown Act makes that apply to suits against the provincial Crown (as this one is). It takes special

circumstances to examine Ministers; the Minister must be the person best informed as to the

Subdivision 2
Disclosing and Identifying Relevant and Material Records
When Afﬁdavit of Records Must Be Served
5.5(1) Every party must serve an afﬁdavit of records on each of the other parties
in accordance with the time period speciﬁed in subrule (2), (3) or (4).
(2) The plaintiff must serve an afﬁdavit of records on each of the other parties
within 3 months after the date the plaintiff is served with a statement of defence,
or the ﬁrst statement of defence if more than one is served.
(3) The defendant must serve an afﬁdavit of records on each of the other parties
within 2 months after the date the defendant is served with the plaintiff’s afﬁdavit
of records.
(4) A third party defendant who has ﬁled a statement of defence must, within 3
months after that ﬁling, serve an afﬁdavit of records on each of the other parties.²
[Alta. Reg.124/10;140/13]
Information Note
An affidavit of records must not be filed unless it is needed for the purpose of an application
or at trial. See rule 5.32 [When information can be used].

Related Provisions
2.8 (affidavit of records in class proceedings) ; 3.68(4) (striking pleadings for failure to file

affidavit of records); 5.13 (obtaining records from third parties) ; 5.32 (affidavits of records not
to be filed); 6.26 (Anton Piller orders).

R.5.5(4) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Rr.12.37 and 12.40. On class proceedings, see R.2.8. On alternative methods of production, see
the Trautwien case.¹
On production of records and information in family proceedings, see the
special Rules at R.12.37 and 12.40 (notice to disclose). On class proceedings, see R.2.8. Rule
5.32 provides that afﬁdavits of records are not to be ﬁled.
B. Basic Method is Afﬁdavit
Every party²
to a lawsuit must swear an afﬁdavit³
listing all the relevant end material records
which he, she or it ever possessed. On subrogated parties taking an active role, see the Jahnke
case.⁴
Discovery of records does more than disclose records; it produces sworn evidence that there
are no more (undisclosed) records.⁵
The Rules do not contemplate one party searching through the records of the opposition.⁶
An
Anton Piller order is an extraordinary remedy that displaces the normal rules on discovery of
records, and enables the plaintiff to attend at the premises of the defendant, without notice, and
take possession of the records of the defendant.⁷
Each party is required to reveal under oath in its afﬁdavit of records all the relevant and
material records in its possession. Each party’s solicitor also has personal duties and liabilities
here.⁸
Solicitors must inform their clients at once when the Rules create a duty to produce, and
explain fully the nature and importance of the process involved. The solicitor must police the
production process, and be vigilant for classes of records which the client seems not to have
produced.⁹
The solicitor advises the client what is producible, and what is privileged. A solicitor
who induces a client to swear an incomplete afﬁdavit of records may himself or herself violate
the Criminal Code, and certainly exposes himself or herself to personal costs and to professional
discipline, if the job is done carelessly.¹⁰

C. Who Swears
See also R.5.4 and notes to it.
Where the party is a company, a minor, a mentally incompetent, or someone else who cannot
personally do the job, someone else may swear the afﬁdavit for the party.²
Aside from that however, one party cannot swear for another where there are multiple
defendants; a single afﬁdavit of records served on behalf of all of them does not sufﬁce. Each
party must swear one.³
The previous references to “adverse parties”, and “persons on whose behalf” the litigation
is prosecuted are not found in the 2010 Rules on production of records,⁴
though R.5.17 on
questioning uses the ﬁrst phrase.
The court has an equitable power to make a Norwich order for discovery from a non-party.
The tests for it are reviewed.⁵
Such orders are exceptional, and a high degree of conﬁdentiality
is expected for banking records.⁶
A Norwich order will not be given to disclose what normal
discovery of the existing parties and their records, will disclose.⁷
A Norwich order was extended
to transactions not complained of, for tracing, and accompanied by secrecy orders.⁸
A non-party
against whom such an obligation is imposed (e. g. a telephone or email provider) is entitled to
reasonable reimbursement of labor or expenses incurred solely to obey the order, if there was no
previous statutory or other legal duty to do that task.⁹
The court has inherent power to order disclosure of records from a non-party, e. g. a company
in which a party has an interest. It is to be done sparingly, and notice must be given of the motion
to the non-party.¹⁰
Ordinarily disclosure from a non-party, is not possible.¹¹

involved somehow in wrongdoing, or it be done to identify wrongdoers, preserve evidence, and
trace and preserve assets? Evidence must be given of these matters and of the importance of
the matter.¹
Merely ﬁling a claim for support as an adult interdependent partner did not entitle one to full
disclosure (which one could get by ﬁling a notice), but the majority differed as to what more was
needed. One thought a bit of evidence, irrespective of whether the other side controverted it. The
other thought that the chambers judge here had severed issues and discovery under R.7.1(1) (c),
1.4(2), or as abuse of process.²
A plaintiff proposing a class action could not force a defendant (which had not yet defended)
to give disclosure of records before the certiﬁcation motion was heard, with limited exceptions
proposed by the defendant.³
One can probably get disclosure of records from a party who has not defended.⁴
D. Type of Suit
One can get disclosure of records in any type of civil action, even one for a penalty or forfeiture.⁵
To get production of records, the applicant need not identify a speciﬁc document.⁶
Discovery is open in a surrogate proceeding if a full inquiry is needed, and though the
Surrogate Rules speak of discovery before trial, they do not speak of pre-application discovery.
Early disclosure has many beneﬁts.⁷
E. Testing Disclosure
An afﬁdavit of records swearing that certain documents are privileged does not create any
presumption that they are, but it is prima facie evidence of the claim of privilege, and so sufﬁces
absent any contrary evidence or cross-examination. If there is such contrary evidence or cross-
examination, the onus of proving privilege remains on the party asserting it.⁸
Now it is clear that one needs no permission in order to cross-examine on an afﬁdavit of
records.⁹
Assuming that leave was needed, it should be given here because it was shown that
electronic records by a material witness had been lost.¹⁰
Failure to give a supplemental afﬁdavit of records as ordered by a judge is usually not bad
enough initially to strike pleadings or dismiss the suit, if belated compliance occurs.¹¹

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.6(1)
F. Duration and Timing
Trial ends the right to disclosure of records. There is no disclosure of records pending appeal,¹
even if there is a pending motion for new evidence.²
For appeal purposes, production or disclosure
of records is discretionary, as is a conﬁdentiality order.³
The defendant’s demanding some sort of
production of records after noting in default is too late, especially when it is speciﬁc and relates
to issues never pleaded.⁴
The duty to ﬁle an afﬁdavit of records within three months is not removed by intent of that party
to move for summary judgment. A party which does not want to have to ﬁle an afﬁdavit of records,
within the three months should move for summary judgment.⁵
If the parties have an agreement to defer disclosure of records but it can be cancelled by either
party, as soon as one party cancels that, the whole agreement is gone, and both sides are obliged
to deliver afﬁdavits of records within the proper time period. Production of records to the other
litigant without doing an afﬁdavit of records is not sufﬁcient.⁶
A self-represented plaintiff in a complex case got a time extension, though incomplete medical
records would not have sufﬁced to extend time.⁷
If one defendant ﬁles an afﬁdavit of records on
time and his co-defendant thinks that it is for both defendants and covers all the records, is that
sufﬁcient excuse for the latter’s failure to ﬁle in time?⁸
Form and Contents of Afﬁdavit of Records
5.6(1) An afﬁdavit of records must
(a) be in Form 26, and
(b) disclose all records that
(i) are relevant and material to the issues in the action, and
(ii) are or have been under the party’s control.
(2) The afﬁdavit of records must also specify
(a) which of the records are under the control of the party on whose
behalf the afﬁdavit is made,
(b) which of those records, if any, the party objects to produce and the
grounds for the objection,
(c) for those records for which there is no objection to produce, a notice
stating
(i) the time when the record may be inspected, which must be
within 10 days after the afﬁdavit is served, and

A. What are Records Covered
If there is enough relevance, these Rules in Subdivision 2 require disclosure of every method
(other than human memory) by which information may be recorded, and (subject to relevance and
materiality²
) this includes many things:
1. any written paper or note, however informal, such as appointments noted on a calendar;
2. any computer record, including previous drafts or backup copies of letters;
3. bookkeeping entries, made manually or by computer;
4. photographs (ﬁlm, printed, or digital) ;
5. sound recordings, movies, camera memories, videotapes, CDs, DVDs, computer discs,
thumb drives, or ﬂash keys;³

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous R.187.1, re-enacted by Alta. Reg.172/99. Previously 1968 R.188 from English O.
31 r.13 of 1936 (#3) ; Judic. Ord. r.192; Ont. Rr.467, 468 of 1897. Similar to 1914 R.366 with changes at the
beginning; like 1944 C. R.227. Subrule (3) was new in 1999, but codiﬁed what was implicit before.
2 See R.5.2.
3 A party to a civil suit can request the disclosure of recordings of private communications intercepted by
the state in the course of a criminal investigation. The records in question are “documents” for discovery
purposes, whether or not they have been transcribed: Imperial Oil v. Jacques 2014 SCC 66, [2014] 3 SCR
287 (¶ 32).

5-23
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.6(3)
6. signs or inscriptions, e. g. on vehicles, buildings, or graves;
7. logs, tapes, or other recordings kept or produced by instruments.
If a record contains parts relevant and material and other parts privileged or otherwise not
relevant, then a party need disclose only the former portions, or produce the whole document with
the other portions blacked out. The court may inspect the full document to settle disputes about
scope of redaction.¹
B. Importance of Disclosure
The Rules on disclosure of records are mandatory and important,²
yet counsel often neglect or
badly handle them. Carefully-prepared afﬁdavits of records lay a good foundation for questioning
(examination for discovery). They save a good deal of time during questioning (examination
for discovery) by informing the parties beforehand, reducing undertakings, removing the need
for many routine questions,³
and removing the need to mark most exhibits. The Rules require
production of records before oral discovery.
Because an afﬁdavit of records and their production are key aspects of civil litigation, and
depend on each party’s and each counsel’s diligence and integrity, deliberate withholding of
important records, or producing (late) altered versions of them, led to an extra $200, 000 costs.⁴
C. Copies of Records
Often one relevant record exists in many copies or forms or formats. For example, there is
the paper original of a letter, its paper ﬁle copy, a draft of it, cross-copies sent to other people,
the electronic copy on the computer where it was prepared,⁵
and photocopies of it which various
people have made. Or an invoice may come in sets of ﬁve (carbon) copies, each on different
coloured paper. Every one of those is a separate record, and if any copy is important, maybe all
should be disclosed in an afﬁdavit of records; disclosing one of them is sometimes not enough.
But ordinarily, duplicate documents need not be listed.⁶
And if timing or possible alteration are
issues, then metadata are producible.⁷
For the afﬁant to look carefully at copies of important records before swearing an afﬁdavit
of records is not pedantry, for two reasons. First, extra copies of important records often bear
marginal notes, receipt stamps, or routing initials. Second, a relevant record rarely lives alone; an
unproduced record usually lives in an unproduced ﬁle of several relevant records. No ﬁle is ever
just a duplicate of another ﬁle. The order and attachment of papers in a ﬁle often tells a story. So
do strings of emails. Papers in a ﬁle usually turn out to be related to each other, though how is
often not evident at ﬁrst. It is often surprising how many different places a business or government
uses to house records about one transaction: e. g. different departments, different branches, and
ﬁles kept by individual ofﬁcials. Electronic communications and various computer storage systems
multiply that further.

R.5.6(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
If a document or recording is relevant enough, it must be disclosed in an afﬁdavit or records,
even if privilege is claimed or going to be claimed, for it.¹
If a record is generally producible, parts of it may be redacted only if irrelevant, revelation is
harmful, and existing protections are insufﬁcient. All three branches must be met.²
D. Objections to Production
As set out in Form 26, the body of the afﬁdavit of records must state grounds for any objection
to production, and it must say which records ﬁt within which objection.³
But reasons for thinking a
document irrelevant or insufﬁciently relevant need not be given.⁴
A Master criticized a party or its adviser for blacking out part of a document because of
supposed irrelevance.⁵
But that is both proper and necessary when a diary, ledger, or the like
contains entries on a number of completely unrelated matters.
The Crown has common-law immunity from any discovery, though where the provincial Crown
is the defendant, provincial legislation on Crown liability often makes it subject to discovery.⁶
But
in child welfare proceedings, the Crown may be subject to a rudimentary form of production of
records.⁷
Discovery of accounting records is not premature just because the suit is over the right to
see them, especially when the parties are joint venturers, not competitors, and the documents
are relevant to other non-accounting issues too.⁸
The real question is severing issues, or undue
onerousness of discovery.⁹
Even if the opposite party uses a release (consent) signed by the owner and thus gets the
relevant records from a custodian, giving such a release does not even meet the obligation (under
R.5.6) to produce all relevant and material records. The party with possession and control must
go to the work of getting and producing those records. A party cannot foist on its opponent its own
failure to give discovery.¹⁰
Privacy legislation does not bar court-ordered production, as it expressly does not limit the
information available by law in a legal proceeding.¹¹
General privacy legislation does not bar one mortgagee from giving a mortgage statement
(how much it will cost to discharge the mortgage) to another mortgagee of the same property,
given the latter’s right to redeem that other mortgage. A court order is a statutory exception
to privacy. The court found implied consent by the mortgagor (debtor). Privacy legislation does
not cut down courts’ powers, nor interfere with the Rules of Court about production. Another
mortgagee was not a stranger to the court proceeding.¹²

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.6(3)
When legislation requires disclosure ahead of time to make available evidence for civil claims,
one should not assume that a judge will screen this disclosure or order it, though if there is a
dispute over disclosure or use then a judge can rule on that. But semble, screening may be
needed if the disclosure is wiretaps of phone conversations?¹
Apparently Alberta courts have ruled that portions of a document produced under the
discovery of records may be redacted out if those portions are not relevant and material. However,
the Saskatchewan Court of Appeal partly disagrees, and says such redaction is permissible only
if the portion to be redacted also would be signiﬁcantly harmful if disclosed. Instead of redaction,
often the better way in Saskatchewan to protect secrets and prevent harm is conﬁdentiality orders,
or restriction on who can see the full document.²
E. Records Now Gone
A party need not create a record, nor have someone else create it, even if at one time the party
had a copy which has since been lost.³
A party need not even create a roadmap to its producible
records, nor need it scan those records into a machine-readable form, if it has not yet done so.⁴
Schedule 3 of the afﬁdavit of records lists records which the deponent once had but no longer
has. The most common examples are original letters which the party sent by post or courier, or
cheques which the party cashed. Records routinely discarded should be listed here, if they really
were discarded, and are not in dead storage or awaiting destruction.⁵
Counselling someone to destroy documents relevant to a pending lawsuit is contempt,⁶
especially when it is done in the face of a court order to preserve records. An effective remedy
requires at least payment by the contemnor of full-indemnity costs (in the trial-level court and on
appeal).⁷
If a party destroys a record which would have told strongly for or against him (depending on
the record’s contents), there is an inference (e. g. for trial) that it would have told against him.⁸
On records seized by the opposing party under an Anton Piller order, see the PTW case.⁹
F. Use of Information Obtained
The opposite party cannot use any document or information got through discovery, for any
purpose except the suit in which he gets it, without court leave.¹⁰
The defendants inadvertently disclosed to the plaintiff documents which they should not have.
When they discovered their mistake, the plaintiff’s lawyer returned them and destroyed the notes

no good evidence of relevance was shown, no spoliation was found, and no adverse inference was drawn:
Ezzedine v. Dalgard 2006 ABQB 826, 405 AR 296 (¶’s 20-27).
9 PTW Can. v. Smith 2024 ABKB 83, JCC 2201 05725 (Feb 16) (¶’s 31-44).
10 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 42, Part G, and the commentary to R.5.33, infra.

5-26
R.5.6(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
which he had made about them. Later some of the same documents were deliberately disclosed
to him, and he had to redo some of the same work. The judge awarded him his thrown-away
costs.¹
See R.5.8n. F.
Where a party gives a copy of some records to the opposing counsel, who reads and recalls
them, the recipient can use that information if it is not privileged. It makes no difference that the
party giving later realized that he or she need not have disclosed the records because they are
irrelevant.²
If the party producing documents in another language does not intend to use them as part of
his or her evidence, especially if the respondents are ﬂuent in that language, it is the respondents
who must arrange translations of that production, especially if it is not bulky. Case law is reviewed.
Who ultimately pays for translation can be decided by costs awards at the end of the litigation.³
G. “Control” of a Record
1. Legal Test
A party to a suit must disclose in its afﬁdavit of records all records in its “control”. Previously
the Rule referred to its “possession or power”. “Control” includes all records held for the party
by its solicitors, agents or other ﬁduciaries, or by bailees.⁴
For example, a physician holds the
information in his or her “chart” (ﬁle) for the patient.⁵
In law, a hospital’s position may differ a little,
but a former patient has a statutory right to get a copy of his hospital ﬁle, so the patient must
disclose hospital records too.
That may be true whenever a party has the legal right to get copies of records from a non-
party.⁶
The obligation of a party (on request from the other side) to produce papers held by non-
parties depends on whether those others are under a legal obligation to provide the papers or
copies of them to that party.⁷
If they are not, then the proper route for the opponent is to move
under R.5.13. It is not enough simply to ask whether it would be “reasonable” for the party to
request the papers from the non-party.⁸
Nor is it enough that one could go to court to get an order
enough.⁹

2 222 and 223 Baseline Rd. v. World Health Edm. (M) 2021 ABQB 22, JCE 1903 13032 (Jan 8) (¶’s 76-115).

5-27
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.6(3)
A party need produce only those records within its power, and what an independent contractor
has is not within the power of the person who hires the independent contractor.¹
“Control” requires an unconditional, legal, exclusive right to inspect or copy the records in
question, not a right which everyone has, and not the ability to ask the court for access which the
court may or may not grant.²
Nor does a party have control over records which he or she holds purely for someone else,
and in which the party has no beneﬁcial interest.³
A civil servant who is sued probably need not
disclose government ﬁles, for example. The administrator of the Motor Vehicles Accident Claims
Act is not obliged to produce an afﬁdavit of records.⁴
Documents owned and held by an individual
are not in the power of a company which he controls, and are not producible in a suit to which it
is a party, but he or she is not.⁵
But though most of a lawyer’s ﬁles’ contents may be privileged, they are enough under the
lawyer’s control that he must list them in his afﬁdavit of records if he is sued. Production of them
is a different question.⁶
When various defendants all list the same documents, they may not have to disclose which
defendant controls which document, as a document could be under the possession and control of
more than one person. A group may hold documents together in common.⁷
The party must get and produce such records; it is not enough to give the opponent permission
to go and get them.⁸
Nor is expense a reason not to produce, nor need the opponent reimburse
or share the expense.⁹
2. Examples
Where a party has manufacturing or production facilities which it controls, then all records
relating to production at those facilities are documents under the control of that party, even if
differing companies have actual possession of them. If a party leaves control or management of
its factory in the hands of somebody else, that does not mean that it ceases to be the property
of the party, nor the business of the party. But if some records do not relate to the business or
operations of the party, but to the business and operations of another afﬁliated company, they are
not within the control of the party.¹⁰

R.5.7(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
A City with a police commission does not control police records.¹
A corporation can only act through its ofﬁcers and directors, so if one of them has authority
to instruct the corporation’s solicitors, he or she has control of the solicitor’s ﬁle for discovery
purposes, even after the company is dissolved, at least for the 6 years stated in the Alberta
Business Corporations Act, s.26.²
If an insurer or similar association provides a member or an insured a free defence by retaining
and paying lawyers to run that defence, both insurer and insured are clients and own the lawyers’
ﬁle and have access to it (though one alone cannot disclose the contents to someone else, let
alone give him the ﬁle).³
H. Description of Records⁴
The aim here is to be able at trial to see whether someone is producing a record which
had not been disclosed earlier; so a generic description in the afﬁdavit will not sufﬁce, even
for privileged records. Each individual record in the bundle is to be uniquely numbered (or the
bundle’s description and marking and the number together must be unique). The contents of the
records do not have to be disclosed.⁵
Claims for privilege must be tied to speciﬁc records and the
grounds for privilege, or type of privilege, identiﬁed.⁶
Persuasive evidence of the privilege cannot
be insisted on, as it might well defeat the privilege.⁷
On producible records, see further R.5.7 below.
If on questioning (examination for discovery) the defendant could be required to identify which
ﬁles are relevant to the claim (e. g. which ones the plaintiff worked on), then an order to “list” them
as part of discovery of records is proper. That need not require making a new list, and may be
satisﬁed by identifying them on the existing list in the afﬁdavit of records.⁸
There should not be a long list of thousands of individual records. There is an odd suggestion
that the Rules do little to let the court remedy that, except for R.5.1(2) ; sed quaere.⁹
On producing a long purely-electronic selection and listing of documents, plus an electronic
version of all their contents, where there are allegations of fraud in many transactions, see the
Demb case.¹⁰
Producible Records
5.7(1) Each producible record in an afﬁdavit of records must
(a) be numbered in a convenient order, and
(b) be brieﬂy described.

4.5(1) (b) (ii) (protocol for organizing records).

The Rules now provide a speciﬁc form for the Afﬁdavit of Records (Schedule A, Form 26).¹
The afﬁdavit must identify the records disclosed, so that later one can unambiguously tell
whether a given sheet of paper or record was disclosed in the afﬁdavit or not.²
A vague description
such as “all correspondence between the defendant and his solicitor” is completely inadequate.
See a full discussion of the topic in the Banque Nationale³
case, and the Dorchak (CA) case.⁴
The Court of Appeal substantially restored Dorchak v. Krupka.⁵
The afﬁdavit need only identify
records disclosed, and numbered bundles will do for that?⁶
Individual descriptions of numerous
records were forbidden.⁷
But the law on that seems to be somewhat different now? Every record
which is material and relevant must be numbered and brieﬂy described in the afﬁdavit of records,
including those for which privilege is claimed. A privileged record can be bundled, so long as
it otherwise complies with R.5.7. Each document claimed to be privileged must be given the
particular reason for its non-production. The description must show how the record ﬁts within
the privilege claimed (and help others assess the privilege claim), but not reveal privileged
information.⁸
Now the 2020 amended version of R.5.8 again clariﬁes how to describe records.
Rule 5.6 requires each party to list (and produce) all “relevant and material” documents under
his or her control. Providing a written “release” (consent) to allow the other party to obtain a record

The 2020 amendment greatly expands the instructions about how to bundle and to describe
the records in an afﬁdavit of records where privilege or some other objection to production is
made, thus clarifying the law and restoring some of the older case law.
A. Non-producible Records in the Afﬁdavit
It is important that the description of privileged records reveal no privileged secrets. One
should not endanger privilege by disclosing too much detail about individual records in an afﬁdavit
of records.¹
But some description of a bundle in a big case would be practical,²
and R.5.7(2) (b) may
and certainly R.5.8 does require that.³
The schedule of privileged records therefore must give
numbered bundles (or some individual description) ; a host of cases hold that a vague generic
reference to records and correspondence does not sufﬁce.⁴
Yet such generic references to
privilege with no marking or description of the records in question, still wrongly persist in afﬁdavits
of records. That is just asking for trouble.
Where privileged records are concerned, the problems with requiring or giving individual
descriptions are even greater. It is well settled that a party need not reveal details about a privileged
record.⁵
The idea that the afﬁdavit must disclose enough objective details about a privileged record
(such as date, author, and addressee) to test the privilege, is completely mistaken.⁶
Bundling was
held sufﬁcient in one case.⁷

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
But the precise ground for claiming privilege must be given.¹
Too vague a claim, or an overbroad
claim, may mean that the party claiming privilege must pay costs, even if most of the papers are
in fact privileged.²
Because of the requirements of R.5.8, an afﬁdavit to claim or explain or justify privilege, even
an afﬁdavit ﬁled for a later dispute over privilege, can express an opinion or conclusion without
violating the common law or the Rules about expert evidence.³
Does the court have a duty to examine the papers if there is a contest over privilege?⁴
The afﬁdavit need not give reasons to think a record is not relevant or material enough to
include or produce.⁵
A case management decision about production of documents or privilege is discretionary and
gets appellate deference, especially where the court has had to balance many competing factors.
Indeed relevance or materiality of records, involves mixed fact and law and some discretion, and
is reviewed on the still more deferential standard of palpable and overriding error. The content
and scope of public interest immunity and solicitor-client privilege are reviewed on appeal for
correctness, but applying the law to particular documents is often mixed fact and law, reviewed for
palpable and overriding error.⁶
B. Conﬁdentiality and Privilege Generally
1. Cross-Reference
On the implied undertaking to keep discovery evidence conﬁdential, see R.5.33.
2. Authorities
Privilege is tricky to research, because many decided cases cite authority very selectively.
Worse still, courts outside Alberta sometimes seem hostile to privilege, though the Supreme Court
of Canada and the Alberta courts strongly support privilege.⁷
Some textbooks also seem unduly
hostile to privilege. Therefore, authority from other provinces should be handled very gingerly.
3. Incrimination?
It is doubtful whether a party may be able to decline to disclose a particular record on the
ground that it would tend to incriminate him or her.⁸

5-33
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
4. Conﬁdentiality
Apart from privilege and crimination, conﬁdentiality is no bar to disclosing or producing a
record.¹
Privilege and conﬁdentiality are not the same. Though conﬁdentiality in many contexts
may depend almost entirely on the intentions of the parties, that is not true of privilege. Parties
cannot by agreement make a document privileged which would not otherwise be privileged,
whether or not they put certain labels such as “without prejudice” on the documents.²
Directors’ non-consent, or foreign secrecy laws, do not let a company disobey a court order to
give an afﬁdavit of records.³
Foreign legislation barring disclosure of certain information about a
company without its consent is no bar to an afﬁdavit of records, if the company itself is the party
and required to provide the afﬁdavit of records.⁴
Conﬁdentiality does not ordinarily excuse a party from listing and producing a record. The
only exceptions to that duty are the established heads of privilege.⁵
Or a rare new head created
case by case?⁶
There is no class privilege for communications with sexual assault counsellors,
as Wigmore’s four criteria are not met.⁷
Those four criteria are useful when creating a novel head
of privilege is suggested,⁸
or in a few case-by-case types of privilege;⁹
but the 4 criteria are not
applicable to individual cases about established heads of privilege, e. g. solicitor-client privilege.¹⁰
If important business conﬁdences are in documents/records which must be disclosed
by discovery, to avoid real dangers, the court may order that only opposing counsel see the
documents, not employees of the opposing party.¹¹
After counsel in commercial litigation accepted disclosure of ﬁnancial records from his
opponent on individual cases about trust conditions of conﬁdentiality, the court would not lift the
conditions.¹²

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
5. Types of Privilege
Among the established categories of privilege are three important common ones: Professional
Privilege, Litigation Privilege, and the privilege over Settlement Negotiations Communications.
Each is discussed infra.¹
The most common ground for objection to production is for privileged records or
communications. There could be the usual litigation or solicitor-client privileges,²
without-prejudice
privilege,³
privilege for informers,⁴
and spousal privilege.⁵
There are other rarer categories.⁶
There
can be statutorily-created privilege, and it overrides relevance and usual discovery and production
duties.⁷
See the checklist in Part G below.
6. Statutory Exceptions?
See also Part C.2 below.
Statutes could create further exceptions (new privileges),⁸
but rarely do so. Conversely, a
statute does not override solicitor-client privilege by general words: speciﬁc clear abrogation of
solicitor-client privilege is necessary, and general words or a power to compel or inspect records,

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
is not enough.¹
Legislation must not impinge on the privilege more than absolutely necessary.²
Usually, conﬁdentiality provisions in statutes are presumed only to bar voluntary disclosure
(gossip), not to bar production under court process such as discovery of records.³
Even strong
conﬁdentiality sections in workers’ compensation legislation were held not to apply where the
Workers’ Compensation Board itself was a party to the suit.⁴
Conﬁdentiality legislation does not
bar the government department which administers the statute from using the information to
defend itself against a suit alleging failure to carry out the legislative duties properly.⁵
Now this privilege requires evidence, and the Crown has the onus of proof, and looking at the
documents themselves (plus a claim of privilege by certiﬁcate) may not be enough.⁶
Legislation referring to privilege is assumed to use such terms in their established common-
law meaning.⁷
Proof of privilege cannot be more stringent before a statutory tribunal than it is in a court of
law.⁸
If some other right conﬂicts with solicitor-client privilege, the privilege persists.⁹
There are a few true exceptions to producibility. They give some protection for conﬁdential
records. If the dangers or costs would grossly outweigh the beneﬁts, R.5.3 lets the court dispense
with production, or impose conditions.
Relevance of the record does not override privilege.¹⁰
7. Public Privilege and Immunity
Another privilege is Crown (or public interest) privilege.¹¹
It no longer is considered absolute,
and must meet certain criteria,¹²
even for Cabinet conﬁdentiality.¹³
Aside from common-law Crown

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
privilege, the Canada Evidence Act s.39 also protects communications in or for the Cabinet of
Canada,¹
and the courts can police this, though their practical powers are few.²
It does not cover
papers which the other side has already seen.³
Cabinet secrecy is important, and initial papers starting a debate should be equally protected.
A substantive analysis is needed to see where disclosure would reveal Cabinet deliberations. Text,
purpose, and context must be looked at. All disclosure legislation balances informing the public
with necessary conﬁdentiality. The legislation and concepts are constitutional. Cabinet conﬁdence
is needed for Ministerial responsibility and so for responsible government. Processes before a ﬁnal
decision must be conﬁdential. Decision making and debates can include false starts, blind alleys,
wrong turns, changes of mind, solicitation and rejection of advice, re-evaluation of priorities, and
re-weighing the relative factors, as a problem continues to be studied. Cabinet conﬁdence enables
collective ministerial responsibility and executive accountability, by allowing private disagreement
and candor. Premature disclosure of Cabinet secrets can cause or exacerbate ill-informed or
captious public or political criticism.⁴
Public interest privilege balances two competing interests: getting evidence for litigation,
and not harming certain government constitutional activities.⁵
Not all Cabinet documents are
so privileged; it depends on their content.⁶
It is very important to allow frank discussion among
Cabinet members, in and out of formal Privy Council or Legislative Council meetings, as later
they have to defend Cabinet decisions, even if they did not agree with them. Difﬁcult compromises
must be encouraged and can be made only if there is no undue external interference. Six main
factors for the court to weigh are listed, but the government has the onus of establishing this
privilege in each case.⁷
The common law creates Crown immunity, and it can be removed only with clear and
unequivocal language in a statute, as the (federal) Interpretation Act s.27 conﬁrms. A section in
legislation about suing the Crown makes the Crown subject to the “rules of practice and procedure
of the court”, which means that in such cases the Crown’s immunity is removed, but only when
the Crown is a party. The Crown’s investigator may refuse to attend an examination for discovery,
if neither he nor the Crown is a party.⁸
A party disputing a claim of public-interest immunity must ﬁrst show a threshold of relevance to
the issues at hand, either on a very broad issue, or relevant to a narrower issue. The second step
then is the strong public interest in maintaining the conﬁdentiality of deliberations among ministries
of the Crown. Ministers must be able to speak freely in Cabinet deliberations, without fear of
others later comparing their private views with government policy. And without fear of the dangers
of stating unpopular or politically incorrect positions. Cabinet discussions are not an occasion to
deliver considered judgements. The parties asserting the immunity have the onus of proof. The

immunity covers both documents and oral discussion. The six factors to test such immunity are
level of decision making, nature of policy involved, content of the particular document, timing of
disclosure, importance of the document in this lawsuit, and whether unconscionable government
behavior is alleged.¹
8. Medical Privilege?
In common-law Canada, medical records are conﬁdential but (except for some psychiatric
records) not privileged in court.²
For conﬁdential psychiatric records, there is now a partial
privilege which would allow disclosure of some records only, edited to remove non-essential parts.
The court should inspect the documents.³
9. Multiple Copies
It is possible for a copy or a group of copies of a record to be privileged where the originals are
not privileged, possible for some entries in a book to be privileged and others not privileged, and
possible for notes on a producible record to be privileged.⁴
Duplicate documents need not be listed and veriﬁed. Privileged and irrelevant records
get different treatment.⁵
If the record is important, a duplicate might be relevant if it disclosed
knowledge, notice, or intent. Notes on a record make it a fresh record, and if that is relevant and
material, the record is producible.
A spreadsheet on a computer which changes from time to time as new data are entered, could
have litigation privilege for one version or stage of it and not earlier ones. But a party cannot shield
an otherwise producible document from discovery simply by inserting some information about
the litigation into it, and then claiming privilege for the whole document.⁶
Where different parts
of the later stage of the document are created for different purposes, some privileged and some
not, the party may lose privilege because the dominant purpose is not litigation. The solution for
that danger is to maintain separate versions of the document for different purposes. Tainting with
litigation information does not sufﬁce.⁷
10. Inferences?
An adverse inference should not be drawn against a party for claiming any type of privilege, if
there has been no motion to compel an answer or otherwise test the claim of privilege.⁸
But there
may be an exception if incompetence of counsel is argued.⁹

Where there is a dispute as to whether correspondence and analysis of it are privileged or not,
and the judge has no evidence of why or how the papers were created, but has read them, and
cannot tell the purpose, the judge cannot ﬁnd privilege, because the onus is on the party asserting
privilege.¹
Conversely, the onus is on the party who asserts that privilege was waived.²
First-hand evidence about why a record was created is powerful, and someone disagreeing
needs to adduce good evidence to challenge that.³
11. Asserting and Testing Privilege
On descriptions of privilege for records in an afﬁdavit of records, see the Min. of Mun. Affairs
case,⁴
and Part A above.
A relevant enough record must be disclosed by the afﬁdavit of records, even if privilege is
claimed.⁵
Sorting documents and claiming privilege is for the two opposing counsel, and the courts
should not be expected to receive a big pile of all the records or all those in one category, and
then to sort them all into privileged and non-privileged because of some broad general claim of
privilege or objection.⁶
On creating procedures to test contested privilege issues, see the Goodis case⁷
and R.
5.11n. But a lay tribunal with an interest and a power to communicate information to others,
cannot inspect documents for which a claim of solicitor-client privilege is made, without explicit
words in the legislation.⁸
If a tribunal looks at records in order to rule on a claim of privilege, any
privilege is not thereby lost.⁹
If a tribunal does review records to assess possible privilege, the
standard of review by a court of the decision on privilege by a tribunal is correctness, not merely
reasonableness. The unredacted documents should be provided to the Court of King’s Bench to
be held sealed.¹⁰
But a statutory tribunal with power to decide legal and factual questions, especially exclusive
jurisdiction over them, can rule on disputed settlement privilege.¹¹
However, it cannot insist on
persuasive evidence of the privilege, lest that information destroy the privilege.¹²
See further R.
5.11n.
An afﬁdavit of records swearing that certain documents are privileged creates no presumption
that they are, but it is prima facie evidence of the privilege, and sufﬁces absent any contrary

A proper afﬁdavit swearing to privilege because of the purpose for creating

Asserting the privilege in the way required in an ordinary lawsuit in court, sufﬁces as a valid

No privilege attaches to a Notice of Constitutional Question submitted to the Crown, and the
Notice cannot be sent on trust conditions restricting its use. Its purpose is to give notice.⁴
C. Professional Privilege (Solicitor-Client)⁵
1. General
(a) Basic Nature
“Professional” privilege is that which attaches to communications relating to legal advice
between solicitor⁶
and client.⁷
This privilege is recognized by the law because of the importance of
allowing full and frank communication between a solicitor and his or her client.⁸
This privilege applies to all types of organization, governmental, private, business, or in
between.⁹
(b) Legal Advice is Sole Test
For the privilege to attach, there need only be communication between the solicitor and client
for the purpose of seeking or giving legal advice.¹⁰
It is not necessary that any litigation be in
existence or contemplated, nor that the correspondence be in furtherance of litigation.¹¹
Nor does
it matter why the client wants the legal advice, so there is privilege for advice from a Crown lawyer
to the Crown which may be used in developing policy.¹²
If the solicitors investigated and gave legal
advice in general, then solicitor-client privilege is created, even if it has to do with a dispute or
possible litigation.¹³

All solicitor-client communications within the privilege are protected, as a request from the
client for legal advice can usually be inferred; one does not test each individual communication
or part of one.¹
This privilege is broad, and applies to all interactions between client and solicitor
where the lawyer is engaged in giving legal advice.²
Advice is broader than telling a client the state
of the law, and includes what should be done in a given situation, and maybe what is within the
usual scope of the professional relation.³
Solicitor-client privilege attaches to more than just the
advice given by the lawyer to the client. It includes information the client gives to the lawyer and
his or her staff, the fees charged, and maybe even the existence of the lawyer-client relationship.
The lawyer needs to know all the facts.⁴
Privilege depends on the circumstances surrounding creation of a record or communication,
not just its subject matter.⁵
The privilege extends beyond the request and the advice given, to a continuum of all
communications directly related to the seeking, formulating, or giving of legal advice.⁶
If a person communicates directly with his or her solicitor and asks about that person’s own
legal interests, that is presumed to be conﬁdential and privileged.⁷
The test for solicitor-client privilege is whether the communication was made to seek lawful
legal advice from a professional legal adviser in that capacity.⁸
A client’s name is sometimes
privileged, and the mere fact of his having consulted a certain lawyer may be privileged.⁹
The tests given above are the only ones. For solicitor-client privilege, getting advice need not
be the dominant privilege.¹⁰
People must be able to conﬁde in their lawyers.¹¹
There can be a solicitor-client relationship
