356, affd 2004 SCC 31, [2004] 1 SCR 809; cf. Angus P’ship. v. Salvation Army, infra (advice to other

5-41
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
creating privilege without any express retainer of the lawyer nor account rendered, and a number
of factors are relevant to whether the relationship existed, including reasonable expectations, who
gave orders, and who paid.¹
Information necessary to get legal advice is privileged if communicated.²
Parts of a
communication to a soliicitor for legal advice are privileged even if they merely communicate
facts.³
The information sent to the solicitor need not be relevant, for the client does not know what
is or is not legally relevant, and the privilege for the communications is to be assessed as a whole,
not letter by letter or email by email, or fragment by fragment.⁴
If the issue is whether the document was created to get advice, one looks at the continuum of
communications, not separately at each place or part. The two people must be able to communicate
freely and candidly and need not speciﬁcally ask for advice,⁵
and within a client company there
can be internal communications in order to request or get advice or discuss advice received.⁶
Not all advice from a lawyer is privileged, e. g. non-legal purely business advice.⁷
But a lawyer’s
draft document usually involves legal expertise, and so communications about the draft with
the client are usually subject to solicitor-client privilege. However, communication of a draft to
counsel for another party is usually assertive, or a statement of a position, even if the tone is
collaborative.⁸
But the record or the evidence may show otherwise.⁹
Instructions to a lawyer, even
in a conveyancing matter, are privileged,¹⁰
Privilege extends to both telling what the law is, and to
advising what should be done in the relevant legal context.¹¹
[Footnote 11 continued from prior page]

106, JCC 1801 09968 (¶’s 19-20). It extends to a licensed foreign lawyer, even if he gives legal advice here:
NEP Can. v. MEC OP 2013 ABQB 540, 571 AR 38 (¶ 23).

5-42
R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
There is no exception where the instructions immediately become more or less public because
the lawyer sends a letter accordingly.¹
Instructions and discussions with a lawyer about drafting
one’s will are privileged.²
During the testator’s life, a will is privileged.³
Where a communication expressly says that the author wants the recipient solicitor’s legal
advice, the communication is privileged, even if some of the information relayed is relevant to
another person’s retainer of that solicitor, where it is part of the factual basis given by the author
for the advice which the author requests for herself.⁴
(c) Groups of People
Members of a client group requested and gave information relating topically to those in which
legal counsel were directly involved. They were part of a continuum in which legal advice was
given, and they essentially transmitted or commented on the work products which were privileged.
The communication need not speciﬁcally request or offer advice, as long as it can be placed
within that continuum of communication in which the solicitor tenders advice. The privilege is not
limited merely to the initial recipient of the legal advice and opinion; later spreading of the advice
or commenting on the advice are also protected.⁵
After all, most often legal advice is requested
and needed in order to consider it and act on it, so it may have to be disseminated within the client
organization.
Anyone seeking legal advice can beneﬁt from such a privilege, including the Crown or
government bodies or employees. It is strongly in the public interest for those wielding wide
powers to know and follow the law.⁶
Corporations and public bodies have privilege too.⁷
When an administrative tribunal consults
its solicitor, even as to a pending decision, that communication is privileged.⁸
It is possible to have two different topics about two different people, in one email to a solicitor,
so that most of the email is privileged, but one short paragraph is not.⁹
2. Importance and Protection
(a) Broad, Important and Protected
Professional privilege is important, and not to be whittled down.¹⁰
It is close to

absolute.¹
It is fundamentally necessary to the administration of justice, the highest privilege
recognized by the courts, and should be maintained to the extent possible.²
There is no common-
law jurisdiction for a statutory ofﬁcial to ignore or override legal privileges.³
A law ﬁrm’s creditors
cannot be allowed to breach the privilege of the ﬁrm’s clients.⁴
Apart from established exceptions,
it is to be recognized without balancing interests in individual cases.⁵
It is hard to set aside solicitor-client privilege.⁶
Only the client can waive it, and the onus of
proof is on anyone asserting waiver of privilege.⁷
Deliberately not claiming privilege for one brief
email in a chain of emails does not waive privilege or make claiming privilege for the rest unfair
or misleading, if the one revealed merely acknowledges receipt of notice that certain work is no
longer required about something.⁸
Solicitor-client privilege is also protected by the Charter,⁹
especially where a law ofﬁce is
searched by the police.¹⁰
Professional privilege is protected by ss.7, 8 of the Charter.¹¹
Sec.488.1
of the Criminal Code is unconstitutional and void, because it gives the police the documents
seized, if any of the number of strict procedural steps (such as time limits) are missed by the
lawyer’s client, who may not even know of the search.¹²

5-44
R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Legislation can interfere with such privilege no more than is absolutely necessary.¹
Privilege
is no longer a mere rule of evidence, but a substantive right.²
If the claim of privilege is contested
and there is examination of a party or witness to help test that, one cannot ask for particulars
detailed enough to defeat the privilege.³
The width of discovery is irrelevant to questions of privilege.⁴
Because of the Constitution,
the state cannot, without justifying it, put duties on lawyers which undermine their duties to their
clients’ causes. The duty to the client is a principle of fundamental justice. Legislation is bad if it
requires lawyers to collect more information than they think necessary to represent their clients.
Sec.1 of the Charter does not justify this.⁵
(b) A “Class” Privilege
Solicitor-client privilege is a “class privilege”, which does not have to be factually established in
each case; it attaches to the mere relationship itself between solicitor and client. The client need
not prove prejudice. Nor need he or she show an objectively-reasonable basis for asserting the
privilege. Nor can the person seeking the information argue unfairness, inconvenience, relevance,
prejudice, or ﬁduciary duty. The relative fairness to the two parties is not to be balanced.⁶
(c) Loss of Privilege
It is hard to set aside solicitor-client privilege.⁷
Deliberately not claiming privilege for a
communication does not give up privilege for other related communications.⁸
It takes very clear explicit language in a statute to abrogate privilege.⁹
In court, the standard
of review seems to be correctness, for the meaning of the word “privilege” in a statutory ofﬁcer’s
home statute, is that of the term of art in general law and has no different meaning in that statute.¹⁰
See also Part B.6 above.
Usually there can be no adverse inference against someone for claiming solicitor-client
privilege or refusing to waive it. But there can be such an inference against someone claiming
that the same solicitor was incompetent.¹¹

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
Where a communication expressly says that the author wants the recipient solicitor’s advice,
the communication is privileged, even if some of the information relayed is relevant to another
person’s retainer of that solicitor, where it is part of the factual basis given by the author for the
advice which the author requests for herself.¹
The privilege in question belongs to the client, and can only be waived by the client.²
A change
in ownership or control of a client company does not affect that, nor does a liquidation, after the
liquidator has been discharged and the directors resumed control.³
3. Supposed Exceptions
(a) Non-Exceptions
At one time many cases⁴
tried to draw a difﬁcult and often illogical distinction between privileged
communications and their content, suggesting that the latter are not privileged. That distinction
is wrong where a lawyer’s fees to his client and information about them are involved,⁵
and wrong
generally.⁶
If a fact can be proved without recourse to a lawyer or communications to or from the
lawyer, it may be admissible in evidence; but to require its disclosure by one who learned or can
learn or recall it, only through a privileged communication, would be to destroy the privilege.⁷
If a
pie is privileged, so is its ﬁlling.
A judge should not inquire into privileged solicitor-client communications, even to see why the
client has not shown up for court in a custody case.⁸
(b) Innocence in Criminal Prosecution
Any exception to privilege must be really necessary and stringently constrained, such as clear
need (no other way) to prove a reasonable doubt as to innocence in a criminal case.⁹
It takes clear
proof and necessity to invoke that exception.¹⁰
That innocence exception does not extend to other
criminal defences, such as abuse of process or entrapment.¹¹
The exceptions to solicitor-client privilege are only for innocence at stake in a criminal case, a
clear and imminent threat of serious bodily harm or death, or communications to further a criminal

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
purpose. No general exception for urgency or necessity exists, nor could be reconciled with this
privilege’s fundamental purposes.¹
Generally, if some other right conﬂicts with solicitor-client privilege, the privilege persists.²
(c) Crime or Fraud
There is an established exception to privilege for records or communications made in order
to facilitate the commission of a crime or fraud, whether or not the lawyer was acting in good
faith.³
But this exception to privilege for commission of crime, does not apply to a client asking
a lawyer whether or not a contemplated step would be legal, even if it ultimately turns out to be
illegal. Otherwise the most cautious law-abiding citizens would have little privilege and getting
any advance legal advice would be penalized. Canada is awash with technical semi-criminal
regulatory offences which no ordinary citizen would imagine could be illegal. Many areas of
modern law are bulky and complex, often counterintuitive. The exception to privilege applies only
if the legal advice facilitated the crime, or if the lawyer otherwise becomes a dupe or conspirator.⁴
There is an exception to privilege only if the client knew or should have known at the time he or
she sought the advice, that the act would be illegal.⁵
Privilege applies to requests for legal advice
(and the lawyer’s reply) made after the criminal or dishonest transaction is ﬁnished.⁶
A mere allegation of fraud or crime is not enough to remove privilege; there must be enough
evidence to give a prima facie case of it.⁷
A court should not of its own motion raise the exception
for crime, especially in the absence of proper evidence to support the exception.⁸
One exception to this privilege is proven blameworthy conduct, including abuse of process
by the party claiming the privilege.⁹
A contingency-fee agreement does not change the privilege
rules.¹⁰
(d) Miscellaneous
That the client is itself a law ﬁrm does not change the scope or existence of the solicitor-client
privilege.¹¹
A contingency fee agreement does not change the privilege rules.¹²

5-47
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
Common-interest privilege is discussed, including whether it is true privilege or an exception
to loss of privilege by waiver. It is not the same as common-interest privilege.¹
It requires solicitor-
client privilege, plus a sufﬁcient common interest respecting the part of shared communication,
and where interest in completing a transaction is not itself enough.²
Solicitor-client privilege survives death of the client, with some exceptions where wills are
involved.³
Surrogate R.85(2) reﬂects the exception by allowing disclosure and production from
someone who took instructions for the will as pre-trial disclosure. If the case however is pre-
application, conﬁdential information is not needed. Privilege bars earlier disclosure in a Surrogate
proceeding.⁴
If a client asks her solicitor for legal advice about the client’s own position, the communication
is privileged, and the privilege is not lost because the communication also covers another persons
position.⁵
That a solicitor tells the client to keep records, or says that the records must ﬁrst come to him,
is not enough automatically to make them subject to solicitor-client privilege; one needs evidence
that this is done to get or receive legal advice.⁶
4. Examples
A letter intended to be sent to someone neither the legal adviser nor his client, to give notice
to that person, has no legal privilege.⁷
Protecting the amount and contents of solicitors’ accounts by solicitor-client privilege is a
question of central importance to the legal system as a whole, and outside the specialized area
of expertise of a privacy commissioner or staff.⁸
The courts should not try to draw up a list of
what documents are and are not protected by professional privilege, but a lawyer’s or notary’s
accounting records may be privileged.⁹
The established principles of privilege can run into slightly novel fact situations when applied to
a criminal prosecution.¹⁰
But legal advice and draft afﬁdavits by Crown lawyers can be privileged.¹¹
Privilege is also a reason to exclude a paper from a formal return to judicial review.¹²

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
That the owner of the privilege is a trustee of other things does not destroy the privilege,
where the suit is not directly about those other things.¹
Trust documents are not privileged as
such, even if some of the trustees are solicitors.²
But a lawyer’s accounts to his or her client are
likely privileged.³
Where an insurer retains counsel to defend an action on behalf of the insured, the lawyer owes
a duty of good faith and loyalty to both the insurer and the insured, and both are his clients, even
if there are issues, such as coverage, on which he cannot act. Against the outside world, both
clients are entitled to privilege.⁴
See also Part D.3
The impact of a receivership on contemplating a lawsuit, is discussed, in the Indep. Electric
case.⁵
Patent-agent privilege should ﬁt within the deﬁnition of solicitor-client privilege, but one case
treats it as a statutory privilege under s.16.1 of the Patent Act.⁶
The identity of those presently instructing a shell company’s lawyers is privileged, but who did
that in the past is not?⁷
Having a non-lawyer investigate the facts about complaints against an employee, before
dismissing the employee, created neither solicitor-client nor litigation privilege, even when the
inevitable suit by the employee was ﬁled and served?⁸
D. Litigation Privilege
1. General Rules
(a) The Tests
The second common type of privilege is Litigation Privilege.⁹
It attaches to records or
communications which have been created for the dominant purpose of furthering litigation. In
order for this privilege to attach, it is necessary that there be litigation existing or contemplated.
Litigation privilege is important, and ensures efﬁcacy of the adversarial process for all litigants,
with or without a lawyer.¹⁰
Litigation privilege is also a class privilege. It is not decided case by case, balancing factors and
policy.¹¹
It can be enforced against non-parties or regulators.¹²
This type of privilege is fundamental

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
to the adversary system.¹
After all, to deny such privilege would be to discourage preparing fully
for trial, and to punish making notes of such organizing thoughts!
This type of privilege does not require that any solicitor-client communication be involved.
Litigation privilege is separate from legal advice (solicitor-client) privilege; one does not need
both: either one will bar production; and they have different rationales.²
The Supreme Court
has greatly heightened the distinction between solicitor-client privilege and litigation privilege.
Litigation privilege is neither directed at, nor restricted to, communications between solicitor and
client, and typically the communications are with some non-party. It includes non-conﬁdential
communications³
and even non-communicative material and is (of course) limited only to the
context of litigation. It is most important to note the different rationale. Litigation privilege exists to
facilitate investigation and preparation of a case for trial, so it protects a process, not a relationship.⁴
They are not even two branches of the same tree?⁵
All litigants have litigation privilege, even self-represented ones.⁶
The litigation to be reasonably contemplated includes prosecutions or regulatory enforcement.⁷
Whether a litigious dispute exists or is contemplated, is to be determined objectively?⁸
The
mere fact of investigation after an event does not establish that the dominant purpose was new
or contemplated litigation.⁹
There is a review of the authorities on the “dominant purpose” test for litigation privilege.¹⁰
This
does not require a sole purpose or exclusive purpose.¹¹
The privilege gets wide scope once the tests are met, and exceptions to it are narrow.¹²

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Relevance of the record does not remove the privilege, and there is no need to waive the
privilege if there is no intent to use the record at trial.¹
Litigation privilege, created when a lawyer is retained for contemplated litigation, covers all the
lawyer’s ﬁles on that matter.²
Not all a lawyer’s work leads to privilege, e. g. if no litigation or advice is involved, and so not
all a lawyer’s accounting records are necessarily privileged.³
So counsel’s involvement at some
point is not controlling.⁴
Clerical work by a solicitor or his or her employee to collect and organize
records, is not privileged?⁵
On a claim of litigation privilege in a civil suit, see the Hootan case.⁶
Each record created must be evaluated separately, so even if the dominant purpose of the
internal investigation as a whole was litigation, some documents created or collected during it may
not have that purpose. The court should examine each document or group of like documents.⁷
Therefore the tests or purpose needed for litigation privilege refer to the time that each individual
record is created and existing at that time. Not to intent or motive to start or carry on the
investigation which later led to creating the records.⁸
So the fact that the initial investigation had
several purposes is far from conclusive.⁹
Litigation privilege also extends to other litigation which is related and it does not have to be
very close, and even remote connection is enough.¹⁰
Labels like “privileged”, or their absence, tell little about whether there is in fact privilege.¹¹
Therefore, the potential uses of a document, or actual later uses, are not the test and do not
prevent a document’s being found to be privileged.¹²
And information gathered in contemplation of
litigation need not be completely quarantined, or left unused for other purposes, and such other
uses are not conclusive evidence of the reason for creating the record in question.¹³
(b) Onus
Ordinarily, a court ruling on privilege should look at the documents in question, to see whether
their elements were later used as implied, or whether those elements came from the non-privileged
investigation, not from the reports. That is because the issue is the purpose for creation at the time
of creation, done for each individual record, not for the background investigation.¹⁴

5-51
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
The onus of proving circumstances creating privilege is on those who claim privilege.¹
The
party claiming privilege must give evidence to support the privilege, not merely say there is legal
advice that the record in question is privileged.²
The party claiming privilege does not have to
prove a negative, such as creating a list of other possible motivations and then disproving each
one. If the other side wants to argue that way, it must prove it.³
That means that the party wanting
to see the document can explore that other possible motive in cross-examination.⁴
The onus of proving privilege stays on the party asserting privilege. Involvement of a lawyer,
or passing records through him, changes nothing.⁵
But a sworn bald assertion is not enough evidence.⁶
Conversely, it does not require a lot
of detailed evidence, and the evidence of privilege should not be denigrated by calling it “self-
serving”; often the party is the only witness with knowledge of the intent.⁷
(c) Examples
The contents of a witness statement, especially if taken on instructions of counsel, are prima
facie privileged.⁸
A prosecutor’s notes of witness interviews are privileged.⁹
And contents of
brieﬁng a witness for trial are privileged.¹⁰
An expert report got by solicitors for purposes of ongoing
litigation is privileged (subject to an exception in R.5.35).¹¹
Where a medical specialist gave a
report for treatment purposes to the referring physician, that specialist may not be able to act as
the litigation consultant or expert witness for the opposite side, but the other side can subpoena
him on the subject of his report, and what led to it.¹²
Whether certain documents were made for the dominant purpose of litigation is difﬁcult if
there was no procedure to label such documents, and even more difﬁcult where environmental
or regulatory concerns, business concerns, and seeing what repair would be needed, all had to
be addressed.¹³
It would be harmful to require that an expert witness go to the witness stand without explanations
or information about the legal process. To require that that be done formally in writing, would
be very slow. Though litigation privilege attaching to experts cannot be used to shield improper
conduct, it takes evidence to support a reasonable suspicion that counsel improperly inﬂuenced
the expert, not a mere demand or accusation. Instructions to this witness need not be disclosed.¹⁴

Surveillance of the opposing party, or other investigation of him by this party’s private

Who is presently giving instructions to a shell company’s lawyers is subject to litigation

of one witness by many parties with different intentions and interests, had no common purpose,

On a non-lawyer hired to investigate complaints against an employee, who is then dismissed
and sues, see the Prosser case.⁴
2. Applicable Time to Test Privilege
The time to test privilege (verify the dominant purpose⁵
for creation of a record)⁶
is when
the record in question was created as a tangible object. The test is the purpose for preparing or
creating the documents, not the purpose for getting them, and pre-existing documents gathered
or copied are not automatically privileged.⁷
Privilege depends on the circumstances for creating
the communication, not its subject matter.⁸
A host of fact situations occur, and many decisions decide what facts create privilege.⁹
There
is no ﬁxed rule for whether an insurance company always or never contemplates litigation when it
investigates an automobile accident. It is a question of fact in each case what was the dominant
purpose.¹⁰
Nor does legislation requiring a business to investigate each accident, exclude litigation
privilege if litigation was contemplated.¹¹
A company policy that a report must be created after

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
every accident, may found privilege, if that type of business is vulnerable to suits and gets a huge
number of claims each year.¹
The contemplation of litigation is broad. It may cover a possible suit against one’s insurer
(arising at the time of a ﬁre), and litigation against whoever tortiously caused the ﬁre, including
subrogated litigation. It is the same ﬁre and the same damage. So a settlement with the insurer
may well not end the privilege.²
Even if some duty to repair a problem is known to exist, if a
threat of litigation is known, consulting for that could be the dominant purpose; evidence that it
was, is plausible. A threat of litigation can arise various ways. It need not await ﬁnal results of
investigations and a formal report on certain facts.³
3. Multiple Access
Privilege bars letting an outsider read the record, even in order to see if it is privileged.⁴
But
two parties to a suit with similar interests may have common-interest privilege for discussions
between them.⁵
There is no privilege in a suit between two people where the two people (though
opposing) jointly own the information and the privilege.⁶
See also Part C.3(d).
A party can get production from its opponent of papers to which solicitor-client privilege
applies, if the applicant was a party to the papers or was the client, or if the applicant was made
to pay for the advice.⁷
4. End of Litigation Privilege
On waiver of privilege, see subpart F.
Making or using notes to refresh one’s memory during an examination for discovery does
not automatically make the notes privileged; a privileged dominant purpose must be shown. The
test is whether these notes were created for the litigation, not whether the background papers
were. Even if the notes were privileged, it was waived, as the notes were actually used to refresh
memory to answer the questions on discovery.⁸

5-54
R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Litigation privilege ends when the litigation does. There is no point to keeping the litigation
privilege once the litigation is over.¹
However, it is not over when essentially the same legal ﬁght
is still going on, such as related litigation. That includes separate proceedings with the same or
related parties arising from the same or related cause of action, or even proceedings raising
issues common to both lawsuits and with a common essential purpose.²
Records prepared for
criminal prosecution were held not privileged once it was over, even in a suit for civil redress for
the manner of conducting the prosecution!³
Death of one of the owners of litigation privilege is not
enough to end the privilege.⁴
Though two suits over the same ﬁre on the same insurance policy may not cause privilege for
the ﬁrst to continue to the second, that is a poor analogy to one lawsuit covering two things: issues
decided by a partial arbitration, and issues not included in that. The issues had not changed.⁵
In the early 2000s, it was not yet clear that litigation privilege was separate from “solicitor-client
privilege”, so a reference to the latter in an Act of that period can be interpreted as covering both
types of privilege, especially if the section in question was more procedural than substantive. That
the Legislature intended to abolish one type by omission is very unlikely; it takes clear language
to repeal them.⁶
The privilege is again the privilege of the client, and can be waived only by the client. This type
of privilege, like the privilege over solicitor-and-client communications, can be waived expressly
or by knowingly giving a copy of the privileged document to one’s opponents,⁷
or by attempting
to rely on the privileged document to obtain some relief from the Court. This is commonly done,
for example, when expert reports are used in Court. See further Part F below. On expert reports,
see R.5.37n.
Sometimes a medical examination under R.5.41 can end privilege for certain medical-legal
reports, because of R.5.44(3). But a statutory examination by a certiﬁed medical examiner does
not end such privilege?⁸
E. Without-Prejudice Communications
1. General Rule
(a) Tests and Scope
The third type of privilege is now also called “settlement privilege, ” or “negotiation privilege”.
It attaches to communications to further a possible settlement⁹
of an existing or contemplated

litigations dispute.¹
The law encourages settlement,²
and accordingly permits the parties to
exchange communications with a view to resolving their disputes. Settlement privilege is important
and fosters public policy, and assists freer, quicker negotiations.³
Such communications are privileged and must not be disclosed to the court, and such
disclosures may be struck out and ignored.⁴
The reasons for recognizing settlement privilege
apply just as much to disputes which have a public interest component as to private disputes.⁵
This correspondence may not be used in evidence if the settlement efforts fail.
The tests for this type of privilege are a litigious dispute existing or contemplated, a
communication expressly or impliedly intended not to be disclosed to the court if negotiations fail,
and attempt to settle as the purpose of the communication in question.⁶
It covers a judicial dispute
resolution session, or an “E. I. C. C. ” in Family Docket Court.⁷
Some hint of possible compromise or
negotiation is necessary for this privilege, and a mere assertion of a right is not enough without
that.⁸
A key question is whether the purpose of the group of communications was to try to effect
a settlement.⁹
Without-prejudice privilege is an established “class” privilege.¹⁰
Establishing expiry of limitation
periods is not an exception to this privilege.¹¹

R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The court may properly infer that the group of communications of which the letter or email or
statement was part, was intended to facilitate negotiation.¹
Given the public policy, this privilege applies just as much during an interlocutory motion in
chambers as it does to substantive issues at trial.²
Negotiations’ contents are privileged even in
interlocutory proceedings.³
Without-prejudice correspondence about an offer and its rejection,
were struck from an afﬁdavit.⁴
This settlement privilege extends to negotiations with a prosecutor
to bargain down a criminal charge by plea.⁵
Without-prejudice privilege for negotiations in a
criminal case has exceptions, including misconduct by the prosecutor, or proving that a ﬁrm deal
was made.⁶
This privilege applies even if no admission of a fact is involved.⁷
Maybe the mere fact of some
negotiation is not privileged, but everything else is, if the privilege is established.⁸
The proposed agreement can be a mere delay or forbearance, not a full settlement.⁹
The intent of the author of a “without prejudice” letter is not the only factor.¹⁰
Where there is
a dispute and a threat, in the circumstances the court may conclude that legal steps, including
litigation, were contemplated. The court is slow to assume correspondence was a bluff or not
serious. And if the overall purpose of the correspondence was to settle the dispute, and if more
information was needed to do so, then likely all is privileged.¹¹
The court assumes that settlement discussions and mediation are both conﬁdential and
privileged.¹²
The heading on the record in question is not conclusive.¹³
In addition, if the parties
have clearly stated in their settlement agreement that it will remain privileged and conﬁdential
and not be put into evidence, that is conclusive as least so far as the intentions of the parties are
concerned.¹⁴
If none of the exceptions to that presumption apply, then the document is privileged.¹⁵
Settlement privilege and other considerations bar examination for discovery (questioning)
questions about the witness’s settlement agreement with a non-party.¹⁶

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
(b) Exceptions or Waiver
Showing a party’s intent, or waiver by only one party, is no exception.¹
Even the author cannot
disclose to the court details of a without-prejudice settlement proposal. This privilege belongs to
both parties, and one of them alone cannot waive it.²
Settlement privilege should not be overridden by a desire to make sure that the plaintiff has
not been overcompensated, unless there was some indemnity-type relationship between the
defendant and the persons with whom the plaintiff had settled.³
Where freedom of information legislation exempts from disclosure “privilege”, the Commissioner
has no authority to override settlement privilege, even by considering broader aspects of public
policy or a desire for openness. There is no common-law jurisdiction to ignore or override legal
privileges.⁴
The Supreme Court says that exceptions to settlement privilege require showing that on
balance a competing public interest outweighs the public interest in encouraging settlements.
Purely theoretical arguments do not sufﬁce.⁵
Exceptions to settlement privilege are construed
narrowly.⁶
Though fraud or the like may be an exception, it must be proved, not merely asserted.⁷
It is necessary to know the timing of the papers to see what was contemplated at that time,
and some limited discovery as to such timing may be granted, if such information is not available
yet.⁸
Where both parties ﬁled and relied on statements of what the agreed settlement terms were,
that validly waived the privilege as R.4.20 conﬁrms.⁹
Without-prejudice privilege was overridden because of ambiguous evidence as to wrongdoing.¹⁰
Labor grievances and their various steps do not attract settlement privilege?¹¹
2. Automatic End of Privilege?
If an agreement or settlement is reached, the correspondence can be used to prove the
settlement contract.¹²
The words “without prejudice” are presumed to cover only the period before

But the end of the dispute does not end the privilege; it only ends to prove
existence or interpretation of the settlement if that is disputed.²
The privilege continues against
any parties to the suit or dispute who are not parties to the settlement agreement.³
If it is alleged that a mediation ended in an interim agreement, then the consensual outcome is
not privileged. This is not a request for content of negotiations or discussions during the mediation.⁴
Pierringer agreements were made (to amend the statement of claim to claim against certain
defendants who did not settle, only their share; and to settle with other defendants). Settlement
privilege protected the amounts paid by those settling, even though the negotiations succeeded.
Withholding those amounts did little harm to the non-settling defendants, who received all other
contents of the agreements.⁵
3. Miscellaneous
There was initially a surprising suggestion that settlement privilege does not apply where the
“drop-dead Rule” (R.4.33) is involved.⁶
Under that Rule, only the fact of negotiations would be
admissible, not their contents.⁷
There is a special without-prejudice privilege enacted in s.10(5) of the Divorce Act respecting
proceedings to achieve a reconciliation, not conﬁned to proceedings before a person appointed
by the court. The four branches of the Wigmore test for creating new privileges are satisﬁed, to
exclude marital counselling records.⁸
There is no privilege against questions just because the subject was communicated by the
defendant’s lawyer to the plaintiff’s lawyer. The mere possibility that the defendant could have got
legal advice before deciding, does not bar a question as to why a decision was made, or by whom.
Especially if bad faith by the defendant is pleaded.⁹
A payment under the Insurance Act is without prejudice and privileged by statute.¹⁰
Merely
suppressing the offer and extracting other parts of the letter to quote is not permissible.¹¹

A letter headed “without prejudice” and offering a settlement of the claim was admissible and
not privileged, because it was “not a bona fide attempt to effect a settlement”. Though the letter
gave the addressee either of two options, it did not discuss how to settle, and was not intended to
begin to discuss, a settlement. It was a take-it-or-leave-it proposition. Later one adjustment was
made to the letter, but the judge thought it not big enough to change that.¹
Can public interest outweigh this privilege, as its only purpose is to avoid arguments that one
side’s position is weak?²
F. Waiver and Loss of Privilege³
1. Who Can Waive
The client owns the privilege,⁴
not the lawyer. Privilege can be lost only by waiver by the owner
(client),⁵
not by mere failure to claim it before a court.⁶
An allegation said to waive privilege must
be by the owner of the privilege, not by the person attacking privilege.⁷
If there is more than one owner or client for the privilege, it takes consent by all to waive the
privilege.⁸
If the client owning the privilege is mentally incompetent and his trustee needs the
information to bring a suit over the client’s property, the solicitors cannot assert privilege to block
production.⁹
Only the owner of a privilege can give it up, not a regulatory tribunal.¹⁰
A successor in title, such as a trustee in bankruptcy, does not necessarily have the right to
waive the predecessor’s privilege. Privilege is a personal right, and so not all privilege is “property”
which vests in the trustee in bankruptcy, though in some situations the trustee may be able to
waive.¹¹
A trustee of a mentally incompetent testator cannot waive privilege for instructions to a
solicitor by the testator to make a will, during the testator’s life.¹²

But a custodian of a solicitor can tax his account, and the client cannot bar that on grounds
of privilege.¹
The opposing party, or the court, cannot erode someone else’s privilege by disclosing or
making public privileged documents without the consent of the owner of the privilege.²
2. Waiver is Difﬁcult
(a) General
See also subpart 2(c) below.
It takes clear unusual circumstances to produce waiver of privilege.³
Waiver of privilege should
be narrowly construed.⁴
Exceptions to without-prejudice privilege must be narrow.⁵
A waiver of
solicitor-client privilege must be clear and express.⁶
So it is very unlikely that answering questions
in the opposing lawyer’s cross-examination waives privilege.⁷
Express waiver of privilege and “implied” waiver for fairness, are discussed. The latter does not
turn on subjective intent of the disclosing party, but on unfairness. Since privilege is so important,
merely disclosing receipt of legal advice, or communication with a lawyer to get it, does not waive
privilege. For waiver, the seeking or receiving legal advice must be material to the issues in the
suit, and the privilege holder must have attempted to rely on the legal advice to justify a particular
course of action, making keeping the privilege unfair and the partial revelation misleading. A
ﬁshing expedition is not to be permitted.⁸
Some waiver of privilege is not intentional, and is created
by the law, not the intent of the person waiving). Waiver of privilege can occur if the owner uses the
record in a manner inconsistent with the privilege, or otherwise treats it as no longer conﬁdential,
e. g. in argument or pleadings. Or waiver could come from voluntary disclosure to an unrelated
third party. There is no ﬁxed test for such inconsistent disclosure. Criteria for waiver include
absence of any legitimate interest still to be protected, unfairness or prejudice to the adversary,
and undermining the administration of justice (the second and third overlapping).⁹

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.8(5)
The onus of proof is on him or her who asserts waiver.¹
Waiver of solicitor-client privilege without actual voluntary disclosure of the conﬁdential
information is based on fairness and consistency. For example, a party may deny that his counsel
settled a claim, and say that if it did, the client had not authorized that.²
(b) What is Not Waiver
Still less is privilege lost by inadvertent production, forcible search, or surreptitious unauthorized
interception of the material.³
It takes knowledge and clear intent by its owner, to waive privilege.⁴
Waiver of a right requires an unequivocal and conscious intent to abandon it, though if there is
no direct evidence of intention, then that party’s behavior, viewed objectively, may be considered.
Courts presume that a person intends the natural consequences of his or her acts.⁵
Attempts to recover papers inadvertently disclosed are not waiver of privilege.⁶
Inadvertent
disclosure does not permit the court to balance the prejudice or unfairness to the two parties (the
owner of the privilege and the one disputing the privilege).⁷
Inadvertent production because a
paper was put into a ﬁle of papers properly disclosed does not remove privilege, if the owner of
the privilege had no hand in putting the paper into that ﬁle.⁸
What must be done after a privileged
paper is inadvertently disclosed, and how soon, are discussed.⁹
Inadvertent disclosure of papers
subject to public interest immunity and the criteria for their return, are discussed.¹⁰
There is an extended review of the duties of a lawyer who receives from another party records
which might be privileged, including what he cannot do, and who he must notify.¹¹
On making use
of them, despite court orders forbidding that, see the Bennett Jones case.¹²

5-62
R.5.8(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
There is no loss of privilege because the topic of two proposed lawsuits is similar.¹
Nor is
privilege lost because one of the lawyers involved had a conﬂict, nor because the owner of the
privilege did not object to the conﬂict.²
Merely getting legal advice does not waive privilege unless there is active reliance on the
advice to support one’s claim, or putting one’s mental state at issue.³
Mere denial of a fact does
not waive any privilege and even silence in a pleading is a denial. Relevance of the fact does not
end privilege about it.⁴
(c) Waiver by Pleading or Arguing
Implied waiver required both implied intention, and also fairness and consistency. One cannot
disclose what one chooses and withhold the rest.⁵
There is a long discussion of loss of litigation privilege by use and reliance by an expert about
to testify.⁶
See also subpart 4 below.
If the law required giving to a regulator privileged documents, probably doing so would not
remove privilege, unless legislation said that it did remove privilege. Cooperation with regulators
is to be encouraged, and giving privileged documents to a regulator which then promised to keep
them conﬁdential, might well not waive privilege. But the promise would have to be a clear and
complete undertaking not to use the documents or the information in them. That law seems to
apply both to statutory public (governmental) regulators of the owner of the privilege, and also
professional regulators of the owner’s employees and ofﬁcers.⁷
(d) Limited Waiver
Where the alleged waiver of privilege is just about instructions to the solicitor, not about legal
advice, it is easier to ﬁnd waiver, but the waiver is conﬁned to the precise information given, and
does not extend to the broad topic.⁸
And not all revelations to third persons waive privilege, especially if it is litigation privilege and
not privilege for communications with a legal adviser.⁹
For example, a limited waiver of privilege for

certain advice for certain limited purposes does not waive privilege for the rest of the contents of

And common interest with the person to whom a communication is disclosed may negate

If associated parties had to confer to gather facts to give to lawyers to get legal

by the legal advice provided, that is privileged too. But not when the discussion goes beyond that
into operationalizing the legal advice.³
However, usually disclosure to a person or entity adverse
in interest is waiver.⁴
That includes disclosing to a regulator proposing enforcement or prosecution
or discipline.⁵
3. Examples
When a privileged opinion or report contains facts or instructions, the following things may
often waive privilege for the document and the facts in it; for example, ﬁling an afﬁdavit or giving
evidence relying on the contents of that opinion or report;⁶
pleading reliance on the advice;⁷
pleading holding an independent, fair investigation which upheld the complaints in question;⁸
or reliance on the instructions;⁹
expressly pleading knowledge of the law;¹⁰
suing for shock and
nervousness when such things are the subject of a psychiatric report;¹¹
pleading non est factum

Merely serving an expert witness’ report on the other side does not waive privilege over
discussions between the party employing the expert, and the expert himself, where the expert’s
conclusion is not founded on some information coming only from the client. Serving the report
waives privilege only over the report itself and all “foundational information”. Communications
between the client and the expert usually entail both solicitor-privilege and litigation privileges:
The opposing party may also want to know what information the expert got but did not rely upon,
but until the expert is testifying at trial, that cannot be compelled.¹²

waive privilege for those statements?¹³

that ignorance stopped the limitation period from running, waives privilege.¹
Likely arguing on

When a lawyer is sued by the client, that will often engage a waiver of privilege by the client
sufﬁcient to allow the lawyer to defend the claim. However, when the lawyer sues the client, the
lawyer cannot necessarily rely on privileged or conﬁdential information obtained while representing
the client.³
Litigation privilege is not lost by showing the communication to board members of a party
owning the privilege.⁴
Some cases say that waiving privilege for a report waives privilege for its drafts and working
papers.⁵
Voluntary production by one side to the other side in a personal injury suit of an expert
report does not waive litigation privilege for the ﬁle or background papers of the expert. The court
should not do anything to deter parties from giving early disclosure of expert reports to the other
side.⁶
A deliberately false tip to a police conﬁdential tipline, by the wrongdoer to throw the police off
the scent, has no informer privilege if those facts are proved.⁷
Criminal informants have started a practice of revealing their informer relationship so that the
Crown cannot prosecute them for new crimes they have committed. So the court gave an order
for further and modiﬁed conﬁdentiality.⁸
Starting a new lawsuit (pleading the evidence unnecessarily) based on inadvertently-produced
obviously privileged notes, was plainly improper.⁹
That a law ﬁrm may have breached its duties of conﬁdence to a past client by revelation to
another newer client, is no reason to refuse to protect the original client as privilege from a still
further and newer client or opponent.¹⁰
Many parties with different interests and intents met a witness together and interviewed him;
that neither created nor waived privilege.¹¹
4. Timing and Procedure
Evidence can be ﬁled in court only by waiving privilege for it, but there is not always an
obligation to waive privilege for it before trial, e. g. where it is to expose the opponent’s perjury,
without a chance to tailor her evidence.¹²
The Rules do not bar admission at trial of a privileged
document by the holder of the privilege, especially to impeach a witness. The court can prevent

Who Makes Afﬁdavit of Records
5.9(1) Subject to subrule (2), an afﬁdavit of records must be sworn by
(a) the party,
(b) if the party is a corporation, by the corporation’s corporate
representative, or
(c) if a litigation representative is appointed for a party, by the party’s
litigation representative.
(2) A suitable person, other than the lawyer of record of the party, may swear the
afﬁdavit of records if
(a) it is inconvenient for the party, the corporate representative or the
litigation representative to do so, and
(b) the parties agree or the Court so orders.

Related Provisions
2.24 (lawyer of record).

See also R.5.4 end notes to it.
There can be case management for discovery of records.³
Even if a partnership has since been dissolved, and certain of its alleged partners sued have
defended, and even if they deny that they were ever partners, that does not excuse them from
discovery and production of records.⁴
If one party fully discloses its records and is examined on that, and its ofﬁcers and employees
admittedly have no other records and swear to that, and can be examined, there is no point
ordering those ofﬁcers or employees to ﬁle a further afﬁdavit.⁵
And it is very doubtful that they are
in possession and control of their employer’s records anyway.
Subsequent Disclosure of Records
5.10 If, after a party has served an afﬁdavit of records on other parties, the ﬁrst
party ﬁnds, creates or obtains control of a relevant and material record not
previously disclosed, the ﬁrst party must
(a) immediately give notice of it to each of the other parties,
(b) on written request and on payment of reasonable copying expenses,
supply each of the other parties with a copy of it, and

Related Provisions
3.68(4) (striking pleading for failure to comply) ; 5.14 (copying of records) ; 5.16 (non-use

of undisclosed records); 5.27 (continuing duty to disclose) ; 5.38 (continuing obligation of

Rule 5.10 imposes a continuing duty to give disclosure of records, to prevent trial by ambush.²
Once a party has ﬁled an afﬁdavit of records, he or she must give prompt notice of any further
relevant records which are created, or discovered, or which come into his or her possession.³
In a lawsuit of any size, further records will inevitably be created and found, so supplementary
afﬁdavits of records should be ﬁled and served from time to time. That surely need not be done
daily. But if an important record comes to hand, notice of it must be given promptly.
Ordinarily extra copies or duplicates of a record disclosed need not be disclosed.⁴
Privilege is no excuse for not putting a new record in an afﬁdavit of records; it should be in
Schedule 2.⁵
Order For Record to be Produced
5.11(1) On application, the Court may order a record to be produced if the Court
is satisﬁed that
(a) a relevant and material record under the control of a party has been
omitted from an afﬁdavit of records, or
(b) a claim of privilege has been incorrectly or improperly made in
respect of a record.
(2) For the purpose of making a decision on the application, the Court may
(a) inspect a record, and
(b) permit cross-examination on the original and on any subsequent
afﬁdavit of records.⁶

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.11(2)
Related Provisions
3.13 (cross-examination on affidavits) ; 3.68(4) (striking pleading for failure to comply) ;

A. Introduction
Rule 5.11 gives a remedy where one thinks that there is something inadequate in, or wrong
with, an opponent’s afﬁdavit of records. Rules 3.13 and 5.11(2) (b) also allow one to cross-examine
on an afﬁdavit of records.¹
On later bars to using undisclosed records, see R.5.16.
On how to describe papers for which privilege is claimed, see Rr.5.7n. and 5.8n. On redacting
(masking) some passages in a record, see R.5.6 n. D, above.
B. Showing a Defect
Sometimes an afﬁdavit of records will be insufﬁcient on its face; for example, it may not follow
the prescribed wording, or it may contain only a very vague reference to papers, with no numbers
or bundles or other identiﬁcation.²
But if the afﬁdavit is regular on its face, then one needs
evidence to show that it is incomplete, such as an opposing afﬁdavit showing that the deponent
of the afﬁdavit of records is known to have received certain relevant papers which are not in his
production, or showing that certain papers for which he has claimed privilege were created at a
time or for a purpose which suggests that they are not privileged.³
These are some reasons that an afﬁdavit of records has been held insufﬁcient: that the
deponent had not even looked at the records of her earlier litigation;⁴
parts edited out of relevant
papers were also held relevant;⁵
omission of draft and ﬁnal ﬁnancial records, in a suit to share in
proﬁts;⁶
no one person knew what or where the records were, and the deponent of the afﬁdavit
knew little,⁷
an issue pleaded expressly by the opponent was considered invalid and irrelevant.⁸
But if privilege is claimed, the questions cannot demand particulars detailed enough to defeat
the privilege.⁹
English cases and old Canadian ones are unreliable on this topic, because at one time one
could not controvert an afﬁdavit of records, or could only do so on much narrower grounds. Where
insufﬁcient production is alleged, the court presumes that the party producing the record acted

e. g. a communication with a legal advisor of the author, seeking or receiving legal advice: Blough v. Busy

R.5.11(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
in good faith, but that is rebuttable, e. g. by evidence that relevant and material records have
not been produced. Then a further and better afﬁdavit may be ordered. Speculation, intuition or
guesswork is not enough. The court allows for the fact that one side has access to the records
and the other does not.¹
Someone seeking additional production/discovery must give a legal or
factual foundation for saying that additional relevant and material records exist. Many pieces of
indirect evidence here, all pointing to that conclusion, sufﬁced to show deliberate lying, resulting
in several remedies.²
A party may move for further production of documents, without ﬁrst cross-examining on the
opponent’s afﬁdavit of records; there are other ways which will show the afﬁdavit to be inaccurate
or incomplete. If an afﬁdavit says its statements are based on industry experience, and that records
have been reviewed, that is evidence that such records are relevant enough to be producible.³
The mere fact that a few relevant records were not mentioned in the afﬁdavit is not enough to
order a new afﬁdavit, especially where the deponent (a liquidator) is less familiar with the papers
than his opponent.⁴
A ﬁrsthand afﬁdavit swearing to the purpose for which a record was created (or an inquiry was
begun) is powerful evidence, and anyone challenging the privilege claim has to lead evidence to
disprove it.⁵
C. Excuses and Remedies
It is not an answer to an order for further and better answers on questioning (or disclosure of
documents), that doing so would cost money and put the party to some trouble.⁶
It is not enough to say that the deponent has the onus of proof in the suit and omissions will
hurt her.⁷
If the questioning party gets a denial on examination for discovery (questioning) that something
occurred or exists, he can follow up by seeking the records on that topic, and the method to get
and test objections to production of them, is the usual one of discovery of records, apparently
including R.5.11.⁸
A request for examining for discovery (questioning), or more details, cannot be
used to postpone producing accounting records seemingly very relevant to the suit as pleaded.⁹
Someone seeking an accounting should ordinarily get production of his opponent’s books
of account from the outset of the lawsuit, and not have to wait for a judgment for accounting?
Especially if the books would also be relevant to damages.¹⁰
Many of the records in a suit were in Turkish and it would cost millions to translate them. The
contract called for litigation in Alberta. The plaintiff had to furnish any English translations which

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.11(2)
it already had, and also to produce the Turkish originals. The court would decide whether the
translations should be used instead of the originals.¹
The case cannot be set down for trial until an order for a better afﬁdavit is complied with.²
A
defence was struck out for several years’ failure to produce relevant records and undertakings,
and it was too late then to question their relevance.³
Remedies for non-disclosure of records are usually orders by the case management judge,
not striking out pleadings.⁴
When a party was allowed to produce a redacted record, the original document had to be kept
in its original full form and be available to the trial judge, if the redacted one was put into evidence.
A dispute over the redaction should be decided the same way that a dispute over privilege would
be.⁵
On spoliation (destruction of evidence), see R.5.16n.
A party to litigation may have more than one way to get certain records. In addition to discovery
of records or questioning (examination for discovery), they may also have a statutory (substantive)
right to receive copies of certain documents such as company minutes or ﬁnancial statements.
And if oppression is shown, giving certain information might be one of the types of relief given.
Some contracts give the right to demand or inspect certain records. If there is a substantive
right to the document, there is no need to show that it is relevant and material.⁶
Where a company’s records have been disclosed and examined on and its employees and
ofﬁcers have no other records and swear to that in their afﬁdavits of records, and they can be
examined on that, there is no point to ordering a second afﬁdavit of records from the ofﬁcers or
employees.⁷
If a document is inadequately disclosed in the afﬁdavit of records, that does not require the
other party to challenge the adequacy of disclosure then, i. e. seek further particulars.⁸
On selecting records purely by a computer search for a certain name, see the PTW case.⁹
D. Inspection by Court
Rule 5.11(2) (a) lets a justice or an applications judge inspect a paper where there is a dispute
about whether it is privileged.¹⁰
Or the court may order inspection by a court referee.¹¹

5-74
R.5.11(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The Court will not review documents to see if they are really privileged, unless some basis
is shown for the challenge to the claim of privilege.¹
Rarely should the court inspect records to
see if they are privileged; usually that should be decided on afﬁdavit evidence.²
That can be the
description in an original or a supplementary afﬁdavit of records, or an afﬁdavit sworn for the
dispute over privilege. That makes sense, as the key fact for privilege is usually the object or intent
for making the record, which often is not mentioned or even revealed by the record. Since privilege
comes from the purpose for creation, not from the contents of the paper or record, inspection
gives only indirect evidence.³
But if the facts needed to decide a privilege dispute are largely
lacking (not provided) then the court must review the records in question, to determine the main
“focus”, the purpose for preparing or creating the record (not that for obtaining it).⁴
An afﬁdavit making what is on its face a proper claim of solicitor-client privilege, makes a
rebuttable presumption that the communications are privileged.⁵
An examination by the court was ordered, to attempt to resolve an apparent discrepancy
between an original afﬁdavit of records and a supplementary one.⁶
If an inspection is to be held, the judge must personally review the documents; it is an error
to let opposing counsel do it.⁷
If informer privilege is involved, the judge may hold an in camera
hearing to decide on privilege, and not let the defence counsel attend, if the documents contain
information which could help identify the informer.⁸
(The courts have extended that inspection
by analogy to disputes over relevance as well. ) The inspection does not stand alone, and any
afﬁdavit or other sworn evidence of facts said to create the privilege must also be weighed.
Where a party moves for production of records and takes no position about redaction, but
gives the judge both original records and a redacted version of them, the judge can look at them.⁹
The trial judge should not inspect a record said to be privileged; a different justice (or an
applications judge should.¹⁰

5-75
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.12(1)
Penalty for Not Serving Afﬁdavit of Records
5.12(1) In addition to any other order or sanction that may be imposed, the Court
may impose a penalty of 2 times the amount set out in item 3(1) of the tariff in
Division 2 of Schedule C [Tariff of Recoverable Fees], or any larger or smaller
amount the Court may determine, on a party who, without sufﬁcient cause,
(a) does not serve an afﬁdavit of records in accordance with rule 5.5
[When an afﬁdavit of records must be served] or within any modiﬁed
period agreed on by the parties or set by the Court,
(b) does not comply with rule 5.10 [Subsequent disclosure of records],
or
(c) does not comply with an order under rule 5.11 [Order for a record to
be produced].
(2) If there is more than one party adverse in interest to the party ordered to pay
the penalty, the penalty must be paid to the parties in the proportions determined
by the Court.
(3) A penalty imposed under this rule applies irrespective of the ﬁnal outcome
of the action.¹
Information Note
One of the additional sanctions that may be imposed is the striking out of pleadings. See rule
3.68(3) [Court options to deal with significant deficiencies].

Related Provisions
1.5(4) (non-compliance with Rules) ; 3.68(3) (b) (striking pleadings for failure to provide

affidavit); 5.1(1) (e) (purpose of Part 5) ; 10.33(1) (f) (costs for non-compliance) ; 10.49 (penalty
for non-compliance with rules); 10.52 (contempt).
A party who ﬁnds these deadlines too conﬁning should apply beforehand for a time extension,
not sit back and wait until they have expired.²
Unlike the earlier Rule, penalties for failure to ﬁle an afﬁdavit of records are no longer
mandatory, probably rendering obsolete the criticisms of it by some Masters, and rendering moot
statements by justices that the previous Rule was compulsory. But the tests or excuses in the old
and new Rules do not differ much.³
Under the current Rule, a proper explanation for the default is
needed, to avoid the sanction.⁴
A party may be penalized for failing to produce records where the argument that they may
not be relevant is so weak that it is unreasonable to argue it.⁵
A party which was required to give
discovery of records had not done a proper job, and so the opponent was given access to the hard
drives of the computers of the defaulting company.⁶

R.5.13(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Under the previous Rules, “sufﬁcient cause” included whether a case was complex, or whether
the volume or location of records so required. Serving in time by fax is sufﬁcient excuse, with the
sender’s machine saying sending was complete, even though the addressee denied receipt; he
did not complain of getting no afﬁdavit.¹
There are many accidental ways that it could have gone
astray at either end; diligence sufﬁces.
A late afﬁdavit of records meant that the opposing party did not know enough facts to decide
whether or not to accept the tardy deponent’s offer, and so the offer did not yield double costs.²
Even one week’s tardiness incurs the double costs penalty in R.5.12 (old R.190). Though the
new Rule says “may impose a penalty”, the new Rules aim for speed and efﬁciency and expressly
discourage delay in disclosure (R.5.1(1) (e) ). Now due diligence and some unavoidable accident
is the only valid ground for excusing double costs.³
The sufﬁcient cause to bar the costs penalty refers to sufﬁcient diligence or to the legislation or
court rulings. It requires maximum effort thwarted by extraordinary circumstances which the party
could not control, a hard test to meet. Forgetfulness is not enough. A ﬂood, accident, miscarriage
of a courier, or a medical emergency, would sufﬁce. Double costs were imposed.⁴
Early July
Canadian and American public holidays were not enough excuse.⁵
Because of repeated long-standing and admitted failure to give disclosure of ﬁnancial
information such as property values, in a matrimonial property suit, and because of the lack
of any excuse, that spouse’s statement of defence and counterclaim was struck out, and the
other spouse could move for summary judgment and schedule an assessment of damages. Any
adverse inference would be up to the judge then presiding.⁶
A remedy or penalty is awkward where the defaulting person ﬁled no pleading, but was ordered
to ﬁle an afﬁdavit as a person on whose behalf the action was prosecuted.⁷
The fraud exception to res judicata does not apply to failure by one party to produce a
publicly-available document which has little bearing in law on the issues. Indeed, simply because
a party does not produce a document which ultimately turns out to be producible is not enough
in itself to be fraud.⁸
Obtaining Records From Others
5.13(1) On application, and after notice of the application is served on the person
affected by it, the Court may order a person who is not a party to produce a
record at a speciﬁed date, time and place if
(a) the record is under the control of that person,
(b) there is reason to believe that the record is relevant and material,
and

(2) The person requesting the record must pay the person producing the record

Related Provisions
5.5 (who must discover records) ; 6.26 (inspecting property) ; 10.25 (obtaining records in

lawyer’s possession).
A. Introduction
This longstanding Rule is a curious hybrid or compromise. It allows a party to secure existing
papers²
or records held by a non-party. This Rule and R.6.26 on inspection afford the only means
of disclosure by strangers to a lawsuit. Other Rules are conﬁned to parties or their employees,
assignors, et al. Therefore, used properly, R.5.13 can be very valuable.³
B. Limited Use of Rule
However, the courts have interpreted R.5.13 as having a narrower purpose: it is not to be
used to ﬁsh, and can be used only if the relevance of the papers is shown.⁴
That is especially
important if privacy interests of non-parties would be involved.⁵
Despite where R.5.13 is located
in the Rules, it cannot be used to discover what records the non-party may hold; it is merely to be
used to see speciﬁc records and copy them (and certify the copies).⁶
In theory, the Rule exists to
avoid making the non-party come to trial merely to produce a few papers.⁷
Therefore, a party wishing to use this Rule must prepare the ground carefully beforehand,
and give the court evidence that the non-party actually possesses or controls certain records.⁸

R.5.13(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
logical assumption is not enough.¹
And give evidence that the documents are probably relevant.
Indeed, they must have enough relevance to justify disturbing the non-party to produce them.²
So relevance only to a collateral issue is not enough, especially when there is evidence it is not
connected to the issues on the pleadings.³
The tests are not relaxed just because the non-party
is closely related to an (adverse) party.⁴
There is a third requirement also: the applicant must show that it is impossible to get the
records by making an order against one of the existing parties to the suit.⁵
But it is not necessary to show exceptional circumstances to use this Rule.⁶
If a party comes into possession of a relevant paper, his opponent need not make a R.5.13
application; the ordinary Rules on discovery and production of records make it automatically
producible.⁷
Where legislation gives someone a right to see certain types of records, whether they are
relevant or material is irrelevant; it is not discovery of records, and the party declining to produce
is entitled neither to costs nor security for costs.⁸
C. Crown Immunity
Rule 5.13 does not bind the Crown (if it is not a party to the lawsuit).⁹
The R. C. M. P. cannot be
subject to an order under R.5.13 because the Crown is immune from discovery except in actions
to which it is a party.¹⁰
But if the Crown is willing to produce ﬁles about a party to the suit if he
consents, the court can (after a request on examination for discovery) force him to consent.¹¹
[Footnote 8 continued from prior page]
v. Reinhard (#2) 2000 ABQB 927, 12 CPC(5th) 148 (police investigation of an accident). And if the non-party
is part of the media, the tests are even stricter: Fullowka v. Royal Oak Mines (Re C. B. C. ) 2001 NWTSC 4,
[2001] 5 WWR 719, affd 2002 NWTCA 3, [2003] 2 WWR 213.

5-79
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.13(2)
D. Procedure
An order can be made under R.5.13 only if the holder of the papers is ﬁrst given notice and a
chance to respond to the motion for production.¹
It may be able to raise conﬁdentiality (especially
if the relevance of its papers are questionable).²
On conﬁdentiality as a bar to R.5.13, see the
annotation to R.5.8. A law ﬁrm had to reveal some objective details of money passing through its
hands.³
Given R.5.23(b), if a non-party (such as an employee or solicitor) can be questioned, then
there is no need for a R.5.13 order in order to have him or her bring papers to the questioning.⁴
In the ﬁrst instance, the party applying under R.5.13 should pay the costs, though the court
may later vary that.⁵
There is a discussion as to implied undertaking against collateral use of discovery information.⁶
But it is doubtful that it covers R.5.13.⁷
Refusal of an order under R.5.13 in one action did not bar such an order for the same records
in a later different action.⁸
Rule 6.22 can be used to examine a psychologist in another province about his refusal to
produce his ﬁle about a patient who is a party to an Alberta lawsuit and to produce the sealed ﬁle
to the Alberta court.⁹
E. Special Types of Litigation
A new (additional) action, intended ultimately to be certiﬁed as a class action, saw an
unsuccessful request for production of records by the defendant government. The proposed order
would allow anyone involved with the child welfare system for a certain 24-year period to get their
court ﬁles and child welfare ﬁles, without starting any lawsuit, or even being arguably part of the
new proposed class action.¹⁰
In criminal cases, there is a Charter right to have the court consider whether to order disclosure
to the defence of records or information in the hands of non-parties.¹¹
Sometimes it is a form of
discovery and not merely production at trial?

R.5.14(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
There are a few other statutory means to get papers from non-parties. The Bank Act¹
has
sections broadly similar to R.5.13, and they are compulsory. The Health Information Act²
lets a
patient (or executor) apply to the court for a copy of the patient’s hospital records. In practice those
are routinely provided on request.³
Freedom-of-information legislation (federal and provincial) is
a large topic, beyond the scope of this book. Equity also gives many beneﬁciaries the right to get
information from their trustees or ﬁduciaries, and that probably extends to physicians and patients
as well.⁴
Besides statutes, contracts sometimes give a right to get records or to view them.
Inspection and Copying of Records
5.14(1) Every party is entitled, with respect to a record that is relevant and
material and that is under the control of another party, to all of the following:
(a) to inspect the record on one or more occasions on making a written
request to do so;
(b) to receive a copy of the record on making a written request for the
copy and paying reasonable copying expenses;
(c) to make copies of the record when it is produced.
(2) This rule does not apply to a record for which a claim of privilege is made
unless the Court orders the record to be produced for inspection.⁵
(3) The Court or a party to an action who receives a computer-generated
document that was ﬁled with the court clerk may request the person ﬁling that
document or causing it to be issued to provide a copy of it in an electronic
format.⁶

Related Provisions
5.6(2) (c) (stating time when records may be inspected) ; 5.8n (privilege) ; 6.16 (producing

records at examinations); 6.26 (inspection of property)
A. General Rule
A litigant is entitled to more from its opponent than an afﬁdavit of records. It is also entitled
to see all the producible records listed in that afﬁdavit, i. e. , those in the ﬁrst schedule. The party
inspecting cannot be forced to work off copies if originals exist, and may examine the original

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.14(3)
records at length, and make copies of any or all of them. Usually, the solicitor possessing the
records makes an empty ofﬁce or boardroom available for examining the records, and will make
photocopies in return for reasonable payment of the cost of copying.¹
Now that cameras are
electronic and cellphones take pictures, copying important records is easier.
This Rule adds a new requirement that some electronic copies be provided, although this
was common practice before.²
On when computer records must be produced in specie (e. g. the
hard drive of the computer), see King’s Bench Civil Practice Note 14, “Guidelines for the Use of
Technology in any Civil Litigation Matter”, and the Innovative Health Group decision.³
If further
tests are needed, one might have to resort to R.6.28.⁴
Where a large volume of papers was disclosed in an afﬁdavit of records, the litigant owning
them was ordered to lend them for inspection to the opposing counsel, whose practice was never
to leave a client alone with such papers.⁵
If a record contains parts relevant and material and other parts privileged or otherwise not
relevant, then a party need disclose only the former portions, or produce the whole document
with the other portions blacked out. The court may inspect the full document to settle disputes
about scope of redaction.⁶
Absent a claim of privilege, whether a record produced in discovery
of records may be redacted is a fact-speciﬁc inquiry. Personal ﬁnancial records were in question
here. If the plaintiff can compare and see what is redacted, he has an onus to show redaction is
improper?⁷
Where a party moves for production of records and takes no position re redaction, but
gives the judge both original records and a redacted version of them, the judge can look at them
and order that only the redacted version be produced.⁸
A narrow test (including harm) was given
for redacting parts of documents disclosed.⁹
The Rules require that each party disclose its records, and there is no “self-help” provision for
one party to search through the documents of the other.¹⁰

R.5.14(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
B. Excuses and Exceptions
It takes very special circumstances to impose conditions, restrictions, or ﬁnancial burdens on
this right to inspect records, so long as proper notice is given.¹
There is no discretion under this
Rule, and inspection goes as of right.²
But a party to litigation cannot be compelled to do work to
produce a report or document which does not yet exist.³
A mere plea (in the statement of defence) that all the assets of the company have been sold
and assigned, including records, is not a plea of a record or document, and so whether or not old
R.193 has an equivalent in the new Rules, the statement of defence gives no right to inspect the
records of the sale.⁴
On redacting (masking) a passage in a record, see R.5.6 n. D.
C. Use of Records Produced
See R.5.33 and notes to it. Information got through discovery could be used to add claims and
parties in the same suit, and to complain to the Law Society.⁵
Production of a contract may be a sufﬁcient admission as to who are the parties to the contract.⁶
The Ashdown Hdwe. v. Singer rule on summaries of transactions etc. as proper evidence
was applied to computer-generated summaries.⁷
The various legal and ethical rules about a lawyer coming into possession of privileged records
inadvertently disclosed by an opposing litigant, do not apply to records merely irrelevant but not
privileged, especially if the initial production was deliberate, even if without much thought.⁸
D. Miscellaneous
Though legislation gives a right to communicate with the Court of King’s Bench in French,
on judicial review neither the tribunal (respondent) nor the opposing party need translate its ﬁles
into French.⁹
Where tens of thousands of records in Turkish were produced, the recipient would
have to pay for any translation it wanted, in the ﬁrst instance, but the producing party had to give
security for costs. Some general knowledge of contents, or rough translations were suggested.¹⁰
Material was obtained from an American court on condition that it could be ﬁled in a Canadian
court only if sealed, and the Supreme Court of Canada agreed with sealing the ﬁle. The panel

Admissions of Authenticity of Records
5.15(1) In this rule, “authentic” includes the fact that
(a) a document that is said to be an original was printed, written, signed
or executed as it purports to have been, and
(b) a document that is said to be a copy is a true copy of the original.
(2) Subject to subrules (3), (4), (5) and (6), a party who makes an afﬁdavit of
records or on whose behalf an afﬁdavit of records is ﬁled and a party on whom
an afﬁdavit of records is served are both presumed to admit that
(a) a record speciﬁed or referred to in the afﬁdavit is authentic, and
(b) if a record purports or appears to have been transmitted, the original
was sent by the sender and was received by the addressee.
(3) Subrule (2)
(a) does not apply if the maker or the recipient of the afﬁdavit objects in
accordance with subrule (4),
(b) does not prejudice the right of a party to object to the admission of
a record in evidence, and
(c) does not constitute an agreement or acknowledgment that the
record is relevant and material.
(4) The maker or recipient of an afﬁdavit of records is not presumed to make
the admission referred to in subrule (2) if, within 3 months after the date on
which the records are produced, the maker or recipient serves notice on the
other party that the authenticity or transmittal of a record, as the case may be, is
disputed and that it must be proved at trial.
(5) Notwithstanding that the maker or recipient of an afﬁdavit of records does
not serve a notice under subrule (4) within the time provided by that subrule,
the Court may order that the maker or recipient is not presumed to make the
admission referred to in subrule (2).⁴
(6) This rule does not apply to a record whose authenticity, receipt or transmission
has been denied by a party in the party’s pleadings.
[Alta. Reg.124/10;140/13]
