2 H. Z. v. Unger (M) 2015 ABQB 167, 2015 CarswellAlta 408 (Mar 10).
3 Stringham Denecky v. Sun Media Corp. (M) 2016 ABQB 692, 47 Alta LR(6th) 129, revd 2017 ABQB 687.
4 Quite similar to previous R.192, re-enacted by Alta. Reg.172/99. It came from 1968 R.190(1) to (3), which
was new in 1968, but parts of it came from Eng. O.32 r.2 (1937) and O.27 r.4. Cf. Judic. Ord. r.189.

5-84
R.5.15(6) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Information Note
A party who invokes subrule (4) unreasonably may be ordered to pay costs under rule
10.33(2) [Court considerations in making a costs award].

Related Provisions
5.2(2) (no admission of relevance or admissibility) ; 6.37 (notices to admit).

The previous version of this Rule was similar. It was frequently ignored, thereby wasting a lot
of time on questioning (examination for discovery) and at trial. This Rule provides another reason
to have formal disclosure of records. If a party ﬁles and serves a proper afﬁdavit of records,
that creates a number of deemed admissions. Both parties are deemed to admit that all the
records listed are authentic, that all copies are true copies, and that letters etc. were sent and
received.¹
That dispenses with proof of those things at trial,²
and saves having to ask about them
on questioning.
One can avoid such a deemed admission about a particular record by denying the admission
or its topic in one’s pleading, in one’s afﬁdavit of records, or in a reply to an opponent’s afﬁdavit
of records.³
There are costs penalties for refusing to admit these things when one should have
admitted them.
But this Rule does not make the correspondence or memos listed in an afﬁdavit of records
admissible as evidence of the truth of their contents (except maybe if it meets the tests for a
common-law admission by the author).⁴
Nor is disclosure of a record in an afﬁdavit of records
always of help in making it admissible at trial, and ordinarily that is not sufﬁcient (even under this
Rule) on its own for admissibility, though often it is a big help.⁵
Production of a contract may be a sufﬁcient admission as to who are the parties to the contract.⁶
Merely listing a redacted record in the afﬁdavit of records is not an admission whether the
portions edited out or not are relevant and not privileged.⁷
Proof that a party possessed a record is some evidence that the record exists, and also is
evidence that the party knows what its contents show or say, and of his or her connection with
the events in them, and even his or her state of mind. If there is additional evidence that the party
recognized, adopted, or acted on them, then the record can be received in evidence to prove the
truth of the contents so recognized etc. , as an exception to the hearsay rule.⁸
But if “facts” are

neutral cite).
2 Or on a motion for summary judgment, or judgment on admissions: 1103785 Alta. v. Exxonmobil Can.2008

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.16
uninformative without technical or scientiﬁc opinions about what they mean, a judge cannot draw
inferences about what they mean without qualiﬁed expert evidence. The hearsay exception for
records in possession does not change that.¹
Undisclosed Records Not to be Used Without Permission
5.16 A party who
(a) does not disclose a relevant and material record in an afﬁdavit of
records referred to in rule 5.6 [Form and contents of afﬁdavit of
records],
(b) does not disclose as required by rule 5.10 [Subsequent disclosure
of records] a relevant and material record that is found, created or
obtained, or
(c) does not produce a relevant and material record in accordance with
a valid request to do so under rule 5.14 [Inspection and copying of
records]
may not afterwards use the record in evidence in the action unless the parties
otherwise agree or the Court otherwise orders on the basis that there was a
sufﬁcient reason for the failure to disclose.²

Related Provisions
5.10 (subsequently discovered records) ; 5.27 (continuing duty to disclose).

Rule 5.16 imposes a severe penalty for omitting a record from either one’s afﬁdavit of records
or one’s production of records.³
One cannot put the record into evidence on any application⁴
or at
trial or on appeal, or after, without showing⁵
a good excuse for the original failure. The Rule does
not even expressly allow waiver, though in cases of inadvertence R.1.5 might allow that, probably
on fairly stiff terms. On terms, see the Paterson case.⁶
The tests for allowing later use of the undisclosed record are a reasonable explanation,
amount of prejudice, how essential the evidence is, and the ends of justice.⁷
Sufﬁcient reason

R.5.17(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
for the failure to disclose must be given; otherwise the court cannot allow use of the undisclosed
document in evidence.¹
Non-disclosures by the opponent are not excuses; two wrongs do not
make a right.²
This Rule is important. It was used in mid-trial to bar new documents never before disclosed,
which meant that the trial judge did not have enough evidence to quantify commissions payable,
and so nothing could be awarded or deducted under that head.³
On sufﬁcient reason for non-production, see also R.5.12n.
Reasonable explanation of the non-disclosure is the ﬁrst line of inquiry, and the party seeking
admission most meet that branch of the test. Privilege is no excuse for non-disclosure. The party
objecting to use of the document not in the afﬁdavit of records need not show that his evidence
would have differed had it been in that afﬁdavit; knowing of it could have changed other aspects
of the other party’s litigation strategy.⁴
Can a defendant produce records as trial exhibits which were not produced in disclosure of
records and which an answer to an undertaking given during questioning had said did not exist,
because the court-appointed monitor had seized many records, including these ones, and the
plaintiff had access to those?⁵
The Rules do not bar admission at trial of a privileged document by the holder of the privilege,
especially to impeach a witness. The court can prevent trial by ambush, e. g. by giving the other
party an adjournment, with costs.⁶
Spoliation is a rule of evidence providing that when evidence has been intentionally destroyed,
a rebuttable presumption of fact arises that the evidence would tell against the spoliator. Whether
there has been spoliation should often be decided at trial.⁷

R.5.17(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
(3) This rule applies whether the person to be questioned is within or outside the
Court’s jurisdiction.¹
Information Note
The Class Proceedings Act contains limitations on who can be questioned when the action
is a proceeding under that Act.

Related Provisions
2.8 (discovery in class proceedings) ; 5.2 (what is relevant and material) ; 5.3 (limiting

discovery); 5.4 (duties of corporate representative) ; 5.23 (duties of other witnesses) ; 5.27
(continuing duty to disclose) ; 5.31 (use of transcripts) ; 5.37 (questioning experts) ; 6.19
(interpreters) ; 9.29 (examination in aid of enforcement) ; 10.33 (costs)
A. General
Examination for discovery is very different from cross-examination, and calling both
“questioning” in the 2010 Rules is very confusing. On examination for discovery (e. g. under this
Rule), the person examined is a party or connected with a party, and usually expected to be
informed.²
On what is “relevant and material”, see R.5.2n. Subject to that, anything reasonably relevant
can be asked, and the court will not pick apart each question minutely.³
On timing of discovery (questioning), and multiple examinations of a witness, see R.5.20n.
This commentary (to this and the following Rules) is mostly about the law. For a long, practical,
detailed guide to strategy, tactics and technique before and during an examination for discovery
(questioning), see Côté, Systematic Advocacy, Chap.8 (Juriliber 2017).
There are useful general guides to questioning in Harris’ book.⁴
B. Who Can Be Examined
1. Parties to the Suit
On selecting a corporate representative, see R.5.29n.
The lawyer for each party can question orally under oath each other party who is “adverse
in interest. ”⁵
For example, in some construction suits, two defendants (builder and architect) are

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.17(3)
almost alternatively liable, and so are ﬁerce opponents of each other. Conversely, in many other
suits, two defendants may have identical interests.¹
Whether parties are “adverse in interest” for
the purposes of questioning is determined on the pleadings as a whole, and not on an issue-
by-issue basis.²
However, the pleadings are not the only source for adversity in interest, and it is
not limited to opposite parties.³
The adversity can be pecuniary interest, or any other substantial
interest.⁴
It may sufﬁce that the party in question has a clear interest in disproving an allegation
against someone else.⁵
One can question every adverse party, however many there are,⁶
and one can question an
adverse party who has been noted in default.⁷
Alberta Rules make a third party a full party to the
suit.⁸
Something like R.1.7(2) was given broad effect to let an intervener question, in the Loewen
case.⁹
That a party is adverse in interest only determines that he or she can be questioned, not what
precise questions can be asked.¹⁰

Certain other people who are not parties can be questioned, but mere witnesses cannot.¹¹
Those who can be are.

To use Rule 5.17(1) (d) about a former employee, one needs evidence that that person appears
to have relevant and material information that was acquired because of the present or former
employment by the adverse party. That she once worked for that party, with no other details, such
as the nature of her position or duties, does not sufﬁce. Nor does an assumption that she has
relevant information. That is error.²
The defendant was denied the right to examine for discovery four corporate “witnesses”. On
whether the proposed topics were relevant and material, or just ﬁshing, topics should be realistic,
not merely speculative, and not entail disproportionate expense.³
The former Rules previously allowed questioning of a person for whose beneﬁt an action was
prosecuted, but that Rule has been abolished.⁴
3. Restrictions
Charter protection, or common-law privilege against incriminating oneself, are no bar to
being questioned, nor to answering particular questions.⁵
Someone suspected of an offence can
be compelled to testify so long as the proceeding is not really pre-trial interrogation; there is
no violation of s.7 of the Charter because the answers would be inadmissible at later criminal
proceedings.⁶
Someone from whom the tax authorities demand a large penalty, alleging false
statements, is not someone “charged with an offence” under the Charter s.11, and so can be
questioned by the Crown in his appeal against the penalties.⁷
The Crown has common-law immunity from all civil disclosure. If it is sued under its liability
legislation, that statute often makes it subject to discovery, but if it does not, the Crown is still
immune.⁸
Legislation on suits against the provincial Crown, common-law Crown immunity, and
case law, say that examining for discovery a Cabinet Minister is to be exceptional. There must

R.5.17(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
be special circumstances, and he or she must be the person best informed. Both tests must
be satisﬁed. The test is strict to prevent ﬁshing expeditions, and keep questioning relevant and
material.¹
Because so many classes of people may be questioned, and the scope of relevance is fairly
broad, conscientious or aggressive lawyers may discover (question) too long. R.5.3 and R.5.19
are the prime defences against that. R.5.19 lets the court limit the number of employees or ex-
employees to be examined, or disallow any particular employee’s examination, but parties rarely
apply under it. Rule 5.3 gives a broader power to control or end questioning, but only in fairly
strong cases. Both Rules 5.3 and 5.17(2) put limits on costs for too lengthy questioning.
In a wrongful dismissal suit by a former executive, leave to question the very busy head
of the big defendant company was given. She had personally been involved in the executive
performance assessments. They were contentious issues.²
A party could question directors of the
opposing company; the topics proposed sounded very material, and the examination would not
take long. For efﬁciency, the ofﬁcers of the company would be questioned ﬁrst, and then the non-
ofﬁcer directors would be questioned, all on the same day.³
4. Miscellaneous
In an estate ﬁght, the court need not permit examination for discovery of all non-party siblings,
especially if a case management judge has already decided what persons should be examined.⁴
The Administrator of the Motor Vehicles Accident Claims Act need not submit to questioning.⁵
A person certifying a return to a judicial review motion is not open to questioning. A Return is
not the same as an afﬁdavit of records.⁶
5. Counsel for the Witness
A topic often overlooked is who (if anyone) will be the lawyer for a non-party questioned, such
as an employee. There are few, if any, cases on it, but the topic bears careful thought.⁷
The Law
Society of Alberta’s Code of Conduct warns about conﬂicts of interest, and directs a lawyer to be
very careful who is his or her client. The lawyer for a party cannot instruct or advise the witness
unless he or she acts for the witness.⁸
Persons who do not so act, have no right to intrude and tell
a witness not to answer a question.

5-93
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.17(3)
C. Purpose of Oral Discovery
On privilege, see R.5.8n and R.5.25n. On what is relevant and material, see R.5.2. On what
are proper questions, see R.5.25n.
There are many aims of questioning (examination for discovery), and many uses to which the
transcript can be put.¹
The two aims which shape the questions most, are preventing surprise
at trial, and getting admissions for use at trial. So such questioning can do more than seek
information.²
(Both of those aims can be subdivided further. ) Some lawyers emphasize the ﬁrst
aim, and conduct long exhaustive questioning. Others emphasize the latter, plan certain key
admissions which they want, and stop when they have got them (or clearly will not get them).
A third aim of questioning is common: to size up the person examined (as a possible trial
witness or as the subject of the suit).
The ability to object to a question under R.5.25(2) (c) on the basis that it is unnecessary
or unreasonable, should be narrowly interpreted. It is no objection to a question that the party
questioning could get the information another way, e. g. from the Land Titles Ofﬁce.³
It can try to
get the witness to agree to a suggested answer, much as cross-examination does.⁴
Rule 5.4(2) speciﬁcally addresses the obligation of a corporate representative, and R.5.23
addresses the obligations of other witnesses.
Demanding too much detail and too many background documents on examination for discovery
can go beyond what is relevant and material.⁵
Usually in commercial litigation, a party’s understanding or thoughts about its obligations is
irrelevant, or at best of tertiary relevance and contrary to rules against asking re matters going
primarily to credit or credibility. So no examination for discovery questions on that are proper.⁶
The questioner can ask to have papers or records produced if they are under the control of
the party questioned.⁷
One need not prove that a certain class of papers exist, in order to demand
a search and production; each party has a duty to search for and disclose relevant papers.⁸
The
formal appointment for questioning should therefore tell the witness to bring the papers with him
or her to the questioning?⁹
Papers identiﬁed by the witness are often marked by the court reporter as exhibits, but the
reporter or examiner never keeps them; the party or witness owning them gets them back.

R.5.17(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The lawyer questioning cannot require the witness to draw a sketch, nor write anything.¹
There is a very limited scope of questioning while there is an undecided forum non conveniens
motion?²
At the end of questioning, the lawyer for the party questioned may ask questions of his own
client, under R.5.25(5), but that is rarely done in practice.
Proportionality is a general principle under R.1.2(4). Discovery must be kept within reasonable
bounds. It should not be endless and unlimited.³
D. Miscellaneous
Questioning (examination for discovery) for an Alberta suit can be held outside Alberta.⁴
The
witness need not reside in Alberta.⁵
There is no point in making an order here for examination
for discovery of someone abroad if he does not consent and the courts where he lives would not
enforce the order. That country has sovereign rights. That is especially so where the order is not
to get evidence for trial.⁶
Questioning cannot be held outside Canada, nor a commission be given,
simply to let a party avoid an outstanding warrant in Canada.⁷
A King’s Bench Justice has power to order that questioning (examination for discovery) be by
way of video conference. That was appropriate during the COVID-19 pandemic. Questioning in
a private-law dispute probably has no constitutional aspect. Courts must go on functioning, but
should not spread disease. Video questioning was well established before the pandemic. What
is used from a questioning is a transcript, and no justice or applications judge ever sees the
witnesses to it or their demeanor.⁸
Alberta courts have the jurisdiction to order parties to attend in Alberta for questioning in
support of an international arbitration.⁹
On conduct money for the witness, see the notes to R.6.17. On interpreters, see R.6.19.
Such questioning is not public. In theory the Clerk of the Court (or maybe an ofﬁcial examiner)
runs it (though he or she does not actually attend).¹⁰
In a doubtful case, the Clerk can rule on who
can attend. Parties and their lawyers can attend, but usually no one else can.¹¹
It takes fairly strong
precise grounds to exclude a party from the room while another party on the same side is being
questioned in Alberta;¹²
mere reasonable apprehension of tailoring evidence is not enough. Even

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.18(1)
with an order that non-parties not attend an examination for discovery, a live feed to the next room
with others in it is permitted, if they cannot communicate back to anyone inside the room (e. g. by
phone), and if the transcript and feed remain conﬁdential.¹
A witness with health issues showed that attending a questioning appointment would cause
her irreparable harm (part of the traditional tripartite test). But denying a chance to question would
produce greater harm on the other side, and delay the lawsuit or motion pending. So the witness
had to attend, but for two-hour sessions on three different days in one month.²
Failure to attend questioning (examination for discovery) after service of an order requiring
attendance, or refusal to answer questions once there, is contempt of court.³
That is why anyone
questioned should have his or her own lawyer along, to advise which questions by law he or she
need not answer. It takes very strong evidence to show that a party’s health is too delicate to
permit questioning without grave danger.⁴
In a bad enough case, the failure of a corporate party’s
representative to prepare to answer questions, before coming to questioning, could be contempt.⁵
To test whether length of questioning was excessive, the ratio of questions asked to answers
read in at trial is not very useful.⁶
Questioning (examination for discovery) under R.5.17 allows questions about the afﬁdavit of
records.⁷
Persons Providing Services to Corporation or Partnership
5.18(1) Subject to subrules (2) and (3), if
(a) a party cannot obtain relevant and material information from an
ofﬁcer or employee or a former ofﬁcer or former employee of a
corporation or partnership that is a party adverse in interest,
(b) it would be unfair to require the party seeking the information to
proceed to trial without having the opportunity to ask questions
about the information sought, and
(c) the questioning will not cause undue hardship, expense or delay to,
or unfairness to, any other party or to the person to be questioned,
the party may question, under oath, a person who has provided services for the
corporation or partnership and who can provide the best evidence on the issue.
(2) A person described in subrule (1) may be questioned only
(a) by written agreement of the parties, or
(b) with the Court’s permission.

Related Provisions
5.4 (appointing corporate representative) ; 5.17 (examination of officers and employees)

The 2020 amendment extends this Rule to cover partnerships and their employees.
Rule 5.17(1) (d) above on persons “employed” doubtless covers employees. See the notes to
it. The previous Rules might have been broader, and might have covered some agents or some
independent contractors.¹
This has been provided for in present Rule 5.18, which is relatively new,
but now there is a qualiﬁcation for non-employees: questioning true employees or ex-employees
must be attempted ﬁrst. The public-policy tests in R.5.18 are codiﬁed from the old case law.²
So
someone not an “employee” as the term is known in employment law, cannot be examined for
discovery unless the information can be got no other way, and going to trial without the information
would not be fair.³
Rule 5.18 must be interpreted and applied in context. The consultants in question were
retained after the dispute arose and not involved in the decisions creating the dispute, nor were
the consultants under the party’s direction or supervision. Rule 5.18 is for situations where it is
impossible to get the information from the party. Mere convenience, or bypassing the ofﬁcer’s

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.18(5)
duty to inquire and report back, is not enough. Mere inaccuracy of some previous answers is
not enough. Non-parties have no duty to reveal what documents exist. This was a ﬁshing trip.
Suspicion is not enough. Nor is greater efﬁciency.¹
Their questioning is not conﬁned to topics which they learned during the employment in
question.²
Nor to topics admissible at trial against the employer.³
One cannot question a lawyer who was acting as such.⁴
On Cabinet Ministers, see the Civil
Procedure Encyclopedia.⁵
The court can limit the number of employees questioned.⁶
In some cases, R.5.18 would be an independent ground to examine a company’s accountant,
but not where another party (on the same side) was examined for discovery was asked the
relevant questions and undertook to get the answers from that accountant.⁷
The right of a corporation to select who answers for it on examination for discovery should
not be lightly undone by the court, because the consequences for the corporation could be
grave. Especially if the chosen person seems well informed.⁸
Rule 5.18(4) covers use at trial of
questioning answers, and allows a court declaration of use of answers. Though non-parties may
have been contemplated when the Rule was drafted, it is not so conﬁned. There is no unfairness
from the fact that their employer inspection company is a third party who was a contractor of the
plaintiff, not an employee.⁹
No one can force someone, especially a non-party, to attend an interview with (opposing)
counsel, but the court can remove the non-party’s existing duties of conﬁdentiality. The court would
do so only if it is the only way to get necessary information. Not where questioning (examination
for discovery) and the witness informing himself or herself would sufﬁce to get the information.¹⁰

R.5.19 PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Limit or Cancellation of Questioning
5.19 On application, the Court may do either or both of the following:
(a) limit the number of persons subject to questioning by a party;
(b) cancel an appointment for questioning that the Court considers
unnecessary, improper or vexatious.¹

Related Provisions
2.8 (discovery in class proceedings) ; 5.1 (purpose of Part 5) ; 5.3 (limiting discovery) ; 5.17(2)

(costs of examining multiple witnesses) ; 5.25 (appropriate questions)
The Rules now do not limit the number of employees, former employees, or ofﬁcers who may
be Examined for Discovery. Only costs of that are dealt with in R.5.17. Whether the number to
be examined is excessive and should be prevented depends on whether they likely have relevant
and material information. The court may limit the number examined under R.5.19(a), but only on
an application by a party, not an application by one of the people to be examined, if he or she
is not a party. A witness can apply under R.5.19(b), but the person then moving must show the
examination is unnecessary, vexatious, or improper. The onus being on the person applying to
bar or restrict.²
An appointment for examination for discovery was cancelled under R.5.19 because the
judge had under reserve a motion for summary judgment.³
And that occurred where a summary
judgment application was pending and the questioning was a response to it; what to do then is
discretionary, but here the party seeking the questioning had to know the facts about the disputed
issue.⁴
An order staying a suit against all defendants allowed cancelling or barring an examination for
discovery, so an appeal about that was not restored.⁵
When Questioning is to Take Place
5.20(1) Unless the parties otherwise agree, or the Court in exceptional
circumstances otherwise orders, a party may not question a party or person
under this Division unless the questioning party has served an afﬁdavit of
records on the party adverse in interest.⁶
(2) Subject to subrule (1), the questioning of a person is to take place as follows:
(a) questioning by the plaintiff, at any time after
(i) a statement of defence has been served on the plaintiff, or
(ii) the time for serving the statement of defence has expired;

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.20(2)

Related Provisions
5.5 (affidavit of records) ; 12.38 (affidavit of records in family law) ; 12.38(5) (time of discoveries

in family cases).
If the Plaintiff has served an afﬁdavit of records, the Plaintiff can question as soon as the
relevant statement of defence is delivered, or the time for it has expired. A defendant noted in
default may be questioned.²
There cannot be questioning before a statement of defence, in order
to support a motion to certify a class action.³
Questioning cannot take place if the questioning party has not served an afﬁdavit of records,
unless the parties agree or the court so orders.
The Rules do not set the order of examination of the opposing side’s ofﬁcers, employees, and
former ones. It depends on the facts and the strategy, and cases about “near employees” such as
advisers, are not on point.⁴
Examining a variety of employees of a party in a suit with many issues and many acts by many
people over many years, is proper, in this case thousands of plaintiffs (in this class action) and
practices over many years developed by many employees, and applied by even more, especially
at an early stage or before any actual examinations.⁵
Nor can one party ask that the opponent get certain information by a request that the corporate
ofﬁcer give an undertaking. Each party can do its examining in the way that it thinks best. Party
autonomy and parties’ responsibility for control of the litigation, was one of the objectives of the

Footnote
2010 Rules.⁶
An appellant who had been denied various forms of questioning in King’s Bench, on appeal
was allowed it, on stringent terms to preserve the trial date.⁷
If pleadings have been amended,
one can usually get further questioning (examination for discovery).⁸
But amendment of pleadings
does not necessarily give a right to further questioning if there have been previous questioning
and production orders that satisfactorily cover the matter.⁹
A party who amends pleadings (beyond
trivial changes) probably opens himself or herself up to a second round.¹⁰

5-100
R.5.20(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The courts will rarely grant a party (counsel) who has ended questioning a given witness, a
second round to question the same witness, no matter how incomplete the ﬁrst questioning was,
nor what change of counsel has since occurred.¹
Exceptional circumstances are necessary.²
A
third round of questions was not ordered where the witness had done nothing wrong and there
were no special circumstances, as is needed for a second examination.³
There is no right to hold open and incomplete the questioning of one party, while one questions
another party.⁴
But if one is a party and the other a non-party witness, that could be very practical.
Difﬁculty of coordinating schedules is no reason to have questioning without every party or
counsel present?⁵
See also the Strategic Acquisition case, supra, R.517n. B.
Some lawyers think that it is very important whether the plaintiff questions the defendant before
the defendant questions the plaintiff (or vice versa). Usually the party who takes out and serves
a formal appointment ﬁrst, thereby determines who questions ﬁrst.⁶
If no formal appointment
gives an answer to the priority dispute⁷
(e. g. , because counsel merely agreed on a date with no
formal appointment or notice), then it is doubtful that there is any priority rule, though some cases
suggest that the plaintiff questions ﬁrst.⁸
Though it may be customary for the plaintiff to examine
ﬁrst, it is inefﬁcient, slow and too expensive, to require that the plaintiff complete all its questioning
of every witness whom it wishes, before the defendant may question any witnesses on the other
side.⁹
A case management judge can direct that questions begin with one topic, where one defendant
is interested only in that topic.¹⁰
One plaintiff’s evidence should be taken bene esse, as he was 90 years old. However there
was no immediate urgency shown, and each side was entitled to have questioning of the other
side before the evidence of the plaintiff was taken bene esse.¹¹

5-101
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.21(1)
In an ordinary suit, the right to have questioning ends with trial,¹
indeed with an ordinary
certiﬁcate of readiness, and there is no right to question pending appeal.²
But statutory proceedings
may permit some form of questioning, such as written interrogatories.³
Pending an Originating Application, some partial disclosure of records and limited questioning
was conducted. The chambers judge properly exercised a discretion not to adjourn the Originating
Application to allow for more discovery, but certain records were ordered produced.⁴
Limited
questioning was ordered pending return (hearing) of an Originating Notice,⁵
or a motion for
summary judgment.⁶
Resolving the clash of one side’s desire for questioning with the other’s
desire for summary judgment depends on the facts of the individual case,⁷
but a defendant cannot
derail a pending summary judgment motion by giving notice of questioning which he could have
sought before.⁸
Appointment for Questioning
5.21(1) A party may question a person whom the party is entitled to question
under this Part by serving on the person a notice of appointment for questioning
in Form 29.⁹
(2) Rules 6.16 [Contents of appointment notice] to 6.19 [Interpreters] and 6.38
[Requiring attendance for questioning] apply for the purposes of subrule (1)
with one modiﬁcation, namely, unless the parties otherwise agree, the notice
of appointment for questioning must be served 20 days or more before the
appointment date.¹⁰

Related Provisions
5.19 (striking an appointment for questioning) ; 6.22 (examinations outside Alberta)

On cancelling an appointment, see R.5.19 and notes.
Formerly, the appointment was issued either by an examiner (in Edmonton or Calgary), or the
Clerk of the Court (in other judicial districts). Now counsel for the party wishing to ask questions
signs and serves the notice, without permission from anyone else. Examiners are usually the
heads of private court reporting ﬁrms.

5-102
R.5.22 PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
In practice, the Clerk of the Court never attends, and the examiner rarely does. Usually only
a court reporter attends, swears the witnesses, takes verbatim shorthand notes, and marks the
exhibits. Outside Edmonton or Calgary the examination may take place at the court house, but
otherwise it is usually in the ofﬁce of one of the lawyers. Disputes about whether a question is
proper, or other procedure, are usually settled later, after the transcripts have been delivered,
by a motion under R.5.25(4). It is now uncommon to interrupt the discovery to ask the Clerk or
examiner for a ruling, or to attend upon a justice or applications judge in chambers.
Someone who is under formal appointment to attend questioning cannot adjourn it or refuse to
attend unilaterally, even with a letter from a doctor.¹
Someone in poor health may be able to have
the court limit the duration of each day’s questioning.²
Decisions by a case management judge on logistics are reviewed on appeal on a highly
deferential basis. A decision to have live questioning in Calgary (where the action was) and not
in writing, not in London, England and not by video conference, was upheld. The applicant (living
abroad) alleged mere inconvenience and expense.³
Questioning Options
5.22 Subject to rule 5.24 [Oral and written questioning limitations], questioning
may be conducted
(a) orally, under oath, or
(b) by written questions, answered under oath, subject to the limitations
of rule 5.28 [Written questions].

2.4 (disclosure of partners) ; 5.24 (limits on written questioning) ; 5.28 (limits on written

questions); 12.39 (written interrogatories in family law)
Written interrogatories merely to avoid travel were refused.⁴
Preparation for Questioning
5.23 A person to be questioned under this Division, other than a corporate
representative, must
(a) reasonably prepare for questioning, and
(b) bring to the questioning any records likely to be required in respect
of which there is no claim of privilege.⁵

5-103
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.23
Information Note
Note that a person to be questioned under this rule must reasonably prepare himself or
herself while a corporate representative must inform himself or herself of relevant and
material records and relevant and material information under rule 5.4(2) [Appointment of
corporate representatives].

Related Provisions
5.4 (duties of corporate representative) ; 5.27 (continuing duty to disclose)

On what questions are relevant and what are not and so cannot be asked, see R.5.2n. above.
A party (or corporate party’s representative¹
) questioned under these Rules can be asked
about all the knowledge within the control of that party.²
He or she can be required to inform
himself or herself³
from the party’s records and from present or past employees and agents, and
disclose the information.⁴
Or to produce papers which the party controls.⁵
A party need not inform
himself or herself from persons whom he or she does not control,⁶
such as relatives, a spouse,⁷
friends, employers,⁸
or related companies which are not agents. An executor who is a party, or a
nonparty employee of a party, may not have to inform himself or herself, but under R.5.23 must
“reasonably prepare”. If a person who was formerly an ofﬁcer or director of a defunct corporation
is examined for discovery (questioned) in her personal capacity, not as the company’s appointed
spokesperson for examination, she need not refresh her memory from someone whom she does
not personally control (nor adopt that person’s information).⁹
Older cases held that an employee or ofﬁcer or corporate spokesperson may not have to
answer about things learned outside that employment or ofﬁce, but the more recent Alberta cases
are contra.¹⁰
But the witness may have to consent to a former employer’s releasing its ﬁles about
him, if the employer is willing to do so on his consent.¹¹

5-104
R.5.24 PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
In a third-party claim, a request on discovery for information which the party being examined
knows is valid even if the information was obtained under a different contract or different
construction project or from other persons. Rule 3.46(a) (a) makes the third party a party to the
main action.¹
Is the Crown forbidden to answer that it does not know what happened (say) over a century
ago?²
In deciding how much work had to be done for a witness to inform himself to answer
questions (apparently during the questioning, i. e. examination for discovery), it is relevant that the
Crown is a very large organization, and so trying to see if anyone connected with the Crown has
any information on any of hundreds of questions, could be an undue amount of work. The solution
apparently was to be stricter on the standard of materiality and relevance.³
It may not be a sufﬁcient objection to questions on discovery, or proof that it was mere ﬁshing,
to show that 277 undertakings have been requested. Sometimes the two sides have a signiﬁcantly
different perspective on what is relevant and material and so for how to prepare for questioning and
what documents to produce.⁴
Sometimes the witness does not sufﬁciently prepare beforehand.
The rule that a party must inform itself from sources to which it has legal access, does not
extend to records held by a non-party who has an indemnity agreement with the party.⁵
The duty to produce records which one controls, extends to those which one does not yet
possess, but which privacy and government disclosure legislation gives one a right to seek?⁶
Oral and Written Questioning Limitations
5.24 Unless the parties otherwise agree or the Court otherwise orders,
(a) if more than one party is entitled to question a person, the questioning
must be oral, and
(b) a party may not question a person both orally and by written
questioning.

5-105
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.25(1)
Appropriate Questions and Objections
5.25(1) During questioning, a person is required to answer only
(a) relevant and material questions, and¹
(b) questions in respect of which an objection is not upheld under
subrule (2).
(2) A party or a witness being questioned may object to an oral or written
question during questioning but only for one or more of the following reasons:
(a) privilege;
(b) the question is not relevant and material;
(c) the question is unreasonable or unnecessary;
(d) any other ground recognized at law.
(3) A corporate representative may object to an oral or written question during
questioning on the basis that it would be unduly onerous for the corporate
representative to inform himself or herself in the circumstances.
(4) If an objection to a question cannot be resolved the Court must decide its
validity.
(5) After the questioning party has ﬁnished questioning a person, that person
may be questioned by the party for whom the person is or may be a witness to
explain, elaborate or provide context for an answer initially given.²
(6) Following answers to the explanatory, elaborative or contextual questions,
the person may be questioned again about the person’s answers.³
Information Note
The Court has a broad range of powers to deal with situations in which the expense or
difficulty of complying with a rule would be disproportional to its benefit. For example, rule
5.3 [Modification or waiver of this Part] and the Court’s power under rule 1.4 [Procedural
orders].
If there is non-compliance with a rule, a costs award may be made and rule 10.49 [Penalty
for contravening the rules] would apply.

Related Provisions
4.19 (documents resulting from JDR) ; 4.20 (confidentiality of JDR) ; 4.28 (formal offers

confidential); 5.1 (purpose of Part 5) ; 5.1(1) (e) (discouraging delay due to discovery) ; 5.3
(limiting discovery) ; 5.4(2) (obligations of corporate representative) ; 5.8 (documents that
must be produced); 5.9n (privileged documents) ; 5.19 (limits on questioning) ; 5.23 (preparing
for questioning).

5-106
R.5.25(6) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
A. Cross-References
On privilege, see R.5.8n. On what is relevant and material, and what is irrelevant and cannot
be asked, see R.5.2 and notes to it. On undertakings given at discoveries to provide information
later, see R.5.30.
B. Speciﬁc Types of Question
1. General
The ability to object to a question under R.5.25(2) (c) on the basis that it is unreasonable
or unnecessary, should not be allowed to overrule the general rule that relevant and material
questions must be answered. It is not an answer to an order for further and better answers to
questioning or production of records, that doing so would cost money and put the party to some
trouble.¹
The courts should not put limits on examination for discovery which disentitle a litigant from
obtaining discovery information which he could clearly introduce at trial if he had it. And at
interlocutory stages, the court should not too ﬁnely measure a proposed line of argument; a
rational strategy should be enough.²
See further R.5.2 n. B.2.
On questioning under these Rules, the rule against hearsay does not apply.³
(The witness
need not say whether he or she believes the hearsay, even if he or she had to go out and inform
himself or herself about it.⁴
)
However, most of the other exclusionary rules of evidence do apply, such as privilege, or the
rules excluding opinion evidence. (How the latter applies is a little complicated.⁵
)
2. Speciﬁc Types of Question
In general, one cannot ask hypothetical questions.⁶
One cannot ask questions going only to
credit or credibility.⁷
Nor can one ask questions of law.⁸
Nor questions whose answer would require
a conclusion on a point of contractual interpretation squarely for the trial judge to decide.⁹
One
can only ask about the facts, not about how they can or cannot (or will or will not) be proved.¹⁰
One
cannot ask on what facts a party relies for part of his pleading.¹¹
The well known Can-Air Serv. v.
British Aviation Ins. case does not forbid all discovery questions which ask the witness to make
a selection; it is commonly understood that a witness is to answer certain questions based on

6 Stone Sapphire v. Transglobal Comm’ns. Grp. (#2) 2007 ABQB 238, 416 AR 306.

5-107
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.25(6)
what is relevant, not literally listing everything that happened.¹
One cannot ask an unfair question,
such as one which misstates a previous answer.
Generally a layperson cannot be asked on examination for discovery for an opinion, but it is
different where he is a professional sued for breach of duty, as then his competence and expertise
are at issue. If the questions then are not too broad or vague. A question about the state of the
witness’ understanding, expectation, or opinion at a speciﬁc time is a question of fact, not law or
opinion.²
A hypothetical question may be proper, so long as it is not speculative, unrealistic, or
lacking an air of reality, such as commenting on others’ opinions.³
If information or sources outside
the witness’ control arise, maybe to some degree hypothetical questions may be asked?⁴
It is no objection to a question that the witness would likely not know the answer, nor that
the questioner already knows the answer.⁵
The questioner is entitled to get the witness’s answer
(even that the witness does not know), under oath and on the record. Whether one can ask about
previous similar incidents and precautions taken later is complicated.⁶
Issues between the witness
or his or her party (on the one hand), and another party (who is not the one questioning), cannot
be questioned on.⁷
Previous testimony can be put to a witness without using a portion of transcript verbatim.⁸
“Asked and answered” is not a valid objection to examination for discovery questions in
Canada.⁹
It is not proper to object to an examination for discovery (questioning) question because
counsel objecting does not understand it.¹⁰
That evidence was obtained illegally or surreptitiously is not usually a ground to exclude it in
a civil lawsuit.¹¹
Questions on examination for discovery about special damages can ask to have them
organized into categories, and that is not creating a new record.¹²

examined for discovery as a third party need not answer questions as to the plaintiff’s damages, even if she
happens to be the next friend (see now R.5.17(1) (c) ). Examination for discovery of a third party is only on
the pleadings in the third party proceedings, and this third party did not contest the plaintiff’s claim: Collacutt

(Town) 2014 ABQB 458, 591 AR 141 (¶ 107), and Allan v. Epp (M) 2018 ABQB 85, JCC 1501 11199. Allan
v. Epp omits the contrary statement allowing repetition, in the one (1942) case cited! A similar conclusion
following Allan v. Telfer, with similar incomplete authority, is Real Est. Council of Alta. v. Moser 2019
ABQB 106, JCC 1801 09968 (¶’s 6-7). And even if that is a legitimate objection, the Moser case admits four
large exceptions where repetition is proper. The counsel questioning can, in any event, come at a topic from
several directions: Allan v. Telfer (¶ 72).

5-108
R.5.25(6) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
3. Others’ Knowledge
Someone examined can only be asked about what he knows or what is within the knowledge
of persons under his control. So he cannot be asked about information which he or the corporation
got only through litigation discovery.¹
It is perfectly proper to ask questions to learn the names of people with knowledge who might
themselves later be questioned.²
The 2010 Rules call for means in which claims can be fairly
and reasonably resolved in a timely and cost-efﬁcient way, and as names of witnesses are a
proper topic for questioning, at an earlier stage they can be sought and obtained.³
Absent litigation
privilege, the name of a witness must be disclosed on questioning if asked for.⁴
On seeking a large number of undertakings, see R.5.23n.
The court can refuse to make the witness search for information if the search would be unduly
onerous and disproportionate to the information which it could provide.⁵
C. Procedure
1. Applications to Settle Disputed Topics
The new Rule speciﬁes grounds for objections. Any dispute over the propriety of a question
or an answer can be taken to an applications judge (or a justice). Usually the party questioning
waits until the transcripts are available some weeks later, and then issues a written application to
compel answers. Occasionally counsel will still follow the very old practice: they will adjourn the
discovery and attend on an applications judge or justice.
And rarely, the court orders supervision of the examination for discovery.⁶
On a second
appeal from an order to answer questions on a cross-examination on afﬁdavit in the same matter,
the court ordered them answered, and ordered that any questions arising therefrom would be
answered before a master.⁷
An application over disputed questions or answers should relate to speciﬁc disputes, because
the court will rarely make blanket advance rulings about questions not yet asked.⁸
So it is preferable
that counsel objecting to a question then give all his or her grounds of objection, especially as it

2 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 28, Part M.4. But not Crown informers? Leipert v. R. [1997] 1 SCR 281, 207 NR
145. The former Rules speciﬁcally provided that disclosure of witnesses was not a valid objection to a
question.

by the chambers judge hearing the substantive motion: ibid. In another case, a very early trial impended,
but some questioning remained unﬁnished. The Court directed that despite any objections to questions’

5-109
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.25(6)
may be possible to avoid the objections by rephrasing the question; but this may not be strictly
necessary.¹
The court should not order that irrelevant questions be answered just because they
are brief and will do little harm.²
The relevant parts of the transcript must be produced on such
an application about questions objected to. Sometimes records or afﬁdavits of records should be
produced to give the context. Probably a pre-trial ruling on the propriety of a question or answer
is only for disclosure purposes, and does not bind the trial judge.
The Court of Appeal held that the relevance and materiality of a document had not been
decided yet. As the plaintiff sought its production during an examination for discovery, the Court
of Appeal ordered a special chambers hearing in Court of King’s Bench to decide that, and gave
directions to let the plaintiff see a copy beforehand, so that argument would be ready before the
special chambers date, and no adjournment would be needed.³
Whether some issue pleaded is validly pleaded has to be left for the trial judge, and cannot be
decided on a R.5.25 motion.⁴
2. Remedy for Refusal to Answer
The usual remedy under R.5.25 is an order to reattend and answer the question not answered
and questions ﬂowing from it, not an entire second questioning.⁵
If a question is found proper and the witness did not answer it sufﬁciently or at all, usually the
form of the resulting order is as follows. The witness will reattend for a second questioning, at the
witness’s own expense, and without any further conduct money. The witness will pay the costs of
the application to compel answers and of the further discovery, in any event.⁶
On the new session,
the questioner may put the question not answered before (maybe rephrasing it a little), and may
thereafter ask questions arising from the answer given to the question which was in dispute on the
application.⁷
Therefore, even after 3 motions on disputed questions, the party questioning should
not ordinarily be relegated to written interrogatories.⁸
A case management judge ordered a defendant to answer questions which he had presumably
failed or refused to answer, even though that had been raised by the plaintiff’s former lawyer
before the judge had picked a trial date.⁹
Because of R.5.25, a trial judge should not draw an adverse inference from failure to answer
a question?¹⁰
The defendants had conceded liability, so the chambers judge did not dismiss the lawsuit,
but the plaintiff had to pay costs for failure to answer some undertakings and ﬁle a certiﬁcate of
readiness by a certain date as undertaken by counsel to the court. The main aim of contempt in
disclosure matters is inducing compliance.¹¹

5-110
R.5.26(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
D. Role of Counsel
It is highly improper for counsel for the witness to interrupt questions with comments, cues for
the witness, his own answers, or anything but a legally proper objection to the question.¹
Some
lawyers like to do such things to put the questioner off his stride, or to warn the witness. That is
unethical, and can attract very heavy personal costs.²
However, helping the witness ﬁnd the relevant document in a bulky ﬁle is not improper
coaching.³
Counsel should not walk out of the discovery just because he disagrees with a small number
of objections to his or her questions.⁴
E. Miscellaneous
Objections to questions based on the question’s breadth or precise wording do not permit
withholding any answer at all.⁵
A question can ask about previous accidents if the party questioning alleges many pre-existing
medical conditions and denies causing the plaintiff’s present problems.⁶
There is a discussion of the extent to which a witness may qualify his answers, especially
when he tells what information his company has.⁷
Even if the events are over a century old and the records are unclear, a party cannot answer
that it does not know what happened?⁸
Quaere.
Transcript of Oral Questioning
5.26(1) Oral questioning under this Part must be recorded word for word by a
person qualiﬁed to do so
(a) by a method that is capable of producing a written transcript, and
(b) in a manner agreed on by the parties or directed by the Court.⁹

5-112
R.5.27(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
A party cannot both object to answering questions as to which of a large number of
communications by him had been disclosed to others (in a defamation suit), and then later object
that his opponents’ pleadings fail to make it clear which communication is complained of.¹
One side refused to be examined for discovery unless real-time court reporting was used.
The other side objected that it was too slow and expensive. The party who did not want real-time
transcription would not have to pay the extra fee involved. And if that method consumed more
time, the objecting party could later show that, and ask for costs for that extra time. The witness
was ordered to attend without real-time reporting.²
One must constantly recall that in the current 2010 Rules, “questioning” is the name for at least
three very different processes.
Continuing Duty to Disclose
5.27(1) A person who is or has been questioned must, by afﬁdavit, correct an
answer if
(a) the answer was incorrect or misleading, or
(b) the answer becomes incorrect or misleading as a result of new
information.
(2) The correcting afﬁdavit must be made and served on each of the other parties
as soon as practicable after the person realizes that the answer was or has
become incorrect or misleading.

Related Provisions
5.10 (subsequently discovered documents) ; 5.38 (reporting change in expert opinion)

The Rules now expressly deal with correcting inaccurate answers,³
but there had long been a
judge-made duty to correct mistakes in them.⁴
Rule 5.27(1) calls on such a representative ofﬁcer
to correct the mistaken or misleading answer, or if it later becomes correct or misleading because
of new information. Correction is to be by afﬁdavit promptly served, thus preserving the oath and
avoiding ambush at trial. A party receiving such a correcting afﬁdavit can cross-examine on it. The
correcting afﬁdavit must be by the corporate ofﬁcer.⁵
A party whose careless error in answering misleads, may have to pay costs.⁶

5.28(1) Unless the Court otherwise orders or the parties otherwise agree, the
following rules apply with respect to written questions and the answers to them:
(a) the written questions must be numbered and succinct;

Acknowledgment of Corporate Witness’s Evidence
5.29(1) The evidence given by a corporate witness during questioning may not be
read in as evidence at trial unless a corporate representative of the corporation,
under oath, acknowledges that the evidence forms some of the information of
the corporation.
(2) Subject to subrule (3), the corporate representative may refuse to acknowledge
some or all of the evidence of the corporate witness and, if so, must state why,
but is not entitled to refuse to acknowledge the corporate witness’s information
just because the corporate representative disbelieves or disagrees with it.
(3) If the corporate representative disbelieves or disagrees with some or all of
the evidence of a corporate witness, the corporate representative
(a) must acknowledge the evidence as information of the corporation
unless it is inadmissible under the laws of evidence, and

Footnote
1 C. J. T. v. G. A. T.2019 ABQB 851, JCC 4801 126847 (Nov 7). Quaere.

5-114
R.5.29(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
(b) may then qualify the acknowledgment with further evidence that is
contrary to or inconsistent with the corporate witness’s evidence if
the further evidence is based on either or both of the following:
(i) the corporate representative’s personal knowledge;
(ii) a record prepared by the corporate representative or provided

Related Provisions
5.31 (reading in discovery evidence) ; 5.4(3) (evidence of corporate representative)

Examination for discovery under this Part of the Rules is very different from cross-examination
on afﬁdavit, and calling both “questioning” in the Rules is very confusing.¹
In Alberta, a corporation selects its own spokesperson²
for (examination for discovery)
questioning, and the court should not lightly take that away, except in cases of considerable
unnecessary inconvenience and expense.³
Letting someone else select the witness could impose
someone hostile to the corporation and so harm it severely.
There is a general review of this Rule.⁴
Rule 5.29 beneﬁts both sides and puts more information out in the open, encouraging
settlement. Alberta does not limit the number of employees to be examined, but allows for extra
costs.⁵
Only evidence by the corporate representative can be read in under R.5.31, not evidence
of mere employees (or ex-employees). Ordinarily a read-in from the examination for discovery
transcript of a non-party cannot be used in a motion or trial as evidence, even if it is from an
employee or ofﬁcer of a party (indeed even an opposing party), so long as their spokesperson for
discovery has not adopted, conﬁrmed, or agreed with it. Rule 5.29 bars use of this “at trial”, but
it does not say that it is permissible on a motion. Rule 5.31 allows “evidence” from “questioning”,
and says that it must be the evidence “of that other [adverse] party”. A party should not be stuck
with an admission by its employee, with no chance to qualify or explain it.⁶
This Rule provides
a procedure to have the corporate representative admit that the information of the employee is
information of the corporation, although the corporate representative can disagree with it.⁷

5-115
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.30(1)
The Court would not require a corporation to acknowledge as its information the previous
answers by an individual who was not its ofﬁcer or employee and worked for a sister company.¹
Ordinarily a corporate ofﬁcer for examination for discovery who has seen excerpts of
answers on examination for discovery from employees or former employees of his company has
to acknowledge that they are part of his company’s information, unless maybe the employee’s
English was poor, or there is some other rare circumstance.²
Ordinarily one could not ask a (non-party non-spokesperson) witness to read an entire
questioning transcript of some other witness and then see if it refreshed his memory, but if his
previous denial of all knowledge of something seems peculiar, that might be permissible.³
An objection about inadmissibility at trial may not be an answer to a demand to have the
corporation acknowledge answers by employees, especially if the case management judge feels
that such inadmissibility should be ruled on by the trial judge, not before trial.⁴
Undertakings
5.30(1) If, during questioning, a person answering questions
(a) does not know the answer to a question but would have known the
answer if the person had reasonably prepared for questioning, or if
as a corporate representative the person had reasonably informed
himself or herself, or
(b) has under the person’s control a relevant and material record that is
not privileged,
the person must undertake to inform himself or herself and provide an answer,
or produce the record, within a reasonable time.
(2) After the undertaking has been discharged, the person who gave the
undertaking may be questioned on the answer given or record provided.
Information Note
Undertakings should be answered in a reasonable time, and the witnesses should generally
not delay responding until every undertaking can be answered. The questioner is expected to
indicate within a reasonable time whether the undertaking is responsive to the question, and
whether the questioner intends to question further on the undertaking. Further questioning
should proceed when the undertakings are substantially answered, even if some undertakings
remain outstanding, and even if questioning is not completed.

for discovery for narrow purposes.

wants to disagree with answers previously given by the corporation’s employees. That needs an identiﬁed
factual basis, not subjective belief. And must be conﬁned to the topic of the original answers: Marathon Can.

5-116
R.5.30(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
A. General Rule
The 2010 Rules now for the ﬁrst time expressly mention the longstanding practice of
undertakings.¹
A party need not give an unreasonable undertaking; nor need he or she comply with an
undertaking already given, if the court does not think it reasonable.²
Even if events occurred
generations ago and the records are unclear, a party must undertake to answer questions about
them?³
One dictum suggests that making several people work for many hours to dig out answers,
might be unreasonable.⁴
Sed quaere.
The witness is not required to undertake to produce records or information which though
relevant or slightly connected, are not really material.⁵
But a witness can voluntarily give a
broader or unconditional undertaking, and will often gain beneﬁts from doing so, such as not
having to be examined a second time later.⁶
A party must do more than give permission to get a copy of a record; the party must produce
the actual record.⁷
This Rule is for examination for discovery under this Part only, not for cross-examination on
afﬁdavit, which is very different and has much narrower duty to provide (later) more information
which the witness does not know.⁸
B. Enforcement
Ordinarily undertakings given on discovery (questioning) should be answered without any
further demand, and if continuation of the examination for discovery is scheduled, then they
should be answered before that continuation.⁹
Where there is an outstanding undertaking, a promise to answer later is not an answer,
just another undertaking. A statement that the answer will be given at trial (with no particulars
beforehand) is a clear breach. Answers to undertakings cannot be given without prejudice, unless
privilege is also waived, even if the letter serves two purposes. Such privilege removes the ability
on discovery and at trial to question anyone about the responses. An answer to an undertaking
must do more than set the stage for more questioning.¹⁰
If undertakings are not fulﬁlled, they are enforced by applications rather like those to compel
answers to questions objected to.¹¹
Must the undertaking be answered before the ﬁnish of the

10 Hartson v. Park Paving 2021 ABQB 742, 35 Alta LR(7th) 176 (¶'s 48-53, 57-58).
11 Failure to obey the undertaking is also contempt of court: S. W. v. K. T. (#3) 2005 ABQB 298, 379 AR 320. The
motion must be after the end of the questioning (examination for discovery) ?

5-117
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.30(2)
questioning?¹
After a few months, unanswered undertakings can be the subject of an order to
answer.²
They are often answered by correspondence, but the questioner has the right to get them
under oath and to question on matters ﬂowing from such answers.³
Where there is an order to answer undertakings, but many months after the deadline in the
undertaking there is no answer to many of the undertakings, a chambers judge may strike out
the defaulter’s pleadings.⁴
In a similar case, on appeal the repentant litigant was given one more
chance, on stiff terms.⁵
An appeal is too late to complain ﬁrst about unanswered undertakings; the recipient should
move promptly in King’s Bench.⁶
When both parties signed and ﬁled a request to schedule a trial
date, stating that all questioning and undertakings had been conducted and discharged, that
ended any right to move to compel further answers, without leave of the court, which leave would
require some grounds.⁷
Questions were ordered answered, but only on condition of a conﬁdentiality order.⁸
A statement of claim was not struck out for the plaintiff’s counsel’s having answered questions
(instead of the witness answering) and given an undertaking on discovery, especially as the
defendant acquiesced, and there was no intent to conceal.⁹
Delay in answering undertakings and consequent need for several applications or appearances
of one form or another, led to an award of $7500 party-party costs, being about half the expenses
incurred by the other side.¹⁰
C. No Unilateral Withdrawal
A party who gave an undertaking cannot later add procedural conditions to it, such as security
for costs.¹¹

5-118
R.5.31(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
If the witness gives an undertaking to produce a paper or information, he or she must do so
or give sworn evidence of inability to do so; the witness cannot later unilaterally cut down his or
her own undertaking.¹
An undertaking can validly require preparation of a document.²
Though a party usually cannot
be required to create a document, if he or she can be asked for certain oral information and is
asked, but does not, he or she can be ordered later to give the information in writing.³
D. Miscellaneous
It may not be a sufﬁcient objection to questions on discovery, or proof that it was mere ﬁshing,
to show that 277 undertakings have been requested. Sometimes the two sides have a signiﬁcantly
different perspective on what is relevant and material and so for how to prepare for questioning
and what documents to produce.⁴
And sometimes the witness fails to prepare enough.
A host of modern decisions discuss when answering examination for discovery undertakings
starts time running afresh under R.4.33.⁵
An afﬁant cross-examined on his afﬁdavit need not inform himself unless he has previously
reviewed that information, or it would be very important and easy.⁶
One party questioned for discovery cannot require the counsel asking, to make a request for
an undertaking. The counsel questioning can examine in the way he or she thinks best.⁷
A transcript or its production includes any exhibits marked during the processing transcribed,
and answers to undertakings (whether or not marked as exhibits).⁸
Use of Transcript and Answers to Written Questions
5.31(1) Subject to rule 5.29 [Acknowledgment of corporate witness’s evidence],
a party may use in support of an application or proceeding or at trial as against
a party adverse in interest any of the evidence of that other party in a transcript
of questioning under rule 5.17 [People who can be questioned] or 5.18 [Persons
providing services to a corporation or partnership] and any of the evidence in
the answers of that other party to written questions under rule 5.28 [Written
questions].⁹
(2) Evidence referred to in subrule (1) is evidence only of the questioning party
who uses the transcript evidence or the answers to the written questions, and is
evidence only against the party who was questioned.

A. General
Note an article by Campion²
about read-ins from questioning transcripts, including putting
them in during the opening statement.
Most trial evidence includes some questions and answers from questioning (examination for
discovery) in that lawsuit.³
The same process can be used without any afﬁdavit,⁴
on applications or
other hearings in the suit.⁵
It is curious how many lawyers cross-examine on an afﬁdavit by a party
or employee pending a motion, when they might more safely question the deponent under Part 5.
This Rule is only about examinations for discovery, not about cross-examination on afﬁdavits,
nor examining a witness for a pending application. Use of a transcript depends entirely on the type
of procedure in which it was obtained.
B. Who Can Read In
Generally the only person who can read in a questioning (examination for discovery) answer
at trial is the party who asked the questions; the party answering cannot usually read in his
own answers.⁶
There is a common but slight exception. Usually at the opening of questioning
(examination for discoveries) in a multi-party suit, all counsel agree that any party adverse in

Footnote
1 Quite similar to previous 1968 R.214(4). For its history, see the historical note to present R.5.31(1).
2 (Dec ’06) 82 The Barrister 17 (Alta. Civil Trial Lawyers Assn. ).

Edmonton (City) v. Lovat Tunnel Equip.2000 ABQB 265, 265 AR 285.
4 Goldencare Hldg. v. Manu Chugh Architect 2006 ABQB 696, 58 Constr LR(3d) 67.
5 Murphy Oil Co. v. Predator Corp. (#_) 2006 ABCA 69, 384 AR 251. Since an amendment to pleadings
needs little evidence, an afﬁdavit exhibiting examination for discovery answers from a transcript sufﬁces:
323279 Alta. v.328766 Alta. (M 26 Apr ’04) JDE 0203 12469. But cf. Goldencare v. Manu Chugh, supra.
6 Waquan (Mikisew Cree F. N. ) v. R. (#1) 2002 ABCA 110, 303 AR 43; Barnes v. RBC Dom. Secur. (Alta
M 16 Nov ’05) JDE 9703 07929; MacDonald v.330932 Alta.2010 ABQB 689 (Nov 5) (¶’s 31-2). And if he
was examined for discovery only as a corporate ofﬁcer, his answers are not trial evidence against himself:
MacDonald v.330932. See the C. P. E. , Chapter 45, Part B.3. At trial an expert’s opinion was based on facts
provided independently, but also recited some extracts from his side’s discovery answers, to the same effect.
It was a technical slip which did no harm: Lowe v. Larue 2000 ABCA 28, 250 AR 220.

5-120
R.5.31(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
interest to the witness can read in the witness’s answers at trial, to avoid repetitive examinations.¹
But at trial each adverse party must then read in to get the beneﬁt.²
The true exceptions where a party can read in his own answers are narrow. Rule 5.31(3) says
that the court may direct other answers “so connected” to be used. The 2010 Rules do not change
the law there.³
There are fewer decided cases on that than one might expect.⁴
The test seems
fairly narrow. The witness cannot insist that all that he has said on the subject be read in. But he
can add to half an answer, or one which is misleading or out of context. If the party reading in one
answer does not like the addition but the judge allows it, the party reading in can withdraw both.⁵
If a person questioned is not available at trial, her evidence can be read in with leave of the
court.⁶
Previously her side could not read it in at trial, even if the witness was dead.⁷
A Pierringer (partial-settlement) agreement can somewhat cut down on party’s ability to read
in questioning answers at trial.⁸
C. What Can Be Read In
A party cannot read in an opponent’s answers without reading in the question, nor can one
read in part only of an answer.⁹
The utmost caution is needed in selecting questions to read in at trial. It is true that they do not
fully bind the party reading them in; he or she can lead evidence which contradicts them. However
answers which a party reads in become full evidence, but do not bind the trial judge or jury.¹⁰
They
become evidence for and against both the party reading in and the party whose answer is read in
(according to their content). So they might even lose the suit for the party reading them in.

1 Where there are multiple defendants with overlapping interests, questioning (examination for discovery) is
for the beneﬁt of all of them: Hartson v. Park Paving 2021 ABQB 742, 35 Alta LR(7th) 176 (¶ 44).

in Raywalt Constr. Co. v. Bencic 2005 ABQB 989, 386 AR 230 (¶’s 157-65), and 1159465 Alta. v. Adwood

7 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 45, Part B.3; Phillip v. Whitecourt Gen. Hosp.2003 ABQB 247, 337 AR 39. But on

Cdn. Nat. Ry. Corp.2021 ABQB 356, JCE 0503 17424 (May 5) (¶’s 19-51) (using newer common law tests
for hearsay of necessity and reliability).
8 Condo. Plan 9320022 v. Acta Gen.2009 ABQB 623; cf. Cdn. Nat. Res. v. Wood Grp. Mustang etc.2017
ABQB 106, JCC 1001 01541 (Feb 15) (¶’s 416-23).
9 Dix v. A. -G. Can.2002 ABQB 196.
10 Hamblin v. Ben 2004 ABCA 182; 581257 Alta. v. Aujla 2011 ABQB 39, 507 AR 315 (¶’s 10-12), revd 2013
ABCA 116, 542 AR 123; Verwoerd v. Verwoerd 2017 ABQB 272, JCL 1006 00975 (Apr 24) (¶’s 23-27) ; Re
Kozak Est.2018 ABQB 185, JCW ES12 35353. The law does not bar a fact ﬁnding at trial just because it
is inconsistent with a read-in. A read-in can sometimes be fatal to the party reading-in, but evidence and
admissions are not the same. Answers given on examination for discovery are presumed to be evidence
only, not admissions. Generally read-ins are simply evidence for the trial judge to weigh along with all the
other evidence. Like any evidence, a read-in can be contradicted or qualiﬁed by other evidence led by either
party, and the court may assess the read-in in the context of all of the evidence: Abt Est. v. Cold L. Ind. Park
GP 2019 ABCA 16, Edm 1803 0136 (¶’s 81-83).

5-121
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.31(3)
If an answer is partly helpful and partly harmful, it should not be read in, because of that
danger. It should be used instead as a foundation for trial cross-examination.¹
One cannot establish
inconsistency, and hence shake an opposing witness’ credibility, merely by reading in at trial some
of his discovery answers. , e. g. ridiculous or improbable answers. The reading-in tends to have the
opposite effect, making the read-ins part of the case of the party reading in.²
It is error to let a party read in at trial his or her questions to the opposing corporate party’s
employee or ofﬁcer, if she is not their spokesperson (representative) chosen by the corporate
party. The chosen representative would ﬁrst have to adopt those answers.³
Ordinarily a read-in
from the discovery transcript of a non-party cannot be used in a motion or trial as evidence, even
if it is from an employee or ofﬁcer of a party, indeed even an opposing party, so long as their
representative for discovery has not adopted it. Rule 5.29 bars use of this “at trial”, but it does not
say that it is permissible on a motion. Rule 5.31 allows “evidence” from “questioning”, and says
that it must be the evidence “of that other [adverse] party”. A party should not be stuck with an
admission by its employee, with no chance to qualify or explain it.⁴
Hearsay in an answer on questioning cannot be read in at trial unless it is an admission
against the interest of the witness or party questioned.⁵
D. Effect of Read-Ins
A discovery read-in is not conclusive evidence: it can be contradicted by other evidence, and
assessed by the trial judge.⁶
If a party gives two pieces of evidence at trial (live testimony and discovery evidence), and
the two give contradictory accounts of a critical event, they might cancel each other out and
leave no evidence, or they might be reconcilable (along with other evidence). But whether or not
evidence conﬂicts that way, no plaintiff is bound by discovery evidence which he or she adduces
in the sense that the fact ﬁnder must accept that evidence as accurate, reliable, and truthful. Even
if no other evidence contradicts it. Like all other types of evidence, discovery evidence may be
accepted in whole, in part, or not at all. Exculpatory discovery evidence need not be contradicted
directly; circumstantial evidence and inferences from it may sufﬁce.⁷
See also the cautionary paragraph in Part C above. The trial judge must read the extracts
which have been read in, in full, and in context.⁸
But the judge and jury cannot look at any questions or answers not read in.⁹

5-122
R.5.31(3) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Where the issue is whether something (such as a proposed amendment to pleadings) is
arguable, a contrary read-in does not constitute proof beyond all doubt, nor make the question no
longer arguable.¹
A read-in is not evidence against any party except the one answering on questioning (or the
party reading in at trial).²
There is logical value of an admission against interest of a party, if it is
used against that party. But a statement by A that B was negligent or did something illegal, has
little or no logical value, and may not even be hearsay, and the procedural problems or unfairness
with it are enormous. So it is important during questioning to try to get a given admission from each
opposing party, not just one of them.³
And so a company may delay answering until its relevant
ofﬁcials’ examinations are ﬁnished, to be sure that it has all its information in front of its designated
spokesperson. An insured’s questioning answer is not even evidence of his negligence against his
insurer in a suit under the Insurance Act against the insurer.⁴
The Court may limit the use which may be made of evidence got through disclosure in a class
action.⁵
Rule 5.13’s restrictions do not apply to who can use the transcript of a cross-examination on
afﬁdavit.⁶
A party to a civil suit who has to submit to questioning (examination for discovery) by the other
side, for the purpose of sec.13 of the Charter is deemed compelled to testify. If an answer he or
she gives is not incriminating and it is used at a criminal trial merely for impeachment purposes,
that does not trigger s.13 of the Charter. Sec.13 bars the use only of evidence which if admitted
at the criminal trial would prove or assist in proving one or more of the essential elements of the
offence charged.⁷
E. Other Procedure
If a party (usually a plaintiff) testiﬁes at trial before the time comes to read in his answers, he
should ﬁrst be given a chance in cross-examination to adopt them or explain them.⁸
For costs purposes, whether the length of examination for discovery was excessive cannot
be gauged only by seeing how many read-ins from discovery were made at trial. For one thing,
plaintiffs and defendants have different amounts of need to use read-ins, because a defendant
always has a chance to cross-examine the witnesses of the plaintiff at trial.⁹
Rule 5.31 does not codify all the possible uses (at trial) of an examination for discovery
(questioning) transcript, and does not cut down the right to use it for cross-examination, partly
because the Evidence Acts recognize that (common-law) right. Such cross-examination is
impeachment of credibility.¹⁰

Expression protected by s.2(b) of the Charter, and not inherently totally incompatible with
running a courthouse properly, includes broadcasting ofﬁcial audio recordings of hearings. But
Rules of Court or Practice Directions can put some limits on that for protecting the privacy of
litigants coming to court. Increasingly sophisticated journalistic technology does prove to have
dangers. The Rules here were reasonable.³
A prohibition on broadcasting what goes on in court was held to apply to the record of the

before trial which video was put into evidence at trial. Access to exhibits is a necessary corollary
