R.5.33(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The “transcript” to be ﬁled under R.6.7 is not deﬁned in the Rules, but included in it should be
any exhibits marked as such during the questioning in issue. That is true for all court purposes, not
just examination for discovery, or taking evidence on commission, or R.5.32. The transcript does
not include documents merely referred to during the questioning. But it would include documents
which counsel had agreed at or before the questioning would be part, or would be deemed to
have the effect of exhibits.¹
Conﬁdentiality and Use of Information
5.33(1) The information and records described in subrule (2) must be treated as
conﬁdential and may only be used by the recipient of the information or record
for the purpose of carrying on the action in which the information or record was
provided or disclosed unless
(a) the Court otherwise orders,
(b) the parties otherwise agree, or
(c) otherwise required or permitted by law.
(2) For the purposes of subrule (1) the information and records are:
(a) information provided or disclosed by one party to another in an
afﬁdavit served under this Division;
(b) information provided or disclosed by one party to another in a record
referred to in an afﬁdavit served under this Division;
(c) information recorded in a transcript of questioning made or in
answers to written questions given under this Division.

Related Provisions
5.32 (discovery transcripts not to be filed) ; 6.34 (sealing a court file)

A. History
This Rule now enacts the common-law “implied undertaking” not to use discovery evidence
except for carrying on the same action, and so the old case law applies.²
There was always an
implied undertaking by anyone (party or solicitor³
) who got disclosure⁴
of a record, not to use its
contents⁵
for any purpose⁶
except for that particular lawsuit.⁷

Footnote
1 McDonald v. Sproule Mgmt.2023 ABKB 587, JCC 1601 08624 (Oct 17) (¶’s 20-32).
2 P. L. v. R. , supra. This Rule in effect codiﬁes the undertaking: Olikowski v. Nano-Green Bioreﬁneries 2023
ABKB 441, JCE 1403 14906 (Jul 25) (¶’s 68-83).
3 Or a non-party allied to a party? Jutte v. Jutte 2007 ABQB 191, 415 AR 329.

5-125
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.33(2)
Though the Rule enacts the common law, it does not replace the common law and things
excepted by this Rule may still be subject to an “implied undertaking” at common law?¹
B. What Use Barred
Ordinarily that legal rule bars use of a transcript from questioning (examination for discovery)
in another lawsuit.²
But the implied undertaking does not bar use of a transcript of a questioning (examination for
discovery) for Law Society discipline purposes.³
And the information can be used in that same lawsuit, e. g. to get leave to amend its pleadings
without radically transforming them.⁴
And disclosure given expressly to identify the wrongdoers
can be used to sue them.⁵
There is probably no bar on use of information from discovery to expand the same lawsuit
in which it is obtained.⁶
For generations, one of the major uses of questioning (examination for
discovery) or discovery of records has been to amend the pleadings to add other particulars,
other causes of action, and other parties. It would be pointless (and harmful) to compel a second
suit, or to postpone the application to amend pleadings until the trial.
Rule 5.33 is violated if a discovery transcript is released to the police in an attempt to have
them charge the opposing party with perjury. The transcript can be used in court in the same suit
for later motions, but not for that. The party relying on it claimed this whole process was done to
get evidence for the court, but it was done to trap the opponent into a perjury trial. Under Rr.5.32
and 5.33, costs were assessed for this misconduct.⁷
[Footnote 7 continued from prior page]

5-126
R.5.33(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
C. What Information is Restricted
Court orders, afﬁdavits, and cross-examination transcripts ﬁled on a court ﬁle are not secret
(absent a sealing order), and using them in another suit is not a breach of any deemed undertaking
nor R.5.33. Nor are they usually compelled disclosure. If an afﬁdavit was served under that
Division of the Rules, leave should be given to use it. It would be ridiculous to hide evidence in
plain public sight.¹
However, that is only about conﬁdentiality. Often evidence in one suit is not
admissible in another later suit where the parties are different.²
It is very doubtful that the implied undertaking respecting discovery information restricts the
person giving the information.³
It is likely that information got through cross-examination on an afﬁdavit is not subject to any
undertaking as to collateral use, as R.5.33 is conﬁned to Division 1 of Part 5, and such cross-
examination is under Part 6; but the authorities are not clear.⁴
The implied undertaking ceases
to apply when questioning transcripts are made exhibits to afﬁdavits ﬁled on the public record.⁵
Whether there is an implied undertaking restricting use of information got through Crown
disclosure in a criminal prosecution is unclear.⁶
No legislation bars disclosing contents of
wiretaps (got to investigate possible crimes) to parties bringing a civil lawsuit over the same
matters. And the civil plaintiffs may be able to get disclosure of all the intercepts, on appropriate
conﬁdentiality conditions.⁷
Whether there is a similar restriction on use of information from other
legal proceedings is also unclear.⁸
No person is bound by the implied undertaking unless he or she is a party to it.⁹
The “implied undertaking” does not apply to documents which the government gets because
legislation or Regulations require that they be regularly ﬁled, by persons regulated by government
agencies.¹⁰

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.33(2)
An Anton Piller order should limit the use which the applicant plaintiff can make of the
documents seized. It was wrong to let them be used to pursue assets of the defendants in
unrelated matters outside this lawsuit, in other jurisdictions.¹
Anything broader than R.5.33 that
should be called to the judge’s attention and justiﬁed by special evidence and argument.
One should not evade this Rule by going somewhere else where another copy or version of
the information just got by discovery may be found, and using that other copy.²
But what if where
to ﬁnd such information was fairly obvious, it was not sealed or conﬁdential, and it would be found
anyway? What if the general information was well known? Must a party defer discovery until it has
done its full research?
D. Remedies for Breach
Breach of the implied undertaking (R.5.33) is contempt,³
and there can also be other remedies,
such as costs and striking out offending portions of transcripts,⁴
or striking out a new suit based on
the questioning answers.⁵
Or various sealing orders and restriction on custody or publication, may
be given.⁶
Pleadings should not be struck or use of the information banned, unless the breach of
R.5.33 harmed or disadvantaged the other party, so often a ﬁne is a more appropriate remedy.⁷
A second suit was struck out because the information to bring it was got solely from questioning
(examination for discovery) in an earlier suit, despite warnings about the “implied undertaking”
and the clarity of the law.⁸
E. Exceptions
Rule 5.33(1) has 3 express exceptions. Use for impugning credibility (e. g. cross-examination
on previous inconsistent exceptions) comes under subpara. (c). But evidence to make or negate
liability, needs leave under subparagraph (a). Criteria for the latter are described and authority
cited.⁹
The court may waive or modify that implied undertaking (Rule).¹⁰

5-128
R.5.33(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The implied undertaking (Rule) will be lifted by the court if the party wishing to use the
information gives strong reasons that the information would be relevant and material, that the
need for justice outweighs any privacy interests of the litigants in the other suit, and outweighs
the integrity of the discovery process.¹
Fraud or criminal wrongdoing would be a good ground to
lift the undertaking. Also relevant are whether the information could be got elsewhere, whether
the rights of non-parties are involved, and whether the old and new proceedings are connected.²
Leave was given to use information from other suits in this action to defend against accusations
of improper conduct in making grave allegations against many government lawyers. All had to do
with child welfare proceedings.³
But embarrassing one’s opponent, or inducing regulatory agencies to prosecute the opponent,
are not proper grounds to use the information or to get the “undertaking” lifted.⁴
The implied undertaking survives the end of the litigation. The implied undertaking is only
extinguished if the information is used in open court?⁵
F. Conﬁdentiality Orders
On request, courts will often make express conﬁdentiality orders accompanying disclosure.
For example, they may expressly forbid collateral use of the records disclosed, and may greatly
restrict the precise individuals who can see the record disclosed or be told their contents.⁶
Or may
bar publication of a voir dire in a jury trial.⁷
On the constitutionality of such a bar, see the Toronto
Star case.⁸
The terms of a conﬁdentiality order are given in one Ritter case.⁹
[Footnote 10 continued from prior page]
it need not do so just because the information could be got by discovery in the new suit: LSI v. Logani (#1),
supra (fair value of shares discovered used to start an oppression suit). The court should not relieve against
the undertaking even before the discovery occurs: Silver Springs v. UMA, supra. The court would not lift
the undertaking for a later suit after the ﬁrst suit was stayed and arbitrated, in Highland Produce v. Cdn.
Egg, supra. Waiver requires proof of prejudice created by the undertaking: A. M. v. Matthews, supra. Lifting
the implied undertaking Rule for discovery answers does not make them admissible in the other lawsuit, and
the usual Rules of admissibility and of the law of evidence generally still apply: Dreco Enr. Serv. v. Wenzel
Downhole Tools 2008 ABCA 36, 429 AR 57. See also the note 3 notes above.

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.34
But fear of losing government contracts was held no reason to use a pseudonym.¹
On holding hearings behind closed doors, or forbidding publication, see Rr.6.28 to 6.36, and

Conﬁdentiality orders are much better than barring discovery because of fear of loss of
conﬁdences.²
An order to seal a court ﬁle is apt when the ﬁle contains privileged information which could

G. Miscellaneous
There may be costs penalties for getting from a non-party, and keeping, papers which go
beyond the scope of a consent which he has given, especially thereby making him breach a
conﬁdentiality contract.⁴
After a lot of discovery in one suit, the plaintiffs got court leave under R.5.33 to use the
information got in a second related suit, in order to save money. When the other suit failed, the
defendant bank’s costs were held to include costs for its efforts about the discovery in the ﬁrst
suit.⁵
Law Society Rules do not bind the courts, but they also require counsel to tell his or her
clients only material facts. If a statement of claim for divorce and matrimonial property names
a transferee of assets as a defendant too, one counsel can act for all the defendants. The Law
Society Rule and R.5.33 do not necessarily clash, and freedom to choose counsel is important.
The Law Society Rule does not mandate barring common counsel for the defendants.⁶
DIVISION 2
EXPERTS AND EXPERT REPORTS
Service of Expert’s Report
5.34 An expert’s report must
(a) be in Form 25 and contain the information required by the form, or
any modiﬁcation agreed on by the parties, and
(b) be served in the sequence required by rule 5.35 [Sequence of
exchange of expert reports].
Information Note
The court clerk cannot schedule a trial date under rule 8.4 [Trial date: scheduled by court
clerk] unless expert reports, if needed, have been exchanged. See also rule 8.5 [Trial date:
scheduled by the Court], which provides for a trial date to be scheduled by the Court.

Related Provisions

A. References
This annotation is mostly about the law. For full practical advice about choosing, hiring,
and instructing experts (your own, or about your opponent’s problems), see Côté, Systematic
Advocacy, Chap.3 (Juriliber 2017).
Generally on experts, see the Ferguson book.¹
On improper expert evidence, see R.5.36n.
On how independent or partisan an expert witness may or must be, and the role of counsel,
see R.5.36n. On court-appointed experts, see R.6.40. There are general discussions of expert
evidence.²
B. General
1. Need for Experts
The fundamental rule is that usually opinion evidence is not admissible, but the opinions of
qualiﬁed experts are an exception.³
It used to be thought that many types of claim would be
impossible without expert evidence, but that assumption now seems too broad.⁴
If “facts” are uninformative without technical or scientiﬁc opinions, a judge cannot draw
inferences about what they mean without qualiﬁed expert evidence.⁵
Therefore, if a party gets

Footnote
1 Ferguson, Ontario Courtroom Procedure 2009, Chap.41 (2007). The 4th ed.2016 (same chapter number) is
edited by Fuerst & Sanderson. Note on the subject of experts generally, C. P. E. , Chapter 46, Part K; Abrams
and McGuinness, Canadian Civil Procedure Law, §§ 16.231-16.251 (2d ed.2010) ; there is also a 2nd
ed.2010; Hollander, Expert Witnesses in Civil Litigation (Irwin 2017) ; Anderson, Expert Evidence (2nd ed.
LexisNexis 2009).
2 For a discussion of expert evidence re human behavior, and whether it may use hearsay, see the Burns case:
R. v. Burns [1994] 1 SCR 656, 666, 165 NR 374. In a criminal case, the accused was able to call a string
of witnesses to say that the complainant had falsely accused teachers of various kinds of assault. This was
not barred by the collateral evidence rule, as it formed part of the foundation for the opinion of a defence
expert, and so was relevant to a material issue: R. v. C. M. E.2001 ABQB 1087, 305 AR 182; R. v. Mohan
[1994] 2 SCR 9, 166 NR 245; Hovsepian v. Westfair Foods 2003 ABQB 641, 341 AR 1; Guar. RV Centre
v. Schmidt 2007 ABCA 89, 404 AR 26. On whether any kind of expert evidence is permissible on certain
topics, and what the trial judge can take judicial notice of, the court of appeal defers to the trial judge: R.

14, 372 NR 239, but Court of Appeal was revd other grds and King’s Bench restd 2008 SCC 14, 372 NR 239.
Discussion of what threshold test expert evidence has to meet before it can be received as trial evidence.
Brief necessary mentions of topics that are not strictly for the expert to decide need not be removed from his
report: Marathon Can. v. Enron Can. Corp.2008 ABQB 408, 447 AR 46 (¶’s 38-79).
3 Walter v. W. Hockey League (#1) 2016 ABQB 588, 2016 CarswellAlta 2024 (Oct 20).
4 If a trial judge is satisﬁed by lay evidence (friends and relatives) that an accident produced serious
psychological effects to the plaintiff, neither expert evidence nor an identiﬁed psychiatric illness is necessary
to ﬁnd the negligent defendant liable, and to give signiﬁcant damages. Precise deﬁnition or diagnosis is not
needed, as the issue is compensation, not treatment: Saadati v. Moorhead 2017 SCC 28, [2017] 1 SCR
543.

5-131
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.34
from an independent expert information which it previously had received from partial lay people,
that conﬁrmation, however informal, may advance the lawsuit.¹
A trial judge need not entirely accept the evidence of any of the experts, so long as he or she
does not misconstrue the evidence.²
It does not matter whether an expert witness at trial relied on
inadmissible evidence (such as the questioning answers of his own client), if the underlying facts
were independently proved at the trial.³
There are dangers in devaluing an expert’s use of the technical language of his or her
profession, if he or she is not simply hiding behind that terminology.⁴
It is also dangerous to
devalue an expert’s evidence because he became exasperated after 4 days of repetitive cross-
examination by a self-represented litigant (accused) with no relevant training.⁵
If a witness on one
side is devalued for such things though the opposing witnesses are accepted and not criticized
though they did much the same, that can help create a reasonable apprehension of bias by the
trial judge.⁶
Expert evidence about which lay witnesses to believe, and how to assess their evidence, is
unnecessary. Trial judges already know that. Such evidence is not admissible. And a trial judge’s
decision to reject it gets appellate deference. Especially if the evidence is at best of marginal
probative value (e. g. on a minor point).⁷
Generally an expert’s evidence should not draw legal conclusions or engage in legal analysis.⁸
The law of other countries is presumed the same as our law, unless a qualiﬁed expert testiﬁes to
what the foreign law is. But the Judicature Act s.12 lets an Alberta court take judicial notice of any
Canadian province or territory, without expert evidence.⁹
2. Non-Expert Opinions
Opinion evidence from someone not qualiﬁed to give it, has no worth as evidence,¹⁰
even if
backed up by a letter of opinion by a possible expert, which letter is not sworn.¹¹
An expert must
be qualiﬁed, and his or her testimony outside his or her area of expertise should be prevented.¹²

R.5.34 PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
3. Tests for Expert Evidence
The Supreme Court’s new tests for admitting expert evidence are reviewed. First (sometimes
called logical relevance) come relevance, necessity, no exclusionary rule, and proper qualiﬁcations.
After that (sometimes called legal relevance), come balancing potential beneﬁts and risks: a
cost-beneﬁt analysis. The second stage is discretionary, not a bright line. Whether the witness’
experience is sufﬁcient is a decision hard to upset on appeal. Undue prejudice depends in part on
whether the witness was too emphatic or rigid in his conclusions.¹
The trial judge may be able to
call for more expert evidence after the trial judge has reserved judgment at the end of the trial?²
Expert evidence about Canadian law is not admissible.³
In weighing the pros and cons of admitting expert evidence, it is relevant that the only part of
the expert’s opinion which the trial judge can understand, is its conclusion.⁴
A preliminary voir dire of the expert’s competence and suitability, and on what topics he or
she may give opinion evidence, should be held (including cross-examination on that evidence of
competence) and decided ﬁrst, before the expert gives the content of his or her opinion. Mingling
both topics into one hearing with cross-examination on the merits of the opinion before the
evidence in chief from the expert has been heard, often creates a false impression and creates a
number of other evils, including giving the trial judge an impossible task.⁵
Where a ground of appeal is error in admitting expert evidence, articulating and applying the
proper legal test is a question of law reviewable on the correctness standard. Qualiﬁcations and
probative value generally get appellate deference, if the trial judge undertook the gatekeeping
function to see if the evidence was admissible. So the standard of review is generally deferential.⁶
The two-stage trial process of ﬁrst deciding an expert’s qualiﬁcations before receiving his or
her evidence, is a good practice, but no Rule requires it for afﬁdavits ﬁled for an interlocutory
application, and the chambers judge should not require that of his or her own motion if no party
raises it, especially if the application and the afﬁdavit make the expertise and the opinion plain.⁷
C. Assumptions Must Accord With Facts Found
Expert evidence which is not supported by any evidence of the important facts assumed by
the expert has no weight, if the expert does not personally know those facts.⁸
The fact that a
critical fact assumption on which an expert economist did a report is not made out (and is contrary
to the evidence) does not necessarily mean that the evidence of an economist is worthless. She
may give a proper method for computing damages, so if later the judge is satisﬁed of different raw

Therefore factual support is not exactly a question of simple inadmissibility. It is a question of
conditional relevance.
Experts can usually rely on hearsay information of a type traditionally used by their profession.
But not if it was too tendentiously gathered to be a real survey.²
Of course the expert can rely on
facts which he or she has personally observed.³
D. Independence
See also R.5.36n, and Part B.3 above.
Not everyone who is paid or employed by the prosecution or by one of the parties so lacks
independence that his or her opinion evidence is inadmissible. It takes clear unwillingness or
inability to be independent, itself to exclude. Otherwise the degree of dependence just goes into
the overall weighing of costs and beneﬁts of receiving the evidence.⁴
See further R.5.36.
It is preferable that experts not be briefed jointly, but they can read each other’s reports and
(absent an order to the contrary), hear each other testify in court.⁵
The law wisely does not require that an expert witness go all the way to the witness stand
without any explanations or information about the legal process. And requiring that that explanation
be done formally in writing, would be very slow. Though litigation privilege attaching to experts
is qualiﬁed and it cannot be used to shield improper conduct, it takes evidence to support a
reasonable suspicion that counsel improperly inﬂuenced the expert, not a mere demand or
accusation.⁶
Independence need not be tested solely by looking for a ﬁnancial interest in the witness;
evidence upholding his or her own work may be inﬂuenced by pride in his or her own work.⁷
How far counsel can affect or interfere with the opinion which the expert gives is reviewed.⁸
E. What Is and Is Not Expert Evidence
The case law for this area seems to conﬂict to some degree.
There is a distinction between an opinion formed in order to treat the patient and one formed in
order to testify at trial.⁹
Therefore the opposing party (one whom the patient is suing) can force the
treating physician to testify.¹⁰
A party who calls a professional as a witness cannot avoid the expert

Rules by conﬁning the evidence to personal observations and avoiding overt opinion evidence.
He or she observes as an expert, and recounting the observation indirectly (or even directly)
expresses an expert opinion?¹
But the trial judge can let the expert testify without compliance
with the expert Rules, especially if copies of his notes were provided earlier, and no adjournment
was sought.²
Identifying a transcript of telephone calls and identifying what is said, is not opinion
evidence under the expert rules or their criminal equivalent.³
If someone with expertise was
involved in the events (such as the family physician), he or she probably gives facts and opinion,
and it is usually prudent formally to qualify him or her as an expert witness, especially if the
witness proposes to give opinions on topics on which he or she was not involved with ﬁrst-hand
knowledge.⁴
Sometimes someone with expertise can testify without going through the procedure to qualify
her as an expert. Someone involved personally in the underlying events because of her expertise,
can be allowed to explain why she acted as she did, even if she strays into her expertise.⁵
One cannot call one reluctant physician, who will not offer an opinion, as a “non-expert witness”,
to get into evidence reports from other physicians (on this witness’ ﬁle), which other physicians do
not testify; expert reports need ﬁrst-hand evidence and an opportunity to cross-examine.⁶
An exception to the rule against opinion evidence, allows what is on the fuzzy border between
fact and opinion, where a seeming opinion is given as a shorthand way of expressing a number
of small facts lying behind a conclusion, e. g. that Smith was drunk. The lay witness has to be in
a better position than the trial judge, it is one ordinary non-experts can form, this witness has
experience enough, and the underlying facts are too subtle or complicated to narrate effectively
without stating a conclusion.⁷
The Court of Appeal owes a trial judge deference on whether to admit old medical reports or
documents, especially if no live evidence is proposed, and no Form 25 notices had been given for
these reports.⁸
In 3 cases no cross-examination would be possible, and in another instance the
author of the report was not put forward as an expert, and he was being called as a lay witness.
An afﬁdavit explaining and backing up a claim of privilege is not subject to exclusion or expert-
report procedures, just because it expresses an opinion or conclusion, especially as it is doing
what R.5.8 requires.⁹

Where there is no suggestion that witnesses have special expertise, former employees can
testify about how a plant was operated, and with what restraints, and draw common-sense
inferences from those facts. But not conclusions about the capacity of the plant (a big issue in the
suit).¹
A constable could testify that a scent he detected was pepper spray, under the “recognition”
category of opinion evidence by a lay person with personal experience.²
And a certiﬁed examiner under the Minor Injury Regulation is not the same as an expert hired
by counsel.³
The expert Rules do not apply to opinion evidence to the court from an inspector whom the
court has appointed under the Business Corporations Act.⁴
The Legislature can set up a special litigation method for “minor injuries” and can let Regulations
deﬁne that term and set a special procedure to see whether a given case is one. That a plaintiff
gets a medical opinion that it is not minor, is not enough to bypass all that. The defendant can
insist on the legislated assessment scheme.⁵
If proper steps (or any evidence) to establish an afﬁant’s expertise have not been taken, and
his afﬁdavit recites both facts and opinions, the court may receive the factual passages and
exclude from evidence the opinions expressed.⁶
F. Miscellaneous
Usually it is the trial judge who should decide if expert evidence tendered meets the tests for
admissibility. Only in the clearest of cases where an “expert” report does not meet some of the
four threshold criteria in the Supreme Court of Canada’s Mohan case, should some other judge
in chambers rule before trial on that, and even then only to increase trial efﬁciency and not impair
trial fairness.⁷
Expert reports got by solicitors for purposes of litigation are privileged, though R.5.35
legislates an exception for those reports on which the party intends to rely.⁸
A plaintiff serving on
the defendant its medical expert reports does not have to produce raw data or test protocols relied
on by those experts, if they are privileged. Until the witness testiﬁes at trial, that privilege remains.⁹
Especially if the defendants do not show that that material is crucial. Cases on when medical
reports need be disclosed do not go that far. Raw material is not “ﬁndings”.¹⁰
Voluntary production
to the other side in a personal injury suit of an expert report does not waive litigation privilege for
the ﬁle or background papers of the expert.¹¹

R.5.34 PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Whether there is implied waiver of litigation privilege where the privileged material was used
and relied on by an expert witness who is to testify, is complicated by overlap with the Rules of
Court about giving notice of expert reports. The report has the privilege waived by giving notice
of it under Rules 5.34 and 5.35. Not all preliminary discussions and communications and drafts
should be producible. Keeping prelimiary ideas oral is not desirable. But one part of the expert’s
ﬁle often is records given to the expert which then forms the foundation for his or her report.
The Alberta case law is not clear, though R.5.37 would tend to suggest producibility. That was
voluntary intent to waive privilege, and also implied waiver through fairness.¹
For three proposed trial witnesses from the medical profession or related professions, the
Form 25 provided did not specify the expert reports which were to be attached. And documents
attached included one dated later than the Form 25! So the judge had certain attachments
removed from those Form 25s.²
A prisoner got the right to consult an outside medical doctor of his choice, partly to get evidence
for a lawsuit, without exhausting or even attempting his internal avenues of request.³
Failure to serve a report can be excused where the other side has long been familiar with the
report.⁴
But generally on late notice, see R.5.35n. below.
Whether expert evidence is barred if it is on the “ultimate issue” is doubtful, but the judge may
well hold that the opinion is not quite about the ultimate issue.⁵
How to prepare experts’ reports for a motion and how the court should weigh them when
they compete, are discussed.⁶
For breach of Rr.5.34 and 5.35, an expert’s report exhibited to an
afﬁdavit by the expert, was excluded from evidence for an interlocutory motion.⁷
Expert witnesses are subject only to Division 2, not to the general discovery Rules.⁸
As the date for a trial approaches, giving one party an adjournment so it could present better
evidence at trial, would not be fair, nor result in a just resolution. Still less would adjourning a trial
already under way to correct an evidentiary issue. Courts could not then operate in a timely or
cost-effective manner. Mid-trial adjournments would have a catastrophic effect on all trials relying
on expert evidence.⁹
Shortly before an oft-adjourned trial, the plaintiffs and some of the defendants in a medical
negligence suit settled, so those defendants were no longer parties. The plaintiffs had an expert
report which they would probably have led at a full trial, and the remaining defendants (not settling)
would have relied on that evidence. Now the plaintiffs stated that the division of liability no longer
concerned them, so they would not call that expert to testify. The remaining defendants objected,
but a judge refused to adjourn the trial again, and held that he could not force any party to call
certain evidence at trial, nor force anyone else to provide evidence to a party.¹⁰

5-137
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.35(1)
Sequence of Exchange of Experts’ Reports
5.35(1) If a party intends to use the evidence of an expert at trial, the expert’s
report must be served in the sequence described in subrule (2).
(2) Unless the parties otherwise agree or the Court otherwise orders, experts’
reports on which a party intends to rely must be served in the following sequence:
(a) the party who bears the primary onus of proof must serve on each of
the other parties the report of that party’s expert;
(b) the other party or parties must serve their expert’s rebuttal report,
if any, and may include in the report issues not raised in the initial
expert’s report;
(c) the party who served the initial expert’s report may serve a surrebuttal
expert’s report that responds only to the new issues raised in the
rebuttal report.

Related Provisions
5.44(3) (exchanging reports of medical examinations) ; 6.41 (court appointed experts) ; 8.4(3)

(c) (exchange of reports before trial) ; 8.5 (trial scheduled by Court)
A. Sequence
The Rules no longer provide¹
a detailed timeline for serving expert reports before trial.
Rule 5.35 was rewritten because the Law Reform Institute recommended that expert reports
be exchanged sequentially, not simultaneously, explaining why they preferred that. That made
necessary provision for rebuttals to rebuttals (called “surrebuttals”).²
Early in a suit before questioning, the plaintiff ﬁled an expert report, and then demanded
that the defendants either ﬁle a rebuttal or let the plaintiff’s report go in as evidence, though the
defendants were not ready yet to produce such evidence. This was improper. The court instead
extended the time for replies, provided that the defendant produce his report at least 60 days
before trial began.³
Parts of a report were admitted without live evidence in the Plews case.⁴
Disclosure in either direction must be passed on by a party to his subrogee.⁵
Rules 5.35 and 5.44(3) can work together, and there is no gap in the Rules. Exchange of
reports under R.5.35 can be insisted upon without waiting for a medical examination by the
defendant of the injured plaintiff. And a medical examination can then be conducted after the
exchange of reports under R.5.35. One cannot assume that the medical doctor conducting the
inspection will lose all objectivity and honesty on seeing the other side’s report.⁶

Uned 153 (Feb 22). See also the Signalta case, supra.

3 Jeerh v. Yorkton Secur.2004 ABQB 959, 362 AR 1, leave den 2005 ABCA 64, 363 AR 333.

5-138
R.5.35(2) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
B. Privilege
Notice of expert evidence is another type of disclosure of records, preventing surprise expert
witnesses at trial. However, unlike R.5.44(3), this Rule does not much impinge on privilege.¹
One
need not disclose the evidence or existence of an expert whom one consulted but does not plan to
call at trial.²
It is not completely clear what happens if a party genuinely does not intend or propose
to call a certain expert, and then (maybe taken by surprise), during trial changes his or her mind
and decides to call the expert. The complex overlap between ordinary principles of privilege, and
Rr.5.34 and 5.35, is discussed.³
These Rules do not require a party to call at trial an expert witness of whose evidence he has
given notice, and do not require the trial judge to draw an adverse inference from not calling that
witness.⁴
C. Reports
The Rule does not require an expert report; it merely requires disclosure of any reports which
are to be adduced at trial. If the expert is to testify but not adduce a report, what must be disclosed
is the expert’s name, qualiﬁcations, and the substance of his or her opinion in accordance with
Form 25.⁵
The Rules do not require that reports be sworn or veriﬁed by afﬁdavit, if they conform to Form
25, but they must be in a form permitting cross-examination. So a report of one doctor merely
exhibited to the afﬁdavit of a different doctor, will not sufﬁce. If no cross-examination is sought, the
court may consider the Form 25 report. If one side gets the report and does not ﬁle or rely on it,
the other side cannot use it in court.⁶
D. What to Disclose
Defendants gave notice of expert evidence, but not of the factual evidence underlying the
opinion. That came out only at trial, and the expert of the plaintiff was then able to prepare a late
rebuttal report. It was the duty of the defendants to disclose such material. Additional work, and
duplication of work, led to solicitor-client (full) costs of such work by his expert, and part of the
costs for extra work done on an emergency basis.⁷
The Wade case discusses generally disclosure of expert reports, surprise, and advance
notice of objections to expert evidence.⁸
On how much to disclose and the difference between
(late) primary reports and rebuttal reports, see the C. P. E.⁹
Rule 5.35 may interact with whether
there should be a new trial because rebuttal expert evidence was received at the ﬁrst trial which
was not strictly proper.¹⁰

PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.35(2)
Where an expert’s report merely criticizes the report of another, the expert will not be allowed
to give wider testimony about the issues at the trial.¹
E. Breach of Rule
Where R.5.35 is broken, the trial judge has a choice, but most often the evidence is excluded,²
especially if the suit has already suffered too much delay,³
or it would be necessary to adjourn
(postpone) the upcoming application or trial.⁴
Whether to extend the time to comply, or excuse
non-compliance, depends on the amount of delay, the explanation for it, and amount of actual (not
merely potential) prejudice caused to the opponent and to other litigants.⁵
An article discusses the
extent to which an expert can testify about details and matters not covered in the formal expert
statement.⁶
An expert’s report exhibited to an afﬁdavit by the expert, was excluded from evidence
for an interlocutory motion, for breach of Rr.5.34 and 5.35.⁷
Rule 8.4(3) (c) provides that the parties cannot request a trial date until expert reports have
been exchanged. That is serious, and refers to all expert reports contemplated, not merely those
existing. If they are in fact not ready, the lawyers are guilty of misconduct, and may well lose
costs.⁸
The certiﬁcate that the litigation is ready, under R.8.4(3) (c) (form 37) (triggered by R.
8.5(1) (c) and Form 38) precludes a later “surrebuttal” report by an existing expert witness.⁹
And
delay in preparing and serving it was an additional reason to exclude it, as prompt disclosure of
reports is needed to prevent surprise and promote settlement.¹⁰
Furthermore, this report seemed
to go beyond rebutting and introduce new information, and it seemed to opine on legal topics.¹¹
To decide whether to admit or exclude the report late, the trial judge had to read the report, but
judges have to do that all the time.¹²
If the evidence is not excluded, then an adjournment and costs may be involved.¹³
F. Rebuttal
Rule 5.35(2) (c) is not limited to alternative theories, and covers also a new issue, much like
an analogy to the process followed for rebuttal evidence at trial. So here also (experts’ reports), a
party should not split disclosure of its case, nor merely conﬁrm or reinforce earlier evidence given
in its original expert reports, though the precise boundary can be delicate at times. A big factor is
what could be foreseen at the time.¹⁴

1 R. K. C. v. G. S. A.2012 ABQB 614, 71 Alta LR(5th) 41.
2 See the C. P. E. , Chapter 46, Part K.6.
3 Gayton v. Rinholm 2012 ABQB 232, [2012] AR Uned 282 (Apr 13) ; Hess v. Iqaluit (Town) 2012 NWTCA
8, 524 AR 197.
4 Ingram v. Chief Med. Ofﬁcer of Health 2022 ABQB 137, JCC 2001 14300 (Feb 15) (¶'s 3 ff. ). It was
especially bad when a prosecutor got a rebuttal medical report which impaired the competence and reliability

defence expert had ﬁnished. Letting in the new evidence made the trial unfair and a new trial was necessary:

5-140
R.5.36(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
A defendant cannot lead expert evidence at trial not in his pre-trial expert’s report or summary,
by cross-examining the plaintiff’s expert on a topic not in the defendant’s expert report, then
having the defendant’s expert testify on that topic at trial on the theory that is a new development
or rebuttal. Otherwise a defendant could reveal very little before trial and then lead what expert
evidence he wanted, by ﬁrst cross-examining on the new topics.¹
G. Miscellaneous
On experts not testifying as to an opinion, see R.5.34 n. E.
A notice to admit should not be used to bypass the Rules on expert witnesses and when they
have to be called in person, nor should a notice to admit be directed toward proving agreements
between counsel as to how the trial is to be run.²
Rule 3.36 prevails, so a defendant noted in default gets no notice, even of expert reports or
reports for an upcoming assessment.³
A case management judge refused to order a meeting between opposing experts in an attempt
to aid a settlement. Rule 4.14(1) expressly puts this topic within the discretion and mandate of a
case management judge and it is entitled to considerable deference on appeal because the case
management judge knows the litigation intimately.⁴
When lack of notice of expert evidence required a long adjournment of a case, the opposing
party got costs thrown away, but not all trial preparation costs. Some preparation would be
necessary anyway, but some would need refreshing, and some would need amendment.⁵
Objection to Expert’s Report
5.36(1) A party who receives an expert’s report must notify the party serving the
report of
(a) any objection to the admissibility of the expert’s report that the party
receiving the report intends to raise at trial, and
(b) the reasons for the objection.
(2) No objection to the admissibility of an expert’s report is permitted at trial
unless
(a) reasonable notice of the objection was given to the other party, or
(b) the Court permits the objection to be made.⁶

Related Provisions
6.37 (notice to admit)

5-141
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.37(1)
See also R.5.34nnB.3 and D, on objectivity.
Rule 5.36 requires prompt advance notice of any objection to admissibility of an expert report;
the day before trial is too late, and worse is notice received during the trial.¹
Generally on advance objections to expert evidence, see the Wade case. The trial judge must
be careful to delineate an expert witness’ area of expertise, and opinion evidence going outside
it is inadmissible.²
A trial court can and should remove from an expert witness report conclusions prejudicial to
one party but based on speculation on having no basis in the evidence, especially if they are close
to the ultimate issue.³
The threshold for expert evidence is fairness, objectivity, and non-partisanship; trial judges can
exclude their evidence for want of that. But it is a fairly low threshold. Beyond that, independence
or impartiality goes only to weighing cost vs. beneﬁts of admitting the evidence. Relationship
with a party is relevant only if it makes the expert unable or unwilling to be fair, non-partisan and
objective. Apparent bias is irrelevant. Semble, conﬂict of interest is not the test.⁴
An expert need not be totally independent for the court to hear his evidence; this goes to
weight.⁵
But some expert witnesses are hired guns and very partisan, so a witness not connected
with any party may be more objective.⁶
Witnesses with expertise may have differing degrees of
independence. Those not previously involved in the matter and retained to give opinions must
satisfy all of the Mohan procedures, and advance notice must be given, in order to give their
opinions. They have to be independent.⁷
Can a report by a medical expert be excluded because he is not familiar with Alberta practice?⁸
Expert witnesses are professional advisers; a medical consultant for the Workers’ Compensation
Board is similar. Once he or she is qualiﬁed to testify and gives an opinion, he or she cannot be
sued by someone other than his or her client on the basis that the opinion was incorrect or even
negligent. And the duties of a witness limit the extent to which his or her own client can sue the
expert. In the right circumstances the same immunity or protection applies even if the forum for
the opinion evidence is an expert tribunal, not a court.⁹
Questioning Experts Before Trial
5.37(1) The parties may agree, or in exceptional circumstances the Court may
direct, that an expert be questioned by any party adverse in interest to the party
proposing to call the expert witness at trial.
(2) The questioning must be limited to the expert’s report.

Since the expert report is protected by litigation privilege, an examination of the expert should
not be allowed until the privilege has been waived, e. g. by service of the report or other indication

is doubtful, and the court may impose terms on doing so.⁴

Continuing Obligation on Expert
5.38 If, after an expert’s report has been provided by one party to another, the
expert changes his or her opinion on a matter in the report, the change of opinion
must be
(a) disclosed by the expert in writing, and
(b) immediately served on each of the other parties.

5-143
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.39(1)

Related Provisions
5.10 (records discovered later) ; 5.27 (continuing duty to disclose)

This Rule was used to exclude from trial evidence a new possible explanation for a health
incident. That expert witness had had 12 years to think about causation, and had given two written
opinions that he could not determine the cause. On the eve of trial he developed a new theory.
A mere vague email to the other side was not enough notice. It would prejudice the opposing
plaintiffs to admit this new opinion at trial.¹
Use of Expert’s Report at Trial Without Expert
5.39(1) A party serving an expert’s report may, at the same time, also serve
notice of intention to have the report entered as evidence without calling the
expert as a witness.
(2) If a party serves a notice of intention under subrule (1), no objection may be
made at trial to entering the expert’s report as evidence unless, within 2 months
after service of the notice under subrule (1), any other party serves a statement
on the party serving the notice of intention
(a) setting out all or parts of the report that that other party objects to
being entered as evidence under this rule, and giving reasons for the
objection, or
(b) serving on the party a request that the expert attend the trial for
cross-examination.
(3) Agreeing to have the expert’s report entered as evidence without calling the
expert as a witness, either explicitly or by allowing subrule (2) to operate without
objection, is not an admission of the truth or correctness of the expert’s report.²

Related Provisions
5.40 (requiring attendance of expert) ; 8.15 (witnesses not to be called at trial) ; 8.17 (proving

facts at trial)
On adverse inferences from failure to testify, see the commentary on R.8.15 below.
On proving facts at trial by afﬁdavit, see the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia.³
Formal admissions in litigation are deemed conclusive on the point admitted, whereas informal
admissions can be overcome by other evidence. Where the defendant admitted certain reports at
the request of the plaintiff, the plaintiff could not later challenge the conclusions in them, although
the weight to be given to the admitted opinion was for the trial judge.⁴

5-144
R.5.40(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Expert reports should be put on the court ﬁle only by consent or with leave of the court, and if
they are ﬁled without that, they can be taken off the court ﬁle.¹
Expert’s Attendance at Trial
5.40(1) A party who agrees to have all of an expert’s report entered in evidence
at trial, either explicitly or by allowing rule 5.39(2) [Use of expert’s report at trial
without expert] to operate without objection, may, at the same time as responding
to the notice of intention, serve a request that the expert be in attendance at trial
for cross-examination.
(2) The expert whose entire report is to be entered at trial must not give oral
evidence at trial unless
(a) a request that the expert attend for cross-examination has been
served, or
(b) the Court permits.
(3) The party who requests an expert’s attendance for cross-examination must
pay the costs of the expert’s attendance, determined under Schedule B [Court
Fees and Witness and Other Allowances], unless the Court is satisﬁed that the
cross-examination is of sufﬁcient assistance to warrant a different order about
who is to pay those costs.
(4) If the party proposing to enter the expert’s report receives a request that the
expert attend for cross-examination, the party proposing to enter the report may
question the expert at trial.²

Where parties’ experts prepare expert reports which are not designed to be free standing and
are not under oath, and there is not time to revise them, the case management judge should not
(of his own motion) bar evidence in chief at trial by the experts (substituting the reports), and say
that only cross-examination would be live.³
A trial judge may excuse lack of compliance with the
Rules and hear expert evidence, when no adjournment is sought at trial, no particular prejudice
is shown, and there is some advance chance to examine the data upon which this new opinion
was based.⁴
But where there is no attempt to comply with the Rules, the trial judge may exclude
opinion evidence.⁵
Or the judge may adjourn the trial for want of long enough notice, and make the

DIVISION 3
MEDICAL EXAMINATIONS BY HEALTH CARE
PROFESSIONALS
Medical Examinations
5.41(1) The parties may agree that the mental or physical condition of a person
is at issue in an action and agree on a health care professional to conduct a
medical examination.
(2) On application, the Court may in an action in which the mental or physical
condition of a person is at issue do either or both of the following:
(a) order that a person submit to a mental or physical medical
examination;
(b) appoint a health care professional to conduct a medical examination.⁶
(3) The Court may order a second or further medical examination by a health
care professional.⁷
(4) If the plaintiff has been the subject of a medical examination by a health care
professional of the plaintiff’s choice who will or may be proffered as an expert,
the Court may order that the plaintiff be the subject of a medical examination by
one or more health care professionals of the defendant’s choice.

A. General Rule
There are textbooks on this topic.¹

This Rule modiﬁes the former practice that only a plaintiff who sues for damages or

for personal injury could be examined.⁵

The court will see whether the examination seems necessary, and the specialty of the physician
is appropriate, especially if the defendant has already had a previous inspection under this Rule.²

C. Subrule (4) Disclosure
Rule 5.41(4) is triggered when the plaintiff may call a certain expert as a trial witness, even
if the plaintiff prefers not to. But it does not sufﬁce that the plaintiff’s family physician choose the
expert.⁵
Rule 5.41(4) (talking about what experts have already examined the plaintiff) includes
assessments for treatment purposes, and is not conﬁned to assessment to give evidence in
court.⁶
If that is shown and the evidence appears to be relevant, the onus shifts to the plaintiff to
show that there would be risks or other disadvantages to the examination.⁷
If the plaintiff has been examined by an expert on care costs chosen by the plaintiff’s counsel,
and the plaintiff pleads very serious injuries and ongoing care costs, it is fair that a defendant
be able to prepare an expert for trial testimony by ﬁrst-hand similar observation.⁸
In a medical
negligence suit against a hospital and the doctors, separately represented, their interests are
not identical, e. g. as to damages, even if they have not ﬁled third party notices or other pleadings
against each other, and they need not share an expert witness, and can get their own expert
examinations of the plaintiff.⁹

R.5.42(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
D. Tests
See also R.5.44n. B.
Rule 5.41 is not intended to allow the court to order a person to undergo DNA testing.
Someone’s DNA is not a “condition”, the word used in the Rules. But the court thought that it had
an inherent “equitable” jurisdiction to order that, though that such a claim could be defeated by
delay, or by unclean hands (such as not revealing the existence of a brother and without notice to
him).¹
The idea of an inherent power to bypass expressly-legislated limits, is questionable.
E. Minor Injury Regulations
There is a parallel procedure for medical examinations under the Minor Injury Regulations,
and the courts should interpret their time deadlines ﬂexibly.²
The Regulations provide for a
“certiﬁed medical examination”, but the court has no power to order the plaintiff to attend such
an examination, and the Regulation sets its own consequences for not attending. (The plaintiff
here contended that his injuries were outside the scope of the Regulation. )³
The two types of
inspection (R.5.41 and the Minor Injury Regulations) are not mutually exclusive: using one is
not an election not to use the other. The two procedures serve different aims, can be invoked by
different (but overlapping) groups, and are independent of each other.⁴
Under the Regulation, the
report of the examiner is clearly important; but everyone discusses the exact nature of the status
it has as “prima facie evidence”. It would be important for trial preparation and very inﬂuential
for settlement discussions. If a party gives documents to the examiner, he must also give a copy
to the other side, because of the rules of natural justice. That is so whether or not the document
was privileged.⁵
Options During Medical Examination
5.42(1) Unless otherwise ordered by the Court, a person who is to be the subject
of a medical examination by a health care professional may elect to do one or
more of the following:
(a) nominate a health care professional to be present during the medical
examination;
(b) videotape the medical examination;
(c) make a word-for-word recording of the medical examination.
(2) The Court may
(a) deﬁne or limit the presence or role of the nominated health care
professional,
(b) direct that any part of the medical examination, including any
standardized tests, not be recorded, and
(c) otherwise provide direction as to the conduct of the medical
examination.⁶

1 Branson v. Branson 2015 ABQB 227, 20 Alta LR(6th) 187 (estate ﬁght based on disputed paternity).
2 Benc v. Parker 2012 ABCA 249, 536 AR 145.
3 Abbas v. Menhem 2010 ABQB 527, 514 AR 379.

5-149
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.42(2)

Related Provisions
5.43 (costs of examination)

Under R.5.42, the plaintiff examined has a right to have her nominee attend despite the
defendant’s expert’s objections.¹
The professional witness allowed to attend and observe can
be any health care professional,²
though there might be narrow or special exceptions.³
A non-
professional “chaperon” is not permitted.⁴
Such a nominee doctor may take notes.⁵
In one case involving alleged risk to the plaintiff, a
psychological examination was to be videotaped.⁶
The defence cannot require the recording of a
defence medical examination, although the plaintiff has the right to do so.⁷
But one plaintiff was not
allowed to conduct or require mechanical recording for a number of reasons, including equality.⁸
Subrules 5.42(1), (2) let the court limit the way that plaintiffs may exercise their option to
record (or have witnessed) the defence medical examination. Showing genuine concern as to
the reliability of the physician’s account is not the only ground for witnessing or recording. Many
specialists in town refused to conduct an examination if it were taped, but not all did, and there
was no evidence of unsuitability of those physicians who were willing to be taped. Not to mention
the possibility of using related specialties. One specialist favored by the defendant was willing
to be taped. The defendant was told to select one willing to be taped, or select one who would
tolerate a witness selected by the plaintiff.⁹
Video recording of a medical examination of the plaintiff by the defendant’s nominee doctor
is a good thing, and assists both sides in settling the lawsuit. The Rules say that a copy is to be
given to the other side. The other side should be able to make use of the recording, and to do so
before reports are made by experts.¹⁰
Someone being examined by a psychologist has a presumptive right to a recording, especially
of the interview portion, despite the psychologist’s objections, even more if the “patient” does not
speak English and an interpreter will be necessary.¹¹
The Rules of Court (on taping medical exams) do not apply to statutory certiﬁed medical
examinations,¹²
nor do the Rules’ purpose apply by analogy, since such a certiﬁed examiner is
appointed independently.¹³

5-150
R.5.43(1) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
Payment of Costs of Medical Examinations
5.43(1) Unless the Court otherwise orders, the party who applies for the order for
a medical examination must pay the cost of the medical examination.¹
(2) Unless the Court otherwise orders, the cost of
(a) the attendance of a nominated health care professional at a medical
examination, or
(b) videotaping or recording a medical examination,
is to be paid by the party appointing the nominated health care professional or
electing to have the medical examination videotaped or recorded.
(3) The party arranging for the videotaping or recording must provide a copy of
the videotape or recording to the other party as soon as practicable after the
medical examination is complete.
(4) The videotape or recording
(a) may be shown or played at trial only with the Court’s permission,
and
(b) may only be used to verify events at the medical examination.

Related Provisions
6.43 (costs of court experts)

At least in the ﬁrst instance, a plaintiff bringing along his own physician to witness his
examination by the defendant’s expert must bear the cost of having the plaintiff’s physician come.²
For costs of having a nominee attend one’s medical examination by the other side, necessity need
not be proved; a satisfactory explanation generally warrants having the nominee attend and so
costs for that.³
Whether the fee to have a videographer attend can be included in party-party costs after the
trial, is not governed by R.5.43, which merely says that the party applying for the examination
pays up-front the cost of the attendance of the person examined, but not up-front the expenses
of a medical observer to attend and observe, nor a videographer. Since a bill from a medical
observer who attends can be part of party-party costs at the end of the suit, so may be a bill from
a videographer (used instead of an observer).⁴

R.217(2), which came from 1944 C. R.259(2), which was new in 1944.
2 Pohynayko v. Vries 2001 ABCA 20, 277 AR 72. This Rule codiﬁes that. Where a physician nominee showed

5-151
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.44(1)
Conduct of Examination
5.44(1) A health care professional conducting a medical examination may ask the
person being examined questions relating to that person’s mental and physical
condition and medical history, and the person being examined must answer the
questions.¹
(2) If the person to be examined agrees in writing, or if the Court so orders, the
examining health care professional may
(a) take or obtain samples from the person being examined, and make
an analysis of the samples, and
(b) perform any test recognized by medical science.²
(3) The party causing the medical examination to be conducted
(a) must, on request, deliver promptly to each of the other parties a copy
of a detailed written report of the health care professional’s ﬁndings
and conclusions, and
(b) is, on request, entitled to receive promptly from the person examined
a report of every medical examination previously or subsequently
made of the physical or mental condition of the person resulting
from the injuries sustained or the mental or physical condition that
is in issue.³
(4) If a party refuses to provide a report in the manner described in subrule (3),
the Court may order the report to be provided, and if the health care professional
refuses to make the report in writing, the Court may make any order it considers
proper, one of the provisions of which may be the exclusion of the health care
professional’s evidence if that person’s evidence is offered at trial.
(5) On application, the Court may make any order it considers necessary to limit
or curtail a medical examination.⁴

Related Provisions
5.35 (exchanging expert reports) ; 5.42 (presence of observer and recording examination) ;

5.43 (costs of examination)
A. Introduction
New Rr.5.44(3) and 5.41(1) are similar enough to their predecessors⁵
that the old case law
is still useful.⁶

5-152
R.5.44(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
B. Tests, Touching, and Questions
On DNA tests, see R.5.41, supra.
If the court orders a medical examination, the physician can touch the person to be examined,
and the physician may ask the person to sign a consent to be touched and examined.¹
But what
if the consent form goes further than the Rules or the court order, or waives liability for resulting
harm?
Tests by the examining physician need not be the subject of a separate earlier motion or court
leave.²
Giving a blood sample would pose no risk. There is no reason to exclude them from “samples”
in R.5.44(2), especially as they are one of the most common diagnostic tests. The physician need
not draw the blood himself, as the Rule says “take or obtain”. Attending is necessarily incidental
to the taking of the sample. Nor need the examination and the sample or test coincide, especially
as the need for a test probably often cannot be found without an examination. Stress in taking the
test is not enough to bar the order.³
The physician can ask questions, but only on topics germane to the medical question.⁴
If a litigant has to submit to a medical examination, the medical professional running it should
be able to decide how to conduct it, and ancillary tests, unless there is compelling reason for court
interference.⁵
The person examined need not go so far as to establish a real risk of serious injury,
but he or she must establish a compelling reason for limiting the test. That covers injury and other
types of hardship.⁶
Disclosure (by a plaintiff) is important, and needs strong things to override it,
such as maintaining conﬁdentiality of the information.⁷
To get a limit, the plaintiff needs evidence;
the chambers judge is not to take notice of types of tests, or conclude that some psychological
tests are unnecessary.⁸
The court will not authorize tests which are dangerous, or very painful or distressing, or go on
for too long;⁹
aliter with proof of danger.¹⁰
Ordinarily prejudice caused by a medical examination is
compensable in costs.¹¹
But serious risk to the plaintiff from the proposed examination could bar it.¹²
A plaintiff in fragile mental condition got an injunction against surveillance of her conduct.¹³

illness which could explain symptoms, rather than ﬁre sued for).

can be used: Martinez case.

5-153
PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION R.5.44(5)
Rule 5.44(5) on curtailing or limiting a medical examination is about the conduct of the
examination itself, not about whether or how it is recorded.¹
Now that such examinations are under Part 5 of the new Rules, they are subject to new R.
5.3(1) on curtailing rights whose expense etc. would be grossly disproportionate to the likely
beneﬁt, so the examination Rules are no longer absolute.²
C. Exchange of Reports
Read subrules (3) and (4) carefully; they create an additional type of disclosure of records, and
an additional type of exchange of experts’ reports. These medical examinations are sometimes
conducted merely thus to see the plaintiff’s medical reports.³
These subrules apparently override
privilege in medico-legal reports.⁴
After a medical legal examination, R.5.44(3) (b) gives one on
request the right to get “a like report of every examination previously or thereafter made”. That
paragraph allowed the defendant to get an oral report by a physician who was consulted but never
asked to give a written report and was not going to be used as a witness. It did not matter that the
expert who did not give a written report was not a medical expert.⁵
Apart from privilege, a party need not resort to R.5.41 to get a newly-acquired paper from the
opposing party.⁶
When a plaintiff gets a copy of the defendant’s R.5.41 report, it is not privileged,
and in a second related suit the plaintiff must produce it to the new defendant.⁷
Ordinarily the plaintiff has no right to get the examining doctor’s notes, raw scores, or the
professional literature on which he relies.⁸
Nor any right to an order barring discussions between
the defendant’s expert and his counsel.⁹
There is no gap in the Rules, and the court should not require a defendant to decide about
having a medical examination before the plaintiff had to disclose his reports under R.5.35. Rule
5.44(3) would not be useless by the law the way it is stated in the Rules of Court. It is not a bad
thing to use R.5.35 (on exchange of expert reports) to get the medical reports of the plaintiffs
before the defendant has conducted a medical examination. The onus is on the plaintiff to advance
his own action. This disclosure is in line with the general philosophy of the Rules to prevent
litigation by ambush.¹⁰

5-154
R.5.44(5) PART 5: DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION
The goals of speed and economy, and R.1.2, do not let the courts invent new powers or
procedures. An order for a medical examination of a plaintiff cannot call for production by the
plaintiff to the physician of whatever documents he requests.¹
D. Miscellaneous
The court ordered a medical examination called for by the terms of an insurance policy
(between the parties to this proceeding). The insurer can dictate who is the practitioner, and that
practitioner can use his usual methods, and the insured (and apparently the court) had no say in
the matter, whether under the garb of good faith or fairness.²

1 Martin v. Sievers (M) 2014 ABQB 357, [2015] AR Uned 412.

(Apr 6). In Dec.2020, the Supreme Court of Canada conﬁrmed a need for honesty and reasonableness in
enforcing contracts. Would that change the result today?
